                Dedication

To Elizabeth "Beth" Meadows Crissman, we dedicate this Centennial book,
1883-1983, of the community of Ipswich, South Dakota.

Were it not for your many years of collecting and preservation of records and
pictures, much of this book would not have been possible.

You are truly the historian of this community.  You have opened your home and
records time and time again to those who have sought information and knowledge
of the past.  For all of this, we say, "Thank you," and proudly dedicate this
book to you.

                Ipswich 1883 - 1983

We are grateful to the many people who helped in any way with this publication.
We extend special thanks to the Morlocks and their staff.

Chairman, Robert Gross assisted by James Engler and Jack Clarke.

                OUR BEGINNINGS

        Main Street looking north, 1884

What motivates the establishment of a town? What spurs people to leave the
comparative comforts of their previous residences and seek a new life in a then
undeveloped area? What persuades individuals or families that pastures are
always greener, a future more promising somewhere else? Agricultural potential,
assured to a substantial degree of accuracy might have been an enticing factor.
Black loam of considerable depth on flat or gently rolling plains, covered by
an almost endless expanse of native grasses, invited the anticipation of ample
crops and the growth of livestock. Supplying the needs of those so engaged
would prompt the establishment of stores and services to meet the needs of the
settlers.

The location of Ipswich at its present site was dictated by the decision of the
Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul railroad for its projected route west of
Aberdeen in 1883. Three small towns were in existence in the immediate area.
Edmunds was two miles to the southwest, Georgetown was one mile southeast and
Freeport one and one-half miles northeast.

Edmunds County had been created in 1873 and not organized until ten years later
on July 26, 1883. The territorial governor had appointed commissioners for the
county. The little community of Edmunds was the first county seat, but it was
recalled that little business was done there before its removal to Ipswich.
Georgetown, the first settlement of the county in October, 1882 was probably so
named because two of the three original settlers had the given first name of
George. That winter numerous claims were located in the Freeport area. This
community had high hopes for its growth, prosperity, establishment of the
county seat and the location to be served by the railroad pushing westward.

Hopes of these three communities were turned to disappointment upon the
decision of the railroad to construct the furtherance of their line to a
location in the center of a so-called triangle between Freeport, Edmunds and
Georgetown.

On October 2,1883 a town was platted on sections 27 and 28 and given the name
of Ipswich, after a city by the same name in the eastern part of England. Just
30 days later, the board of county commissioners voted on November 1, 1883, to
change the county seat from Edmunds to Ipswich. The three other communities
came to the conclusion that their futures were limited or hopeless. The
residents and businesses lost little time in moving their houses and buildings
to lots in Ipswich. Included in this transition was transplanting the only two
story structure in Freeport, the Allen House Hotel. Most of the buildings were
not large and so the problem of moving was in that respect minimal by our
standards.

        1. Thompson Livery Stable
        2. Drug Store
        3. J.W. Parmley Office
        4. Opera House
        5. Arnold's Land Agency and First State Bank
        6. Baptist Church
        7. Jaquith Residence - formerly Allen House moved in from
           Freeport, now site of Roy Jones house
        8. Courthouse
        9. Warner House, now Bloemendaal residence
        10. Edmunds County Democrat, a newspaper
        11. Thompson residence, present home of Leon Weisgram
        Notice the numerous "comfort stations"

        1. Creamery
        2. Ipswich Roller Mill, F.K. Wing
        3. Depot
        4. Presently Ipswich Farmers Elevator cement facility
        5. W.P. Tate residence - Area at present Holy Cross Church.
           Hall and School
        6. Hotel run by Charles Bostock - Ipswich State Theater now
        7. Rooming House
        8. St. James Hotel
        9. Roller Rink
        10. Ipswich Public School
        11. Congregational Church

It was in some cases a bitter disappointment for those feeling it necessary or
compelled to move. Many had been led to believe in the future growth and
prosperity of their respective localities and the forced discontinuance of these
three towns meant financial losses. All this took place six years before South
Dakota and North Dakota were admitted to statehood in 1889, the 39th and 40th
states of the union.

In the years following, the community would receive many people coming from
Europe. There were numerous German speaking immigrants whose original homes had
been in South Russia. There were frequent arrivals from Wales and a goodly
number settled south of Ipswich in the Powell vicinity. Some immigrants had
other native countries but were in lesser percentage than the Welsh and
Germans.

The following decades would see changes and transitions. Establishment of
churches, schools, organization of service and social clubs took place.
Fraternal organizations located chapters here. Home and business building
construction, street rebuilding and other improvements were somewhat sporadic,
but nevertheless effected.

In the early days a series of disastrous fires destroyed homes and business
blocks, interrupting any feeling of positive security and encouraged more
future fireproof construction. But these circumstances were offset by the
indomitable spirit of the residents who seldom doubted the promise of the
community's future.

        Aerial view of Ipswich

Possibly the most encouraging hopes for the continuance of this locality as it
goes into its second century is the activity of younger citizens in business
and in the professional fields. Add to this the tenacity of the Commercial Club
in its fifty-first year since reorganization and the activity and promotions of
the Development Corporation. The future may also be benefited by youth
activities. These have put Ipswich in the spotlight of attention many times
with their athletic successes, not only in school but in the summer baseball and
softball programs.

These activities encouraged the residents to believe that Ipswich has been, is
and will continue to be a desirable place to live and maintain a home. Ralph W.
Bebermeyer

                Railroads

        First train into Ipswich, November 1883.

The establishment of Ipswich was based on the decision by the Chicago,
Milwaukee and St. Paul railroad to locate its line through where Ipswich is now
located.

The rail line was extended from Aberdeen to Ipswich in 1883 with the first
train arriving here in November of that year. The land agent for the Milwaukee
project was a Mr. C.H. Prior, who was a native of the British Isles. He named
the towns along the route with good solid English names such as Webster,
Bristol, Andover, Groton, James, Bath and Ipswich.

The effect of the railway on Ipswich was tremendous, as business people were
able to obtain their supplies much cheaper. The November 15, 1883 issue of the
Dakota Tribune cites the coal price as an illustration of lower prices. Because
of the railroad, coal could be purchased at $7.50 per ton compared to the $18
per ton price people had been paying.

Trade was much easier for local and country people alike as they no longer had
to journey to Aberdeen to make a trip eastward.

Trade became much easier for the local merchants. They were able to ship goods
out by freight as well as in. The first freight load to leave Ipswich was
several cars of buffalo bones. The May 8, 1884 issue of the Tribune cites the
first freight load: "While strolling around the lumber yards yesterday our
reporter was suddenly confronted by a huge pile of buffalo bones. Inquiry
elicited facts concerning these bones that may be of interest to our readers.
During the past three weeks over 100 tons of bones have been purchased by
dealers in this city for shipment to Chicago where they are ground into
fertilizers."

Ipswich became a supply point for much of the surrounding area as the railroad
was not extended westward from Ipswich until 1886. Freight receipts for the
first two weeks of May in 1884 totaled $5,128.59. Ticket sales were $283.30. One
hundred and fifty-three cars of emigrant goods were received between January 1
and May 1, 1884. The Edmunds County Democrat of November 30, 1889 says,' By
actual count of cars Ipswich has shipped more car loads of stock than any other
station on the Jim River division of the Milwaukee road, this season."

A story in the January 14, 1917 issue of the Aberdeen Sunday American says
"Ipswich is one of the important shipping points on the Milwaukee Road." In
1916, 175 carloads of livestock were shipped from Ipswich as were 49,587
gallons of cream, approximately 780,000 bushels of grain between July 1, 1915
and January 1, 1917. "Of the stock shipped during the year just ended, there
were 87 carloads of hogs, 72 of cattle, 11 horses and five sheep."

Ipswich was a regular passenger train stop for several years, but in 1924 the
community almost lost the service from one of the trains, because Ipswich
businessmen had been patronizing the truck instead of the railway in shipments
from Aberdeen.

An August 16, 1924 article in the Tribune said "The business interests of
Ipswich and the Milwaukee railway are identical." Ipswich needs the good will,
service and cooperation of the railway and the railway needs the same in return
from Ipswich and every other town along its line. This, we are satisfied the
management of the Milwaukee intends to and wants to give. It is impossible for
a great corporation to secure the services of employees who grasp the big thing
in railing operation. If it cannot be had through the railway, the truck will
be tried and this is the reason so much of the Aberdeen-Ipswich business has
been given to the trucks. We are not running the railway, in fact we sometimes
doubt whether we are running our own business, but it looks as though, with the
revolution that is on in transportation the solution will be a gasoline or other
car operated by the railway either on its rails or on the highway..."

The Railway offered several excursion trains allowing area people to attend
festive events at reasonable prices. In 1900 a special excursion train was used
to bring people from Eureka, Hillsview, Hosmer, Bowdle and Roscoe to Ipswich for
the Fourth of July celebration.

The railroad has encountered a steady decline for many years. As the motor
freight industry became well established in the 1950's, farmers were offered an
alternate mode of transportation giving them flexibility in rates, routes and
scheduling. As use of the railroads diminished, many small lines were
abandoned. Use of privately owned cars and trucks changed some phases of
Ipswich life. They were once served by morning and evening local trains and two
afternoon fliers. Then the only two passenger trains passing through here were
the night time Milwaukee Hiawathas, neither of which stopped here. Mail came in
and was dispatched by truck or highway post office services.

        Ipswich depot during the 40's.

Passenger service here experienced periodic changes. At times it was possible
to take a morning local into Aberdeen and return by flier in the afternoon.
These would stop to discharge passengers but would not take on any passengers
from here. Also, one could take a local west bound in the morning and take
another local east bound and return in the evening.

The Milwaukee Road faced the challenge of establishing markets in areas where
other carriers had existed for years. The road's transcontinental line was
completed in 1910; the last transcontinental routes built in 1869 and 1883 in
Nebraska and North Dakota.

The Panama Canal was completed in 1914, taking away much traffic from a line
that did not have enough traffic to support itself in the beginning.

South Dakota's main line from Ortonville, Minn. to Miles City, Mont. was
abandoned by the Milwaukee road in 1982. The line was purchased by the State of
South Dakota and is now operated by Burlington Northern Railroad.

                COMMUNICATIONS

        First airmail to Ipswich, northwest of town. Doris Lass Phyllis
        Crissman. Betty Holdhusen and Shirley Satzinger

In most cases, a small town or a rural community in the 1800's was pretty well
isolated from the world at large. One waited for an occasional newspaper or
word of mouth to learn what the other half was doing. But in 1884 telegraph
service was established between Aberdeen and Ipswich and we were put in touch
with the world!

When the telephone system came into being in 1897, another communication link
was made. A number of Ipswich residents may still remember the early phones in
the community. These were magneto telephones that had to be cranked to get the
operator who then rang the party requested.

In the beginning there were to be only twenty customers to each rural line,
since there were but twenty possible combinations of "rings", and each had his
own. When twenty-seven customers applied for telephones on one line, the
problem was solved by assigning the same "ring" to two members of the same
family  living in separate households. Both answered the ring and upon learning
for which one the call was intended, the unwanted one hung up.

Not that everyone hung up  for the rings all came into each home, and sometimes
another's call would be more interesting than one's own. It was a brave young
man who would call his girl for a date over a rural line. He could be sure that
everyone on the line would know who had called, where they were going and what
they were going to do.

News of trouble and disasters was rapidly passed on to anyone who had neglected
to " listen in", and the same was true of choice bits of gossip.

These rural lines were owned and serviced by W. E. Herrick for many years. They
were later sold to Fred Kranzler, who in turn, sold the lines to the Ipswich
Rural Telephone Association in 1953.

The telephone exchange in Ipswich was begun in 1897 and was operated for the
first ten years by S.H. Rossiter and his daughter, Flossie, who was the first
"central". The exchange was operated in his jewelry stare which was located in
the front of the drug store and later moved to rooms above the present Crissman
Drug Store. The first subscribers were Dr. Robert Hill and the J.W. Parmley
Abstract Company.

In 1905 there were 42 telephones in Ipswich and the first directory, a small
card, was printed and distributed. By 1916 there were 177 town telephones and
14 rural telephones. As of August 31, 1982 there were 708 main telephone lines
served by the Ipswich office.

The telephone company serving this area was the Dakota Central until 1930 when
it was taken over by Northwestern Bell. In January of 1942, the present dial
system was instituted. In 1978 a rural service improvement project was
completed. Some 188 area rural customers were provided upgraded storm proof
telephone service, and all eight-party telephone lines were eliminated. The
International Direct Distance Dialing now enables Ipswich phone users to dial 82
different countries.

But we lost some human touches when we no longer had local operators handling
our calls. It was not unusual, when one asked "central" for a given number in
the afternoon, to have her reply "Call back later. She's taking a nap. The baby
was sick all night." Or "She isn't home. She went to Aberdeen today."

One important service of our "central" on duty was to put out party line calls
in cases of emergency. These calls consisted of continuing short rings until
the operator felt that everyone on the party line was listening. Then she might
say, "There's a fire at the Jones' farm and it's a bad one. Did you all get
that?" Then the fire-fighting husbands who had been dressing while the wives
listened to the message, would be on their way.

When one called for the doctor, central might say, "He's out at Johnson's.
She's having her baby. Shall I call you when he gets back?"

Such personal touches were very comforting far superior to a cold-hearted
recording saying, "The number you have dialed cannot be reached", and you know
that the recorder doesn't even care! Our "centrals" did! ! !
                Survilia Herrick

                Ipswich Tribune

        Mr. Frank Tracy, center, Democrat Editor, with Elmo McAllister and
        Allen Brown.

The Ipswich Tribune is the community's oldest business still in operation in
Ipswich and celebrates its centennial in 1983, as does the city of Ipswich.

The Dakota Tribune was founded by A.B. Chubbuck and D.B. Worthington in May of
1883, in the small community of Freeport. When the railroad failed to reach the
neighboring communities of Freeport, Georgetown and Edmunds, the residents
packed up and moved to Ipswich where the rail line located. The owners of the
Tribune were no different as the. publishers moved their offices after the
September 20 1883 issue.

During its 100 years of operation the Tribune has had a number of owners
beginning with Chubbuck and Worthington. Worthington relinquished his interest
in the newspaper in August of 1884. Chubbuck sold thee paper to J.W. Parmley
June 1, 1909. He published the Tribune for nearly three years before selling to
L.C. and Bettina Jackson on April 20, 1912. The Jackson' published the paper
for nine years and sold it to Walter L. Johnson in April of 1921.

Johnson remained as owner for approximately six years, until he sold the
printing plant and paper to Frank Tracy on October 1, 1927. Prior to Tracy's
purchase of the Tribune, Ipswich had boasted two weekly newspapers, the Edmunds
County Democrat, owned by Tracy, and the Tribune. Upon purchasing the Tribune
Tracy combined the two publications. Tracy died in June of 1928. His wife
continued publication until May 16 1932 when she sold the paper to H.J. and
sons, M.B. and R.W. Bebermeyer.

It is interesting to note that Frank Tracy was the uncle of the actor, Spencer
Tracy. The younger Tracy spent his summers in Ipswich where much of his time
was spent in the Tribune office.

H.J. Bebermeyer and son Merrill B. published the paper while son Ralph W. was
employed at the Watertown Public Opinion. When H.J. died in September, 1947,
the two sons continued their partnership and published the paper together until
1967 when Merrill sold his share in the business to W.L. Combs. R.W. and Combs
were partners until 1971 when Combs bought to become sole owner. One or more of
the Bebermeyers were in business here from 1932 until 1971, 39 years.

        Chet Williams was an employee at the Tribune when the Bebermeyers came
        to Ipswich in 1932 He is shown feeding the old single revolution press
        which was used then and many years after for printing the Ipswich
        Tribune and Roscoe Independent. The press is now in the Pioneer Village
        at Ipswich. Chet was a brother of Mike (Mybra) Williams of Ipswich.

        Glen Bunday, linotype operator and printer at the Tribune in the 30's
        and 40's is feeding the old Omaha newspaper folder. Standing is Merrill
        Bebermeyer, co-publisher.

        Ralph and Merrill Bebermeyer, owners of the Ipswich tribune, then named
        Edmunds County Democrat.

        H. J. Bebermeyer, editor of the Tribune 1932-47 is seen congratulating
        Mrs. Morris (Irene Kraft) Kitchell for having won a subscription
        contest, probably about 1941. She is holding the $500 first prize money.

Combs sold the business to the present owners, James and Frances Morlock in
1976. The Morlocks have two sons, Mark and Mike. Under the Bebermeyers' and
Combs' operations, the Roscoe Independent was printed here in addition to the
Ipswich Tribune. The present owners have included Hosmer with the Roscoe
publication.

Under the Morlocks' leadership, the plant was converted to offset printing,
making composition faster and easier with computerized typesetting in change
from the older hand set and hot metal cast methods.

The format of the Tribune changed from eight to six columns in 1981. The
newspaper also received seven awards in state competition that year.

Other newspapers published in Ipswich during the past 100 years were the
Ipswich Gazette, the Ipswich Independent and the South Dakota Tribune.

                Opera House

I suppose that every small pioneer town had one building which seems, in
retrospect, to have been the most important building in the community. Our
Opera House was such a building. It was located just north of the present
Fireplace Lounge.

In the days before the schools had auditoriums, all events of importance which
required seating space were held in the Opera House.

Earliest memories bring pictures of bazaars, box socials and community dances
and thrill of thrills, traveling companies of actors who presented a series of
stage plays. The Clint and Bessie Robbins' Company was a regular in Ipswich year
after year. Memorial Day exercises were held there, as were high school
graduation exercises and class plays.

The walls backstage presented a record of the events held with scribbled names
and date of performances and the names of performers.

Footlights, a balcony, an orchestra pit and a proscenium upon which were
painted advertisements for Ipswich business places made this the most
sophisticated building in town.

Small wonder that we all settled into our opera seats with shivers of
anticipation whenever we were taken to a performance there. The large rooms on
the second floor were used as meeting places for lodges and, in later years,
served the American Legion and its Auxiliary. These rooms were reached by two
long covered outside stairways, one on the north and one on the south sides of
the building.

        Instructors came from Aberdeen to give dance lessons.

Apparently, Ipswich has always been a community of "can-do"ers.

It was in 1899 that a group of citizens became convinced that there was a real
need for a community meeting place. Consequently the Lodge Association was
formed and action was begun under the direction of Mr. George C. Meadows.

There were no federal funds and no "matching grants" available, but one year
later in 1900, there was a well built two story brick building on Main Street,
known as the Opera House.

Before the year 1900 was over, near disaster struck. Acetylene lights had been
installed in the building. Such lighting was very new and little was known of
the inherent dangers involved in its use.

One evening, just before the Warren Nobles Company was to give its second
performance, gas in the acetylene plant exploded, tearing up the stage, blowing
out two brick partitions in the basement, blowing out windows and doors and
wrenching the building askew.

The plant manager, Elton Williams, was just closing the plant door when the
explosion occurred. He survived with a fractured hand, a dislocated wrist, cuts

about the head, face badly burned and hair singed off. He made his way to
safety through a hole blown in the wall.

Warren Noble, who headed the acting company, had just entered the men's
dressing room and turned to close the door when the blast was felt. He was
severely burned, his face almost unrecognizable, hair gone and his body badly
bruised.

Other actors were injured. There were broken ribs and Lew Gordon who was
leaning over a wardrobe trunk, had a stove blown upon him, followed by a shower
of bricks which flattened the stove. The stove probably saved his life for it
saved his body from being battered by bricks.

Fortunately, the orchestra members were not yet in the pit, for the instruments
left there by the members a short time before, were a complete loss when the
front part of the stage fell into the orchestra pit.

It was a time of panic for the whole town with frantic parents, who had allowed
children to go "just a little early", having no way of learning whether or not
they were safe.

The good citizens of Ipswich rallied about the company of unfortunate actors,
taking them into their homes, arranging for doctors and treatment and giving
them financial aid when needed.

        Trail booster trip in front of Parmley office and opera house.

        Back of Opera House. E. T. Balch owned a garage to the south. Tom
        Ratchford and Sam Hagen worked as mechanics in the garage.

After experts were consulted, it was determined that the building could be
saved by straightening the walls and reinforcing them with steel bars and
supports. Thus the Opera House was saved for 49 more years of use.

The building was purchased by the American Legion in 1945 and its use continued
until it was completely destroyed by fire on April 26, 1949.

The fire was discovered at 3 a.m. by three high school boys who were making
final arrangements to leave Ipswich at 4 a.m. for an annual skip day.

The fire had started on the second floor and when the streams of water struck
the spat, a steam explosion occurred which lifted the roof, the walls bulged
outward and fell, and the roof fell back into the seething fire. The firemen
kept watch for two days until all fire damage was controlled.

The Opera House was no more, but the memories of its existence and its place in
the life of the community persist to the present day.
                Survilia Herrick

                Ipswich By The Tracks by Beth Meadows Crissman

Dakota Territory was officially opened to settlement on July 10, 1859. On March
2, 1861, President James Buchanan approved a bill creating the Territory of
Dakota.

In 1861, President Abraham Lincoln sent William Jayne, his close friend and
physician from Springfield, Illinois, to organize the Territorial Government of
Dakota. Mrs. Mayo Jaquith was a granddaughter of the Territorial Governor,
William Jayne. Mrs. Jaquith came to Ipswich in the winter of 1883, on the first
passenger train and lived in Ipswich the rest of her life.

Dakota was a new frontier, and the people who ventured into it were forced to
withstand many hardships. In spite of drought, blizzards, prairie fires and
grasshoppers, many stayed. Some were not quite sure whether they really wanted
to stay or whether they just couldn't afford to go back home.

I wasn't there for the beginning, having been born in 1896 four miles east and
moving into Ipswich in the fall of 1897, but I was privileged to know many who
were. Pioneers who built up this area had a spirit which was something special.
Survival was the name of the game, and denial a way of life.

Mr. Theron Whitbeck staked his all on a crop of beans the first summer here. He
broke ten acres, bought beans at ten cents a pound, and planted ten acres by
hand, on every other furrow. He had a good crop and pulled them by hand, piled
them in nice piles, and was ready to flail them out, when a big wind came up,
and not one bean was saved.

Weather was a big factor in the early years. The year 1896 was a record year.
Heavy snow came in October and remained for six months. In the 1892 winter the
railway lines in South Dakota were abandoned from December until April. That was
before the invention of the rotary snowplows, so men had to shovel out the
right of way, often to have it closed by another snow storm.

Mr. Robert Jones, father of "Gib", who was originally from Wales, found that by
doing harvest work near Fargo and walking two hundred miles back to his family
near Powell, getting them settled for the winter, and then going to Ohio to
work in the coal mines, he could survive. One year he came from Ohio with one
dollar in his pocket, having sent most of his earnings back to his family.

As my father's experience coming to Dakota must be typical of many others, I
will tell you about it. After finishing his schooling at Spencer Business
School in Milwaukee, his father offered George C. Meadows $250 to stay home one
year before "going west". It was with $200 of this money as his stake that he
with two other boys loaded a railway car with six horses, lumber, chickens,
harnesses, two wagons, feed and provisions.

They arrived in Aberdeen April 1, 1883, after six days and nights on the road.
The cars had to be unloaded where they stopped, immediately, day or night. The
livestock were taken out over a portable chute carried from one car to another
by all hands.

The main track was piled high with immigrant goods of every description. They
had lumber, so built a shed north of the tracks in Aberdeen in order to get
their horses and feed under cover, and the three boys slept on top of the feed
sacks. The railroad entered Aberdeen from the east in July, 1881, and this was
the end of the line.

In April, 1883, in a letter home, my father said that he thought the oats that
he planted that spring were the first oats sown in Edmunds County.

He also wrote, "A road is as wide as the prairie, this is great country! You no
doubt know about mirages, it is as pretty a sight as I have seen. We can see it
here most any bright morning just as the sun is rising; every object seems
elevated, and at the same time drawn nearer. Georgetown, although two and a
half miles distant, does not seem more than a half mile away."

On a bitter cold day, my father went to the Land Office in Aberdeen to make
final proof of his claim. While waiting for the office to open, everyone
crowded into the entry way to get out of the cold. The wooden structure
separated from the main building, and when the registrar opened the office door,
he scolded those who were waiting. A collection was taken to buy nails, and
they repaired the damage.

Ezra Drew, the first settler in Edmunds County, arrived in Aberdeen, September
22, 1882, and his family arrived from lowa, November 12, 1882. In the winters
that he lived on his claim, he always hung a lighted lantern on a pole, so that
wanderers might find shelter from a storm. Luckily, 1882 was a mild winter and
the snow never more than four inches deep. The next spring, when the settlers
came in, Mrs. Drew fed as many as one hundred people in one day, using fifty
pounds of flour for biscuits. She was paid $1.25 for supper, lodging and
breakfast.

Mr. Drew and his wife, Matilda, had the help of their daughter, Mrs. Ira
Dibble, and son, Merwin, who lived near them. They were taught that no one
should share who was not willing to plant and build. Elsie Bosanko (Mrs. William
Bosanko) was a great-granddaughter of Ezra Drew, and her children are
great-great-grandchildren. They are Glen, Norman, Maurice, Dick and Robert.

On September 28, 1882, Mr. W.B. Bold, H.W. Hunter and John Davis built the
first shanties west of Cortlandt. Hunter and Davis returned East, so the sole
occupants of this portion of the county all that winter were the Drews and the
Bolds.

Early in 1883, with the unprecedented tide of immigration to this section of
South Dakota, every claim between Aberdeen and Boldsville, as Mr. Bold's shanty
was known, was taken by actual settlers.

Somebody wrote on Mr. Bold's shanty, "Mr. Bold, Mr. Bold, I've been told, your
wife's a scold." Underneath, he wrote, "It may be true as you've been told, and
yet she's worth her weight in gold."

During the summer of 1883, the Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul Railroad built
twenty-six miles west of Aberdeen and stopped at the center of a triangle
formed by three towns, Edmunds, Georgetown and Freeport. It was these towns
which moved lock, stock and barrel to establish the town of Ipswich October 2,
1883.

Charles H. Prior of Minneapolis was Superintendent of the Hastings and Dakota
division of the Milwaukee Road at that time and the owner of the townsite. He
named the new town Ipswich after Ipswich, England, his native home.

A quote from the Aberdeen Republican September 27, 1883, states, "Ipswich is
the euphonious name on which the new railroad town west delights in. Of course,
the town will have to be temperance, no man half seas over could pronounce such
a name."

Other names suggested for the new town were "Sandwich" and "Stella", no doubt
the "Stella" was suggested by Captain Arnold who wife's maiden name was Stella
Hosmer. The town of Hosmer was named for Arnold

Captain and Mrs. S. V. Arnold in 1883, came to Scranton, and built the first
hotel, which they leased. In 1884, they moved to Roscoe where they made their
home for several years. He was for four years right-of-way agent and townsite
locater for the Milwaukee Railroad, which probably gave him the chance to name
the City of Hosmer, and try for Stella as a name for Ipswich. In 1890, he was
elected to the office of Register of Deeds, and they moved to Ipswich where
they I lived for the rest of their lives.

Mrs. Stella Hosmer Arnold was originally from New York state. Her father was a
well known poet, and a friend of Henry Wadsworth Longfellow. In 1900, she wrote
an article entitled "The Sunshine State", which appeared in an Eastern
periodical and was of such merit that the Milwaukee Railway Company used it for
the purpose of bringing to the attention of the world the peculiar advantages
of South Dakota and involuntarily the people adopted the name "South Dakota,
the Sunshine State".

During the administration of Governor Lee, Stella Arnold served on the State
Womens' Board of Charities and Corrections and was intensely interested in the
betterment of surroundings for the inmates of the penal institutions. She
worked with the Indians and treasured moccasins made for her by Sitting Bull's
daughter.

The October 28, 1883, Dakota Tribune printed, "Soon will the inhabitants of
Ipswich and Edmunds County have another reason for rejoicing. In less than
three weeks time a train of cars will come spinning into Ipswich. How the
people of this section will cheer and lift up their voices when they first hear
the hum of the wheels. At present track is laid as far as Snake Creek. A large
force of men are employed, and one of the track layers informed us that iron
was being laid at the rate of one mile per day."

"The Lord be Praised" was the headline when the first train did come into
Ipswich, the County seat of Edmunds County on November 10, 1883. Now the other
three towns pulled their buildings, some with oxen, into the new town.

Soon after this Mr. S.H. Jumper was in Ipswich and announced that as soon as
needed the St. Paul Railroad Company would donate to the county, for courthouse
purposes, the finest block of lots on the townsite; also that another block
would be given for public school purposes, and that any church organization
desiring lots on which to build their edifices could obtain them by calling the
Edmunds County Bank.

After one of the several fires from which the new town suffered, the railroad
generously volunteered to ship in building material at half price.

In May, 1884, the "y" on the south side of the track was dispensed with to make
room for a turn table. I asked Harland Allen if he remembered it, and this was
his reply "I remember well the roundhouse at Ipswich when it was the end of the
line. I was always awed that a mere high school boy or a man could push a big
engine around."

Mr. and Mrs. A.B. Allen had a hotel in Freeport and moved it to Ipswich with
oxen teams. It stood on the east side of Main Street between Second and Third
Avenues for many years as the oldest house in Ipswich.

There was also a Warner house about the same time on Third Street, on the east
side of the street between First and Second Avenues. Mrs. Warner and Mr. Allen
were brother and sister. Mrs. Warner had five daughters. Luella married Mr.
Addison Stowe who was the first depot agent, and Rose married Elmer Parsons who
had the first hardware store (also moved to Ipswich from Freeport).

Mr. Allen was the first County Treasurer and was very active on committees to
build the new town. Mrs. Allen was the first primary teacher and active in
church work. Their son, Harland, placed globes in the Ipswich Library and in
the high school in their honor.

        Roundhouse at Ipswich when it was the end of the railroad line.

Harland was graduated from Ipswich High School and went on to make a name for
himself. The mens' dormitory at Dakota Wesleyan University in Mitchell is
called "Allen Hall" for him, and he has served many years on their Board of
Trustees. He was also one of the founders and served on the board for Roosevelt
University in Chicago, where they conferred the degree of "Doctor of Humane
Letters" upon him in 1967. For thirty-seven years he was in an investment
company on Michigan Avenue in Chicago and sent out letters pertaining to
economic trends. This caused him to travel worldwide.

In commenting on his life in Edmunds County he said, "How glad I am for the
memories of those early, harsh years, when we seemed to have a crop failure
every other year. Food was scarce, or uncertain to come by in the old frontier
days, and I learned to enjoy any kind that was available.

Moreover moderation was encouraged by scarcity. Both have helped me to enjoy
life ever since."

The Allens lived near my husband's family at one time, and Harland said that
Criss' mother never gave him a piece of pie, she always gave him the whole pie.

Harland said, "My most vivid memories of Herbert Crissman are, and always will
be, of a chubby, smiling faced boy about nine years old riding a hay rake drawn
by Rev. TreFethren's elongated sorrel. And well do I remember the time that
horse got excited, and Herbert took a fast ride. I remember him gripping the
back of the seat with both hands and tripping the tines of the rake with every
jump of the horse until the skin was literally pounded off most of his
knuckles."

Another story was about a prairie fire which the Allens experienced, Harland
stood in a slough of water holding his invalid mother while their buildings
were burned around them.

The first jail in Ipswich was built in the fall of 1884. It was located on the
east side of Fifth Street, between Third and Fourth Avenues. It was solidly
built of two by fours to make it strong. The heavy door was kept locked by a
crude lock with an iron key about six inches in length. (This is in the museum
thanks to Ed Cochran.) Sometimes friends of the prisoners furnished the inmates
with liquor which made it necessary to build high board fence around the jail.
This was so the prisoners could be kept reasonably sober until their release.
This jail must have sufficed until the first Courthouse was built in 1888-89.
The cost of the first Courthouse was $11,000. Orson Cochran used a team of oxen
to haul sand and brick for that building.

At one point a carload of oxen was shipped in to supply those wanting breaking
teams. I remember hearing about my father who thought that he would try plowing
with a team of oxen. My mother said that those oxen took him any place they
wanted to go.

The first cemetery was on the northwest edge of town. Hazel Hagen remembers
seeing it while looking for buttercups and crocuses.

Mr. and Mrs. Fred Hubbard lived for nine years on the land where our cemeteries
are now. He tried to farm and raise poultry, but moved back to Elgin, Illinois
in 1892. When he came back to visit many years later and was told that his land
had been bought for a cemetery he said, "Why didn't I think of that; it was the
only thing the land was good for." He also said that if it hadn't been for the
Ipswich signs and the road map, he would have journeyed on as this was the
prettiest town he had seen on his trip from Elgin to Ipswich. When he left in
1892, it was shacks, hard times, no trees or large buildings and bumper crops
were the biggest dreams which never came true. The nine years from 1883 to 1892
were hard ones, but as he and his wife recall, they were the happiest of their
lives.

"Nothing to make a Shadow" does not apply here any more, and often people who
have been away many years, marvel at the great change the trees have made in
the appearance of Ipswich.

One tree project worthy of notice was promoted by Mrs. James Bartholomew. It
was her idea to plant a row of trees from the town to the cemetery.

Mr. George Loomis had a general store in Georgetown (the town was named for
him.) He was one of the first to move his store from Georgetown to Ipswich. He
was the father of Lee, Pearl, Grace and Rol, all popular young people of that
time. Lee Loomis, after clerking in his father's store, decided to go to
Seattle from where he sailed on the second ship which left for the Alaska gold
rush. It was his stake from Alaska gold rush days which enabled him to start
the Loomis Armored Car Service in Portland, Oregon in 1925. This business was an
immediate success. He built a $100,000 fortress in Portland with steel walls
and bullet and burglar proof windows. He had offices from Vancouver to San
Jose, California. He called his nephew, George Loomis, from the Ipswich State
Bank to come out and enter the business. Since then the Loomis armored cars are
also found in Canada, Hawaii and Alaska.

To know real fright one has to experience the sight of the sky at night lit up
by a raging prairie fire, with church bells ringing and horses racing up the
streets hauling wagons filled with all the available men in town going to the
fire. The only means of fighting the fire was with gunny sacks, brooms, rags
and barrels of water. As Mr. Horace Dailey said, "Prairie fires swept over the
prairies faster than a horse could race. l tried it, and the fire won the
race."

It was one of these fires which caused the towns people to take refuge in the
Courthouse. Grace Loomis packed her worldly possessions in a trunk and carried
it to a waiting vehicle. When the fire was under control, and Grace was to go
back home, she wasn't able to lift one end of the trunk.

The first white child born in Edmunds County was Willard A. Brown. He was born
August 3, 1883, to Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Brown who lived one mile directly
northeast of Freeport on NE 1/4-14-125-68. The Browns came from Freeport,
Illinois as did the Chubbucks and the John Rogers families, thus the name
Freeport. Willard went from the Ipswich School to the University in Vermillion
for his education. His father had a furniture store in Ipswich, and Willard
joined him in that business, and later became an undertaker. He was elected
Mayor of Ipswich in 1957. In 1918, he was united in marriage to Muriel Usborne
of Milwaukee. She was Worthy Grand Matron of the Eastern Star and State
President of the P.E.O. Sisterhood. Willard died in December, 1965, having
lived his entire life in Ipswich and vicinity.

Freeport was the birthplace of the Dakota Tribune. On May 3, 1883, A.B.
Chubbuck and D.B. Worthington presented to the public the first newspaper
published in Edmunds County. The Tribune office was the first building moved
into Ipswich with a location on Main Street. This was on September 27, 1883.
They put out thirty copies printed on muslin that week, price 25c. It was one of
these that my father sent back to his girl in Wisconsin. In a letter thanking
him for it, she said that she could hardly get it away from her father long
enough to read it, he was so interested in it. I think my mother must have
sensed that she was involved in making history, as she kept the Tribune and
started a scrapbook. The scrapbook has been invaluable to me, and quite a few
others looking for their roots.

        Mr. A. B. Chubbuck, first Tribune editor, setting print.

Mr. Chubbuck walked to Aberdeen for the patent sheets needed for his paper each
week. Patent sheets were printed on one side with world news and sold to
publishers to use to publish matters of their own selection, the local news
items and local ads on the reverse side.

At one time he had his office in his yard at home, moving the type setting
stand outside in the summer. He was well known by all children as always having
candy in his pockets for them. He was always the first prairie chicken hunter,
too.

Mr. David Worthington kept a copy of the first Tribune, which he mentions in
his "Memoirs". He said that he and Mr. Chubbuck made $9,000 their first year in
business, "too much money for boys". The money was made by publishing
homesteaders" final claim notices for $6.00 apiece. He went on to say that the
prairie was dotted with cabins occupied by pretty girls holding down claims,
who hod just as much grit as any member of the male sex. In 1884, Mr.
Worthington became reporter on the Milwaukee Sentinel. He went on to Chicago
and West Coast papers before becoming editor and publisher of the Beloit Daily
News in 1897. He died in 1925,

For many years Ipswich had two newspapers, the other being the "Democrat" owned
by Mr. Frank Tracy. His office building was on the corner of First Avenue and
Third Street, and his family lived next door in the former Warner house. Mr.
Tracy came here in 1883 and was the first telegraph operator in Ipswich. In
1927, Mr. Tracy bought the Tribune from Mr. W.L. Johnson, and because it was
the first paper here, he chose not to change the name and ran both papers as
the Ipswich Tribune until 1928.

Spencer Tracy used to visit his uncle when he was a boy and must have enjoyed
it so much that when he became a famous actor, he mentioned these visits in an
article in a movie magazine.

Besides being the Congregational minister in Ipswich from 1885 to 1890, the
Reverend James Kyle was called, "The Father of Labor Day". In that era, U.S.
Senators were elected by the legislatures. On the fifth ballot, Rev. Kyle was
surprised to see that a few votes had been cast for him. On the fortieth
ballot, the Democrats swung behind him, and at age 37, he was elected U.S.
Senator from South Dakota. When he was re-elected in 1897, it was the
Republicans who came to his rescue. While he was in office, he introduced into
the 53rd Congress, a bill which was signed by President Grover Cleveland, which
stated that the first Monday of each September in each year, should be
celebrated and known as Labors Holiday.

Mrs. Kyle, a vivacious brunette, also made a mark for herself as a suffragette
and in Prohibition Circles.

Late in the summer of 1883, the Pioneer had three choices find water, move back
or die. Many wells that were dug turned out to be dry holes. These dry holes
often were not filled in and caused many an accident. One lady was ministering
to a sick friend and was driving home in the dark when one wheel of the buggy
hit the well hole, and she fell in. The horse went on home, and later she was
found dead.

Mrs. Alice Caborn arrived here the first of April in 1883, and said that all
the water she had to use until sometime in June was what they could dip up out
of sloughs, and it was necessary to strain and boil it.

In 1888-89 and 1890, Mr. Will Herrick had a business which provided water for
his customers each day except Sunday. The water sold for 50c a barrel or 10c a
pail. Mrs. Rose Parsons wrote years later   "How joyously we hailed the Herrick
water wagon as it brought the precious cargo of water, we never stopped to
count the deadly germs lurking on the tin drinking cup chained to the tank, but
just drank and drank. The next one waiting fearful all would be gone before his
turn came at the cup."

Olga Herrick told me that her father and Mr. Ed Jackson lived near each other
on farms northwest of town. One day Mr. Herrick approached Mr. Jackson about
starting a general store in Ipswich. Mr. Jackson said that he didn't have the
money at that time. Mr. Herrick said that if Mr. Jackson would keep the books,
he would furnish the money, thus a partnership was formed which lasted until
1929.

The July 12, 1884, Tribune stated that one of Roscoe's land men "took a drink
from our water barrel Wednesday." There was also a place where he could have
quenched his thirst because one of the first wells was right in the town square
and had a pump with a dipper fastened to it. This was also true at the
schoolhouse where a pail and a dipper were provided.

In 1888, a creamery was built. They had vats of buttermilk with a dipper so
people could help themselves.

That leads me to epidemics. One especially bad one was diphtheria, which caused
several deaths. Pupils were not allowed in school until they had been
vaccinated. This was a big decision in those days and a few never did go back to
school.

It was no secret when a family had measles or scarlet fever as a big red
cardboard sign was tacked on the front of the house, and it stayed there until
the whole family was fumigated.

Doane Robinson, former State Historian, reported that as far back as 1901,
Edmunds County had a "poor farm". I don't remember that, but I do remember a
"Pest house" on the east edge of town and as children we always walked as far as
possible from it, afraid we might catch some disease.

Many homes were fenced in with picket fences, some with barbed wire and others
with ornate metal ones. These came in handy when there were "run-a-ways" when
the frightened horses ran wildly down the street and plunged into anything. The
children were taught to immediately take refuge behind these fences. Another
time fences were a protection was when large herds of Texas long-horned steers
were sent up North to be fed on good old South Dakota grass.

        Residence of Dr. G. J. Bloemendaal, formerly the Warner House, a hotel
        in the early days of Ipswich.

At one time Ipswich had extensive stock yards, and many nights we were lulled
to sleep by the lowing of cattle being kept there prior to being shipped out.
There must have been a disease one year because I remember going down to watch
the cattle being run through a tank of slimy, green liquid.

In 1884, Emma Goodell left lowa to take up a claim in Edmunds County, and
college friend, Simeon H. Cranmer, come out and filed on a claim three miles
away. They were married in October, 1884, after she had proved up. They fixed
up Mr. Cranmer's shanty and lived there a few months before moving to Ipswich.
Mr. Cranmer was County Treasurer in 1885, and later cashier in the Bank of
Ipswich. Mrs. Cranmer was State President of the Women's Christian Temperance
Union and made quite a name for herself lecturing and doing reform work in
South Dakota.

Their daughter, Frances Cranmer Greenman, was a well known artist who painted
portraits of famous people in many parts of the world. In her witty book,
"Higher Than the Sky", Mrs. Greenman said that it was her father back in
Ipswich years before who put her mother on the lecture platform. He helped her
write her first speech. It was a success as the town drunk staggered into the
rear of the hall to hoot and jeer at the little woman, but stayed to pray and
came back after the lecture to sign the temperance pledge.

When elections were held and Prohibition won out, the church bells rang loudly
to let the people know the results. There was great rivalry between the
Anti-Saloon League and those opposed to it.

Mr. J.W. Parmley was active in the former, and because of this, a surprise
which he had planned for Mrs. Parmley never materialized. He had bought a house
in Roscoe and moved it to Ipswich as a surprise for her when she returned home
from a visit in the East. One night it was burned to the ground, thought to be
the work of the saloon people.

There was a time when any place where the grass was worn off was called a road.
Mr. Parmley changed that, and he is called Father of the Yellowstone Trail. He
was the first Superintendent of Schools in Edmunds County. This required a lot
of walking going from school to school. He was a natural born speaker and
promoter so was asked to speak at many public gatherings.

About 1900 Mr. Parmley was the first to use native stone when he erected his
office building which still stands and is in use. His daughter, Irene Trotzig,
tells this story about her father. One night after Mr. Parmley had closed his
office a man was killed in an accident or a brawl, and they asked Mr. Parmley
what to do with the body. He obligingly said that he would unlock his office,
and they could lay him out in his back office until morning. The next morning
Mr. McAllister, an employee, came to work not knowing about the dead man and
started apologizing for disturbing the sleeping man. Mr. Parmley told him that
he was not disturbing anyone as he was in for a long sleep, or words to that
effect.

We have Mr. and Mrs. Marcus Beebe to thank for the four native stone buildings
which add greatly to the beauty of our city. Mr. Beebe was a pioneer coming
from Sandusky, New York in 1882 to Aberdeen where he clerked in a store. In
1884, he moved with his family to Ipswich where he was president of the Bank of
Ipswich until his death in 1914. He was appointed a Regent by Governor Byrne.
They lived at first in the back of the wooden structure which was their first
bank. Their second daughter, Inez, liked to tell of the first Christmas there,
trimming a tumbleweed for their tree.

Judge Henry C. Briggs was another historic figure of this area. He came here in
1883 and settled on a claim one mile south of Ipswich. He practiced law in
Ipswich and was always an influence for good in the new town. The Briggs had two
sons, the younger one was editor of the St. Paul Daily News and later
Postmaster in Los Angeles, California. The older son had a prominent position
with Harper Brothers Publishing Company in New York City and wrote a book
called, 'Dakota in the Morning", telling of his younger days in Ipswich.

One interesting thing in the book was about the planting of trees on the claim,
and the work that it entailed. He called the trees, "Prairie Grove", and it was
one of the largest and best groves of trees around. Many picnics and
celebrations were held there, but now one hundred years later, very few can be
seen. This grove of trees which gave so much pleasure to so many was never
thought of as a back-breaking job to plant, but it was. The ten acres had to be
marked with twine to keep the rows straight and had to be watered by carrying
buckets full of water, to say nothing about digging holes in the ground. For
many years, "Prairie Grove" was a living memorial to a deserving man.

No story of pioneer days would be complete without telling about "The Country
Doctor". Our Dr. Hill ministered to the sick people of Ipswich and surrounding
country for forty-four years. If ever there was a life of service, this is
where it was. He was never too tired or busy to answer sick calls day or night,
no matter what the weather. Before the automobile, with his horse and buggy
with a soap stone for warmth, he broke through many a snowbank to get to his
patients. We used to love it when he came in from long drives and patted our
faces with his cold hands. It was often said that just to have Dr. Hill walk
into the sick room made the patient feel better.

He was born in County Down, Ireland, and began his practice of medicine in
Leola in 1894. The first baby he delivered was Grace Herried, daughter of
Charles Herried, later Governor of South Dakota. He married Bird Roe in 1895
and moved to Ipswich. The Hills lived across the street from my family, the
Meadows, which caused someone to make up the riddle, "Why is Main Street not
like other streets?" The answer, "Because there are Hills on one side and
Meadows on the other." Maybe not hilarious today but, at that time, it would
have been quite a journey to find either one.

The Turner family from Leola used to visit the Hill family. They had several
boys, and no matter what they might be doing when the train whistled, they
would dash out into the middle of the street and watch until the caboose went
by. There were no trains in Leola at that time.

There was a story about Dr. Hill stopping into a farm home on his way home from
a call. Nobody was home, but there were freshly baked loaves of bread on the
table. He happened to like crusts so he proceeded to cut off all the heels and
eat them. When the family arrived home, they knew exactly who had been there
because there was sickness in the neighborhood.

Caring friends and neighbors were so helpful especially in time of sickness and
death. They went to the homes where there was sickness and sat with the patient
through the night. In case of death, they stayed with the body to give the
family much needed rest. Often friends helped with digging the graves.

In recalling these incidents, I hope I haven't given the impression that the
pioneers didn't have any fun. They did. Letters written fifty years later never
stressed the seamy side of their lives, but always the pleasurable times.

To quote Mrs. Rose Parsons again, Dear Friends of yesteryears: Wish you could
realize what a thrill Elmer (especially) has had pouring over the papers you
sent which had the pioneer letters in them, bringing back scenes almost
forgotten. It's hard to realize a half century has passed since we skated,
sung, worked, froze and baked many years ago. While I've not forgotten the
prairie fires, hat winds, dust storms, blizzards and lack of water, they are
offset by the memories of the optimistic view we took of life and our social
contacts. I'm so glad for those early experiences and know now what a grand
time we had." Her husband added, "I read everything in the papers and looked
quite carefully to get the old familiar names, many of which had gone from me
with the years. The friendships formed in the years when we came together out
there seem a little more precious than do those formed later in life, and my
thoughts very frequently go back to the friends of those days."

Holiday dinners with four to six families which started in the eighties
continued for forty years in some cases and were fun times.

Mr. Ed Powers and my father were close friends even after the Powers family
moved to Oregon. Both lived to be over ninety years old. This is part of a
letter which Mr. Powers wrote to me after my father died. "You will never know
how I feel not to be able to write to your father. He and I met for the first
time in Minneapolis in 1883, and had our railway cars placed on the same train,
he from Wisconsin, and I from Michigan. All the years since we first met, he
was my trusted friend, no one ever doubted his word  I think back to the day we
were riding on top of that freight train from Groton to Aberdeen looking to
that great stretch of prairie, no stumps, thorns or briars and mighty few
rocks. What were our hopes and ambitions at that time? Some years, and many
experiences since that time, and not many of the ones we met and associated
with since that date are still on the firing line."

  The Future is silent
  Only the past and present
  Can speak to us."

                LOOKING BACK

News Article from Ipswich Tribune

        Cars parked behind Opera house.

The Ipswich Tribune
September 2. 1915
Ipswich an Automobile Town

So long as Ipswich is leading in the substantial things of life, it might not
come amiss to tell to the world the fact that there are mighty few towns with
population of some 700 souls that can boast of 100 automobiles. Lest some may
