The Years of War and Conflict

The Militia Act of 1903, made all male citizens subject to military service and
set up the National Guard as the organized militia. Until that time, the states
controlled the militia units entirely. Guardsmen have a dual status, because
they take an oath of allegiance to their state, as well as to the federal
government.

In April, 1904, a militia company was organized in Ipswich in conjunction with
Company A of the National Guards of Eureka. They became a separate unit
approximately ten years later.

Information regarding their activities has been difficult to substantiate. It
is known that twenty men led by Captain Lockwood E. Smith were stationed in San
Benito, Texas in 1915 and 1916, for eight months of military training. They were
known as the Machine Gun Company. The four machine guns and full equipment was
valued at $10,000 and was brought back to Ipswich and cared for by the company.

        Charlie Hiigle, Victor Olson Loren Parmley, Bob Owens, Julius Hardt,
        Roy Smith, John Strobele, Forest Lindamood, Camburn Daniels, Milo Van
        Dyke, LeRoy Craig, Frank Smith. Dave Picton, Lockwood Smith Maurice
        Mann, Jim Harris, Clarence Keller, Warren Fish and Bill Hardt.

There was a revolution against the reigning Mexican government, and as a result
of this, raids of American settlements across the Rio Grande took place. The
writer was unable to confirm whether or not the Ipswich guards were involved in
actual fighting on the border. Those stationed in San Benito were: Captain
Lockwood E. Smith, William Hardt, Warren Fish, Milo Van Dyke, Roy B. Smith,
David Picton, Frank Smith, Loren Parmley, Charles Hiigle, Camburn Daniels, Ed
ward Boyer, Maurice Mann, Robert Owens, James Harris, LeRoy Craig, Clarence
Keller, Victor Olson, Julius Hardt, John Strobele and Forest Lindamood.

On April 6, 1917, President Wilson, by order of Congress, declared war on
Germany. The local National Guards, enlistees and draftees assembled and camped
on the school ground. This site was chosen because of space and availability of
water. When school started, they moved onto the area north of the water tower
and across the alley to the east in the area around the old Opera House. They
left Ipswich in the fall of 1917, to serve in World War I.

In dedication of an Edmunds County Service Flag in 1918, 370 blue stars were
added for the boys that had enlisted or were drafted. Twelve gold stars for
those who made the supreme sacrifice later replaced the blue stars.

While the boys were away, the folks at home were keeping things going by
working with Victory Loans, Liberty Bonds, War Stamps, etc. Business men and
high school boys helped the farmers with shocking grain during the harvest
season. The Red Cross was very active under the leadership of Mrs. F. J. Tracy,
and she was ably assisted by many others. As auxiliaries were formed, much
sewing and knitting was done for the soldiers, as well as preparing surgical
dressings for their use. Kits were made and presented to each soldier as he
left for service.

The influenza epidemic of 1918, was a trying time for those at home. Dr. Hill
traveled night and day, getting his rest mostly while traveling from one
patient to the other. All nurses were pressed into assisting, as well as many
volunteers, An emergency hospital was started in the Rooming House (east side
of Fourth Street between Alma Avenue and First Avenue) and was ably managed by
Miss Helen Meyers, later Mrs. Harold Hembd.

All was not stress and strife as the social and educational gatherings
continued including chautauquas, home talent plays and prohibition rallies.

Edmunds County servicemen killed in World War I were William C. Dickerson,
Clarence W. Elstad, Clifford Evans, Adolph Forkel, Einar Jorgenson, William
Keppler, Carl Neis, Benjamin J. Picton, Walter L. Stanard, Harry A. Stroup,
Andrew Schaurer and Matt Kirzinger.

World War I veterans from Ipswich still living are George Finley, Emil Bobby,
Harold Gsell, Peter J. Geditz, Lloyd Taylor, Frank Neigel and Conrad Olson. W.
K. Herrick died in 1982 and Joe Kessler in 1983.

Dr. G.J. Bloemendaal served in the Student Army Training Corps while attending
Huron College.

        Shetland pony donated by J.W. Parmley to South Dakota State Red Cross
        sold at the State Fair Huron S.D. September 14 1917 for $974.63.

During the period after World War I, as young men returned to their families,
progress continued in Ipswich. Homes and buildings were constructed and the
Yellowstone Trail was completed.

Chautauquas, Fourth of July celebrations and the visit of William Jennings
Bryan were highlights for residents of Ipswich.

A period of economic growth was enjoyed until the greatest depression in
history began in 1929.

After the "Dirty Thirties" residents felt they had a right to have high hopes
for better times. Banks were in sound condition and paying a 10 percent
dividend to stockholders. Prices were still at low figures. The promise of a
break in the long period of drought gave farmers a reasonable justification for
their perennial optimism and favorable outlook for increased agricultural
production.

War was not to hit until later, but a new draft law required registration of
men between the ages of 21 to 36 before October 16, 1940. The Edmunds County
Draft Board held its first meeting and its three members were Fred Grosz and
Otto Helmer of Bowdle and Willard Brown of Ipswich. This board was to serve
together for six and one-half years with no change in personnel.

The darkest cloud of the forties was cast over the horizon on December 7, 1941.
It was a date that will "live in infamy" in the words of Franklin D. Roosevelt.
On that day, bombers of the Japanese Air Force attacked Pearl Harbor in the
Hawaiian Islands and destroyed a considerable amount of our naval strength at
that location. This happened at a time when Japanese government officials were
in Washington assuring those in command here in this country that differences
between the two nations would be resolved without conflict. From the start of
the Twentieth Century people of this country were being warned that Japan was
the "Yellow Menace" and the prediction was eventually found to be true.

This attack would thrust the United States into war, not only with Japan, but
Germany and Italy. It would put many of the 806 county draft registrants into
military service.

In the years following, 34 service men from Edmunds County were listed as
having lost their lives during the conflict. Twenty-seven of these were
pictured in the "Edmunds County in World War II" book published by the Veterans
of Foreign Wars Post 4807 of Ipswich. In the roll of honor with pictures or
records were over 800, and 30 of these were women, in the WACs, Waves, Marines,
Nurses Corps and Air Force. Many of those listed in the book were commissioned
and noncommissioned officers, including at least four Lt. Colonels, several
majors, about ten captains, near 40 lieutenants, several ensigns, a woman
pilot, probably in the neighborhood of 115 sergeants and many corporals. Many
of the service people were cited for heroic services.

        A visit from Wm. Jennings Bryan after WW I. J. W. Parmley, Mrs. Frank
        Tracy, Mrs. Perry Peters, William Jennings Bryan, James Champlin, Frank
        Tracy, Ann Bartholomew. H. E. Beebe. Helen Tracy and C. H. Rebrud.

On the home front during the war years many people extended themselves in
endeavors to make the lives of those in service more bearable. Public
recognition was given to B. B. (Dave) Hartzell of Ipswich, Paul Elford of
Roscoe and Art Merkel of Bowdle. These men carried on extensive correspondence
with people in the service. In addition to the letters, they performed other
services to aid communication among service people in the same area overseas.
Newspapers of the area regularly published letters from men overseas and
performed other functions helpful to the war effort. Other county people cited
for their services on the home front were Albert Senn of Bowdle, Henry J.
Schipke of Roscoe, the Rev. David Graham, John F. Holdhusen and Captain
Lockwood E. Smith of Ipswich.

Along with other women of the area, Ipswich women took their turn serving
pheasant sandwiches at the USO Red Cross Canteen in the Milwaukee Depot in
Aberdeen. These were provided for the servicemen aboard troop trains that
stopped in Aberdeen. Many letters from service personnel were printed in the
Aberdeen paper expressing their appreciation for this rare treat and indicated
it was a real highlight of their days away from home.

Another thing at home was experiencing the novelty of rationing. Many luxuries
and things considered as necessities were rationed. Food items, gasoline,
tires, et cetera, were among items said to be scarce. When tires wore out, they
were recapped, or maybe you stood in a line waiting your luck to get new ones.
Some things were in short supply and others debatable as to their availability.
Gas coupons were for four and then only three gallons for passenger cars.
People found that their "gas guzzlers" were almost economy cars when driven at
a slower speed, 35 was the speed limit. Some found they could mix stove gas and
kerosene, both unrationed and get to their destinations.

August 14, 1945, brought notice of surrender from Japan. The signing of the
surrender agreement between the Allied Forces and Japan aboard the U. S.
Battleship, Missouri, in Tokyo Bay on September 2, 1945, brought peace to the
world. All Americans could then look forward to the hundreds of service people
being discharged and returning home.

Young men from Ipswich who were killed in World War II were Bruce E. Ruby,
Joseph W. Mastel, Leonard H. Woodworth, Plynn K. Duvall, Randolph Pickett, Carl
Pickett, Thomas F. Archer, Aloysius M. Schumacher, Lloyd Blow, Bernard L.
Williams, Roy E. Samp and James L. Crisp.

Following the war, people were conscious of the presence of infantile
paralysis, and a county committee worked to aid those affected. At one point,
public meetings were discouraged because of the contagious spread of the
disease. It was to be a number of years later that this crippling affliction
could be remarkably and almost completely eliminated through the discovery and
taking of the Salk vaccine.

The Forties on the whole might be termed a unique period. It saw the return of
natural moisture to alleviate the drought. Experienced was World War II with
all of its strife, bitterness, hardship, death and anxiety. Better living and
service to the community came with the establishment of hospital service and
electricity to the rural community. It saw the introduction of dial telephones.
Like all periods there was cause for joy, sadness, but over all a satisfaction
with progress and an improvement in the quality of life.

        Corn Palace, J. W. Parmley originated the idea for this 16 x 48
        structure in 1907. Earl Perrin was the designer. It was located by the
        Milwaukee station and was decorated far several years using corn grown
        in Edmunds County often using as many as nine varieties of corn. In
        later years it was lighted by electricity.

In 1950, the United States became involved in a new conflict when it sent
military forces onto the Asian mainland to defend South Korea from Communist
forces. President Truman received powers to order all members of the reserves to
active duty. The Universal Military Training and Service Act, passed in June,
1951, required male citizens between 18 and 26 to register.

Many Ipswich young men entered service to again fight on foreign soil. Some
volunteered and others were drafted or recalled after being discharged
following World War II. The fighting in Korea ended when an Armistice was
signed on July 27, 1953.

During the Sixties, the United States began sending large numbers of American
troops to fight communist forces in Vietnam. Once again young men of Ipswich
were called to serve in the armed forces. It was not until January, 1973, that
the last of the Americans were withdrawn and prisoners exchanged.

Several of "our boys" received medals for heroic actions in both conflicts and
at least one, was severely wounded in Vietnam and spent many months in
hospitals recuperating.

Neither of these conflicts had much influence on the lifestyle of people at
home, but was the beginning of an era of much protest against government and
unrest among young people. All ages participated in peace rallies and
demonstrations protesting the involvement of the United States in Vietnam.
Women were demanding equal rights and the environmentalists were fighting for
clean air.

        1940, Dale Hagen, son of Mr. and Mrs. W. S. Hagen was appointed to the
        United States Military Academy at West Point, N. Y. Qualifications far
        nominations and appointment include high moral, physical and scholastic
        standards.

Television enabled people at home to view history making events, such as the
assassination of President John F. Kennedy in Dallas, Texas, November 22, 1963;
the shooting of Senator Robert F. Kennedy, a presidential candidate, in Los
Angeles, California, June 5, 1968 (he died the next day); Americans walking on
the moon in 1969, and the resignation of President Richard F. Nixon August 9,
1974.

The possibility of world-wide peace seems to be a dream never to be fulfilled,
as even now the use of nuclear weapons is being protested by demonstrations and
the forming of antinuclear war groups.

Those Memorable Depression Years

The Thirties, vividly recalled by those who experienced youth and adult years
in this locality are described as the worst of times. At the same time, further
reflection bring to memory some beneficial aspects of the period from 1930
through 1939.

What principally brought about difficulty and hard times in this area was the
successive years of severe drought. It is recorded that in 1931, 1933 and 1934
this region was said to have had the worst crop failures of any time that had
been accounted for. In 1932 and possibly other years grasshoppers were not only
numerous and huge but they could accomplish great destruction of all plant
life. Travelers had the experience of having to have the insects blown from
their car radiators if traveling any great distance. Record high temperatures
coupled with high winds contributed and added to agricultural woes.

Those lucky enough to raise somewhat of a crop were plagued with disastrously
low farm prices. Those engaged in agriculture at the time can remember corn
selling for 32 cents a bushel, oats at minus 16 cents, barley at 22 cents in
1932. Hogs sold for as little as three and one-half cents a pound. The next
year the average price for steers and heifers in December, 1933 was $2.70 a
hundredweight. Chickens sold for nine cents a pound and eggs brought 14 cents a
dozen.

If you were lucky enough to have a little money, you loved depression prices. A
single person could eat out at respectable cafes for not more than a dollar a
day. A man could get a good suit of clothes "off the rack" for as little as $15
and if you needed one made to measure the price might not be more than $35.
Would you believe two three-ounce hamburgers for five cents and sometimes pork
chops were advertised for a nickel apiece?

But who had money or a job? At this time when the country had only about 135
million people, unemployment was reasonably estimated at 25 million. This
decade followed the Wall Street crash of 1929 when banks closed and numerous
businesses folded.

In 1933 and several years afterward the black blizzards occurred. Top soil blew
and drifting to fence level height was not uncommon. Shallow lakes dried up and
those with deeper water and considered as recreation spots saw waters recede
from a few hundred yards to a quarter mile or more from their former shore
lines. Those were depressing sights, a transformation of these spots of natural
beauty. Finally some rains did come and better tillage methods were employed to
reduce the amount of blowing dust.

With the Franklin D. Roosevelt administration came Federal programs put into
operation which brought relief to farmers, town and city dwellers. There were
the feed and seed loans, the PWA (Public Works Administration) and then changed
to WPA (Works Progress Administration). These programs ushered in benefits
which could accomplish things which needed doing or had been neglected. Dams,
including Mina and others could be built with WPA labor furnishing some of the
work force. Adversity seemed to bring people together. If the government
brought in a carload of baled hay to town, labor was provided to have it
unloaded.

        West of Ipswich in the 30's.

In addition to the hard times, depression and drought there were other reasons
for this decade to be a time to remember. At this time, J.W. Parmley, a
pioneer, took up residence here and his interest in roads and other projects
was continually evident. It was in these years that he was credited with being
the "Father of the Yellowstone Trail" (US Highway 12). He envisioned and worked
for a Canada to Panama Canal highway and was significantly active in the
establishment of the International Peace Garden on the North Dakota-Canada
border. As a State Legislator he was the author of measures which benefited
road construction in the state for years to come.

It was in the thirties that the Marcus P. Beebe Memorial Library was
established, a gift from his children to the city. This unique structure with
an exterior of uncut prairie stone is a striking example of the skill of a
master stonemason. The Museum was started at this time and located in the
library.

The Bank of Ipswich was robbed in early May, 1932. The president, M. Plin
Beebe, was seriously wounded during the robbery and the bank's cashier, Chet
Doolittle was kidnapped and taken as a hostage and released in east central
North Dakota. Not long afterward the robbers were captured in Minnesota, money
was recovered and the criminals were imprisoned in the state penitentiary in
Sioux Falls. This was a time when other nationally known crime figures were in
the news including John Dillinger, Pretty Boy Floyd and the infamous Al Capone
who was the notorious Mafia leader in the Chicago area. This was also the time
when FBI Chief J. Edgar Hoover was considered a potent force for law and order.


The mid-thirties signaled the end of the bootleg liquor era. The 18th Amendment
was repealed in 1934, bringing the return of legal alcoholic beverages. With
the end of Prohibition, revenue from liquor, beer and wine brought in additional
taxes. Cigarette revenue also went up. Somehow people found the money to expend
on these items.

In sports this was the time of Dizzy and Daffy Dean, effective pitchers for the
St. Louis Cardinals and colorful characters who caught the fancy of baseball
followers nationwide. This was also when Joe Louis was possibly the most
skilled heavyweight boxing champion of all time.

After several years of planning the construction of Mina dam 14 miles to the
east was accomplished in just five months. This was considered remarkable
because there was not the large earth moving machinery available at that time.
A couple of years after construction two substantial spring rains put a sizable
body of water back of the dam, forming a body of water officially named Lake
Parmley. This became a fine recreation spot and a prelude for the site of many
summer and permanent homes. Then was to come the steady improvement of the park
with trees, roads and other facilities. First it was a county project and then
turned over to become a State Park.

Back in those days there was no television to watch but people were satisfied
with radio accounts of noteworthy events and sports contests. These programs
were sent live over the airwaves as compared to recordings which dominate radio
transmission today.

News stories indicated state and national office holders were aware of the
needs of financially stricken farmers. In one year it was reported that 40,000
farmers would need emergency assistance loans to harvest their crops. Senators
Wm. Bulow and Peter Norbeck worked on the problem in Washington and thee latter
announced that arrangement would be worked out for harvest loans to buy binder
parts and twine, Gov. Tom Berry was in the news with the announcement that any
able bodied man on relief who refused to work in the harvest field would be
deprived of further aid.

As the Thirties decade drew to a close people had reason to be apprehensive of
the future. Adolph Hitler was in power in Germany and Mussolini in Italy.
Military build-up was taking place in Germany in violation of the Treaty of
Versailles at the end of hostilities in World War One. War again started in
Europe. Their fears of the conflict's spread proved to be well founded.
                Ralph W. Bebermeyer

Saturday Night - Real Live

Saturday night in a small town! One really feels almost sorry for those who
have never experienced that very special night.

For many years, most farm folks came to town but once a week--on Saturday
night. Mom would hustle the younger children in and out of the bath and dress
them in their "town" clothes. Dad and the boys would come in from the fields a
little early, milk the cows and take their turns in the tub, while Mom finished
filling the egg crates and saw that the full cream cans were ready. Then it was
off to town for the big night.

The kids would stop to buy a bag of popcorn from Henry Lass, then they would
scatter to find their friends. Dad would unload the brimming cream cans and
would wait for the cream check. Mom would head for the grocery store where she
hoped that the price she received for the eggs would cover the cost of the
grocery bill. When the groceries had been packed into the empty egg crates,
they would be lined up across the store, where they would wait until the
families were ready to go home much later.

The biggest attraction for everyone was the weekly band concert at eight
o'clock. The musicians were all volunteers and, in the beginning, some member
of the band would serve as director. Anna Berg, a cornet player and county
employee, or H.A. Stevens, a local grain dealer and composer, frequently served
in this capacity. Some of the compositions of Mr. Stevens were performed by the
band.

The first band stand was of wood placed upon wheels, so that it could be moved
from place to place. At various times it was placed at the crossing north of
the drug store, in the lot south of the theater or on the lot south of the
present Bob Gross law office.

Records show E.L. Cochran was a charter member of the first city band in 1885.
Volunteers carried on this program until a musical director was hired for the
school in 1926. The city band was usually directed by this instructor. At this
time, the minutes of the city council reveal that a sum for the city band
director, players and music became part of the budget and continued to be
through the year of 1942.

The pay of the band members depended upon whether or not they showed up for one
practice a week and were in their places on Saturday night. The amount received
by each member was based upon how long he had played in the band. College
students, home for the summer, would join aspiring high school band members and
all profited from the experience of playing with older musicians. Occasionally,
a vocalist would join the band for a solo or two, adding a bit of variety to
the program.

The city band concerts were a true community effort and were greatly enjoyed by
everyone. After the concert, many people would head for the theater and the
second show. The rest lounged around the parked cars and talked over their farm
problems. The women often sat in the cafe over ice cream and coffee and caught
up on the latest news, occasionally going out to check on the baby asleep on
the back seat of the car.

Around midnight the cars would begin to pull up at the door of the grocery
store where a weary clerk would load the groceries.

Saturday night was over until next week! !
                Survilia Herrick

Our Town

        Main Street looking north in 1887.

        Fourth of July celebration on Main Street, 1908.

Residents discussed the incorporation of Ipswich in December of 1883, but not
until a vote in June, 1884, did it become a reality. County commissioners
divided the town into wards and an election was held to name one trustee from
each district; also, a clerk, assessor, treasurer, marshal! and justice of the
peace.

From its incorporation in 1884 until 1908, Ipswich had a trustee form of
government. At that time, this form of government was changed to the mayor and
alderman type. Though the boundaries have been changed, we have three wards and
two representatives to the council from each ward. They are elected at a city
election for a two year term.

        Ipswich Coronet Band, July 29, 1886 at the return of Major Burke from
        Washington. Major Burke with top hat and whiskers; Coronet, John Owens;
        Solo alto, Elmer Parsons, 1st alto, Everett Parsons: 1st tenor, F.J.
        Tracy; 2nd tenor, Harry Weickert; baritone, Cal Gougins; tuba, Ed W.
        Williams, snare drum, Charles Carleton and bass drum, John Samuelson.

        A bowl of water for the horses in front of  the Parmley House, June,
        1926.

The population of Ipswich was 500 in 1884, 1,187 in 1970 and 1,153 in the 1980
census. This decrease of 34 was the smallest of any town in the surrounding
ten-county area.

Since its beginning, the once designated "Zinnia City" has continued to make
improvements in all areas of its services. Streets at first were just dirt
which accommodated the horse and buggy traffic in all weather conditions. With
the coming of the gasoline powered automobile to replace the horse and buggy,
the dirt streets were inadequate. The first auto in Ipswich came when C. H.
Barron purchased a Rambler in 1903.

Eventually, the streets were graveled, and in 1937, the council gave approval
to the State Highway Department to put concrete pavement on U.S. Highway 12
from 8th Street to Broadway and State Highway 45 from 5th Avenue to Chestnut. At
one time during the late sixties, all other streets designated as such were
oiled. During the mid-thirties WPA labor was available for assistance in a
sanitary sewer project, sidewalks and curbing.

City wells have been dug in various locations and a constant endeavor made to
update water and sewer services to the residents.

Discussion began in 1902 to form a company in Ipswich for the purpose of
supplying electric lights to business places, residences and for use by the
village corporation to light the streets. It was not until 1908 that planning
resumed and in 1909 construction began on an electric light plant. In 1911
electric lights were installed in the Milwaukee Depot and in the St. James
Hotel. An excerpt from a 1930 newspaper stated that "a crew of workmen from
Northern Power and Light Company is busy with the job of installing a 'white
way' in downtown Ipswich." The present downtown street lights were turned on in
December, 1957. In 1945, Montana-Dakota Utilities purchased Dakota Public
Service and since that time has supplied electricity to the City of Ipswich.

One of the largest updating projects was initiated in September, 1980, south of
the railroad tracks, when funds were received from the Housing and Urban
Development program for community rehabilitation. City sewer was installed in
1981, and concrete curbs and gutters were poured in 1982. Funds were available
to remove old, unused buildings and help low income residents make improvements
on their homes. Plans include a park at the corner of Maple and Fifth Street.

Mayors and their years of service:
H.E. Stever, 1908 to 1911, Levi J. Thomas, 1911 to 1919; James A. Champlain,
1919 to 1921; F.E. Lass, 1921 to 1923; Frank Witz, 1923 to 1947; Morris F.
Dehnert, 1947 to 1951; W.A. Brown, 1951 to 1957; H.A. Beck, 1957 to 1965; S.G.
Fisher, 1965 to 1973; John Hein, 1973 to 1975; Peter Geffre, 1975 to 1979;
Mathew Forkel, 1979 to the present time.

In 1883, a post office was established with W.V. Bold being appointed the first
postmaster. Following him were John D. Pratt, James D. Jones, Allen B. Brown,
Arthur B. Chubbuck, Edward J. Engler, Harry Briggs, Levi J. Thomas (acting),
Iris Engler, Bob Blank (acting), Harlan Holsing (acting), Ernest Kub, Harold
Ewing and E. Howard Williams is the present postmaster.

        Trail Day in Ipswich, June 19, 1939.

        Trail Days 1940.

        Trail Day Parade, 1948.

In the beginning, mail came in twice a week and as often as two or three times
a day in the 40's when passenger trains went through frequently. By 1958, all
mail came in by truck and was dispatched by truck or highway post office. It now
comes in and is dispatched once a day by truck. We have gone from the one cent
postcard and three cent letter to the 13 cent postcard and 20 cent letter. In
1963, the use of the five digit zip code was encouraged.

With the passing of time, we have gone from silent movies where a pianist
played music appropriate to changing scenes to the present cinemascope; radio
with all the accompanying static to present day excellent listening AM and FM
stereo; to television first black and white to color with coverage of all major
events and the availability of cable television to city residents in 1980.

From the Dakota Tribune of October 18, 1883 "the lowest price for an inside
Main Street lot on the first three blocks from the depot was $255 and the
highest brought $290. Three hundred and eighty dollars was the highest price
paid for any lot, being the corner of Main Street and Third Avenue. For
comparison, the lots for the new building housing government services
constructed in 1979 were purchased for an average of $2,000. Residential lots
in the 30's were priced from $35 to $50 and in the early 80's average price is
$1,500.

        Trail Day Parade, 1948.

It is not possible to scan over 100 years of "our town" without covering Trail
Days first of which was held on Monday, June 19, 1939 when the city of Ipswich
turned the clock backward to bring back memories of early day history and
pioneer life. Early Trail Day celebrations were preceded by a "Booster Trip" to
surrounding towns. The parade was the major event of Trail Day.

In a May 11, 1933, news story in the Ipswich Tribune, it stated that a Golden
Jubilee committee was established consisting of Mrs. John Evald, W.H. Thielen
and L.J. Thomas. It was decided to celebrate Labor Day weekend September 2, 3
and 4. Jubilee stationery and envelopes were printed, but nothing can be found
to indicate the cancellation or observance of the occasion. As this was an era
of depression, one would wonder if the first Trail Day in 1939 was a delayed
Golden Jubilee.

The observance of our 75 years of existence on June 8 and 9, 1958 was two days
packed with activities hampered only by persistent rain the first day. Carol
Thommen and Gerald Bastian won titles as queen and king and Mrs. Katie Jenkins
and Fred Getsman the pioneer queen and king.

        Junior King and Queen in 75th Anniversary Parade. Galen Gisi, son of
        Mrs. Dorothy Gisi and Kathryn Dehnert, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Maynard
        Dehnert.

        1940 crowd at Ferdinand the Bull Days. Group in foreground left to
        right: Mrs. Alex Boer and son, Veronica Baer (Gisi) and Moletha
        Kranzler.

The observance of Trail Day continued through 1972. In June of 1973, FEM
Electric held their annual meeting in Ipswich. This was a festive occasion as
this rural electric cooperative was twenty-five years old at that time.

We joined the nation in celebration of two hundred years with three days of
activities on July 3, 4 and 5, 1976. The events included a parade, pageant in
the park and our first all school reunion. Many people came from great
distances to meet old friends and family members.

We are looking forward to our Centennial celebration on June 24, 25, 26, 1983.

What will the next one-hundred years bring??

        1982 Ipswich City Council. Back row left to right: Tony Thares. Jerry
        Markovetz, Mike Steen, Andy Thares, Warren Heitz. Front left to right:
        Jake Hammrich, Matt Forkel, mayor and Walter Haux.

Some Wild Flowers of South Dakota by Rose Seeley Miller, South Dakota

Mrs. Rose Seeley Miller, pioneer of Edmunds County, wife of A.H. Miller, was
born April 18, 1857 at Westmoreland, New York. In the spring of 1883, she came
to Dakota Territory, and in 1887 she married A.H. Miller, and they lived on a
farm in Fountain Township (one mile south and one mile east of Craven). They
had one daughter, Joy, who married John S. Clark of Putney, S.D.

Mrs. Miller was a gifted writer, and poet, and her articles were often found in
eastern magazines.

The flora of Dakota - for it was only just Dakota when I first knew it, and a
territory at that - impressed me vividly from the very first. I have spent a
good many months in the South, have lived in New York and in Michigan and am
familiar with the wild flowers, but I have never found anywhere the wealth and
richness and riot of bloom found upon the prairie.

In the spring almost before the snow is gone appears a tiny, insignificant
flower - insignificant save that it is a forerunner of spring and tells the
sweet story of the passing of the winter. It is of a faint, faint pink and
grows close to the ground, so close one can hardly pluck it with a stem. Little
children love this and eagerly carry it to the elders in proof that spring has
come and the winter is over and gone. The next flower that blooms is the Pasque
flower, or wild Crocus. This starts into bud very early, and is rarely later
than April in showing itself, and even in New York State wild flowers do not
bloom earlier. The Pasque flower is of pale lavender and its cups are much
larger than the common Crocus, its stem grows longer, and it is quite as
beautiful as the cultivated Crocus. This wild Crocus sends up buds before the
leaves hardly start, although later leaves and buds grow together. The first
wooly blossoms are found almost leafless, the buds wrapped about with a silky
texture as white as camel's hair and as soft. They grow rapidly and a very few
warm days bring out the flowers in profusion. I have seen acres of them -
almost. One may drive miles across the open prairie and have a path actually
strewn with flowers. Many times have we driven just for the express purpose of
gathering in a harvest of the Pasque flower, and been well rewarded, too.

The centre of this flower is of yellow, and this elongates as the petals fade,
until in time the seed top is as fluffy as the top of a Dandelion. If this
flower could be cultivated where it does not grow wild it would be considered
choice indeed, blooming so early, growing with such rapidity and seeding so
freely; have never, however, succeeded in getting it from seed, nor have any to
whom I have sent seed as far as I have ever learned.

Later on came such a host of blooms it would be difficult to name them. One of
a scarlet color we named the Wild Geranium because its leaves were cut and
notched like those of the sweet-scented Skeleton Geranium. This blooms for a
long period, coming probably in May and lasting some months. Its single blooms
are about an inch and a half in diameter, and they grow in clusters. It is a
low, small-growing plant, but bright and pretty in the green or dun color of
the grass.

One of the flowers with which the prairie is fairly sown, when the season is
wet enough to bring it to perfection, is one that used to grow in my mother's
flower garden, in old New York. We then called it the Spider Lily, or Widow's
Tears; why the latter it is difficult to determine, unless it might be the
evanescence of its duration. This plant has long sword-like leaves, narrow and
of grass-like texture. The flowers bloom in clusters and are of the most
uncommon blue - the fairest, clearest, deepest blue ever seen in any flower.
This is the color of the most of them; some, however, show purple and magenta
color, occasionally a pure white and a very deep pink; these are not common,
but the blue, the common blue is the dearest of all. After a rainy time in June
you may drive out across the fields and see these flowers lifting their cups of
fragrance, for they are very sweet. They are very profuse in blooming and while
the individual blossom fades very quickly, it is hardly noticed as it is so
soon replaced. They fade almost as soon as a Morning-glory after picking and
putting in water, but it is worth one's while just to go out some sweet June
morning and see the ocean of blue spread out in sweeping billows before you.

Another class of flowers found in great profusion is something belonging to the
Lentil family. These are very varied in their production, the first to appear
being of a purplish crimson color, or perhaps the wings of blue and the centre
of purplish red; these grow on flower stems of some inches in length, but their
habit is creeping and the fern-like leaves spread over the ground and the
blossoms, a little more erect at first, soon droop with the heavy seedpods that
begin to form as soon as a single flower withers. These seedpods are almost
round and are edible; the plant is called the Buffalo Bean. The pods, if taken
while very tender and succulent and cooked as per asparagus or green peas, give
a dish somewhat resembling asparagus or peas, and yet not either.

The Bean pods grow to be probably an inch or more in diameter and are much
nearer round than any other shape; they take on a red color as they enlarge.
They are pretty and look good, but the large pods are too tough to be of any
use. Of this same family is the most graceful little plant, bearing pods like
the Sweet Pea. Its habit is more trailing than the Buffalo Bean, and its
blossoms are most graceful and lovely. The flowers grow on long, slender,
trailing stems and are bright blue in color, exactly of a Sweet Pea form, and as
beautiful as the most highly cultivated Sweet Pea ever grown, while their color
marks them as a novelty, for while we have a variety of bluish-colored Sweet
Peas, I have never seen the clear blue found in these wild Sweet Peas. The
foliage is as graceful as the flower, being of a delicate, greenish white or
whitish green. The leaves are almost as delicate as those of the Cypress Vine.
It would seem as if this plant, taken by a plant wizard and put under
cultivation, might bring forth a harvest for its propagator.

Still another of this same family sends up tall flower stalks - no drooping
here, but erect and brilliant flames out of a purple torch made up of small
individual pea-shaped flowers, the stems tough and wiry. Still another is
shaped like the blossom of the old-fashioned red or purple Clover. At a casual
glance you think you have found a Clover, but on investigation you find it is
one of the large family of pea-shaped bloomers of the prairie. Another grows
to be almost a shrub and this bears the some fine, fern-like foliage and has
graceful spikes of drooping bloom and fine tiny white or cream-colored flowers,
still of the Sweet Pea shape, but very small. Few of these have much fragrance.


Another, while dissimilar, has a like feature in its fine-cut foliage. This
plant grows shrubby and bears long racemes of flowers of royal purple and, when
in full bloom, these are tipped with the most brilliant orange; this is the
pollen in each individual flower. These are very fragrant, and the plant, if
not wild, would be considered desirable. However, it is not in much favor
because of its tough, fibrous roots; these extend in all directions and are
difficult to remove. It has gained for itself the appellation of "Shoe Strings,"
from this fibrous root.

The first flowers have little fragrance but as the season advances the blooms
seem to take on deeper hues and a fragrance as of all the summer sweets made
into one intoxicatingly sweet draught. There are countless flowers bearing the
heavy perfume similar to the Sweet Honeysuckle. Among these is a delicate
little flower, growing probably not to exceed nine inches in height. Its
foliage is little to speak of, but its bloom reaches up on the slender stalks,
these crowned with many small blossoms very much like the ragged blooms of the
Sweet Honeysuckle, only these are small, all along a straight stalk, and of a
delicate pink in hue. The habit is for a few blossoms to open at the base of the
stork and as these fade new ones open. This is so dainty and sweet and fair
Milady might well prize it as a treasure among her garden glories.

We find wild Foxgloves such as I have seen cultivated in the East, these of a
faint lavender color and white spotted finely with a reddish brown. The real
Dandelion we do not have as a native, but it will not be long, as someone,
desirous of the real Dandelion greens, planted the seed and we now begin to
find the plants in places. A species of the Dandelion has always been with us;
this is less double and the petals coarser and looser than those of the old-time
flower.

Of yellow and purple we have a great variety. We have no real Daisies such as
are such pests in the Eastern meadows and pastures, but we have something of
the Daisy shape, though large and less graceful. There are a variety of deep
yellow blooms with cone-like centres; these cones enlarge, and when the flower
is in full bloom the brown cone is covered with yellow pollen from countless
diminutive stamens. These emit a delightful fragrance, something of the same
sweetness as that of the Sweet Honeysuckle. Some of the flowers are of the
deepest reddish-brown color and al I have the velvety softness of the Pansy.

A flower that might be a wild Zinnia is also seen; these grow on tall, stiff
stalks, with the crown of one single blossom. They appear mostly in faint pinks
and have a big brown button for centre.

In low, wet places, as the season advances we find the Calliopsis we cultivated
in former places. This grows in the rankest profusion, where it does grow,
which is by no means in all places. Along the roadside is a ditch or place where
earth has been taken out to build up the highway; this holds water for a long
time and along in June we see the feathery tassels of the Calliopsis and almost
before we know it we are dazzled by its brilliant bloom. We want to get out and
pick handfuls, but we find the flowers wilt very quickly and by the time they
could be put in water their heads would be sadly drooped; however, they will
freshen up again. Among these we occasionally find the deep, dark-hued flowers
but the yellow with the dark centre is the commonest.

This article would not be complete without mentioning the Wild Rose, fairest of
all fair wild flowers. These, too, can be found by the acre almost, and the
depth of the color depends a good deal upon whether the weather is sunny or
otherwise.

But the Sunshine State would lose half her sunshine were it not for the golden
glory of the Sunflower. These can be found in all sizes from the graceful
little bloom up to the shrub tall enough to shelter a bird's nest and make a
shadow to rest beneath.

Blest be this garden spread by Nature, filled by her own hands and offered free
to every seeing eye and flower-loving heart.

                BUSINESS

        Wall Implement, later became the First State Bank.

Ipswich got off to a fast start for in a few weeks after the town was platted,
the towns of Georgetown, Freeport and Edmunds (all within a three mile radius
of Ipswich) put their buildings on wheels and moved them into town. On September
20, 1883, the first lots were sold for $200, inside, and $300, corner. The
first building moved in was John Kearn's saloon from Georgetown. On September
27, 1883, the Dakota Tribune was the first building on Main Street and printed
30 copies on muslin. On October 4, 1883, the newspaper announced that the
Edmunds County Bank would be built soon. By October 25, Main Street lots were
selling for $325, inside, and $500, corner. Mr. John D. Pratt established the
first drug store on a lot next to the Tribune. W. V. Bold stated on November 1,
1883, that the Post Office would also be on Main Street.

Ipswich was named County Seat when the town of Edmunds canceled their plats and
the building was moved to Court House Square in Ipswich on November 1, 1883.
The tracks were completed into Ipswich on November 10, 1883.

Before the first year was over (September 1884) Ipswich boasted 102 business
buildings, 107 residences, 900-1000 people, seven large hotels with 200-300
guests per night and an assessed evaluation of over $ 130,000,

On December 30, 1884, fire struck and eight Main Street buildings burned with a
loss of over $30,000. The south end of Main Street was saved.

The Tribune noted in April, 1885, large quantities of buffalo bones were being
brought in from western counties to be shipped out by rail. Ipswich was the
terminus for the railroad and had a roundhouse to turn the engines around. The
rail was extended to Bowdle in the summer of 1886.

The first brick building was erected in June, 1885 by Cox Brothers. The
building was 25 x 100. Later enlarged to 25 x 140 and two stories, it was the
largest building in town when it was destroyed in the 1895 fire.

By 1887, Ipswich boasted 16 attorneys but still only two doctors for 1500
people. The Tribune printed 1400 copies weekly.

M. S. Cochran stated, "I think it was in '90 that Marvin and Orson Cochran,
John and Thomas Kimmitt, N. J. Engler, John C. Meyers, Joe Markovetz and I
formed what was known as the Edmunds County Dairy Association, and we built a
cheese factory six miles west of Ipswich which was in operation for 15 years.
We sold cheese for six cents a pound; eggs sold sometimes for five cents a
dozen, butter eight and ten cents a pound, cows 10 to 12 dollars a head and
wheat 40 cents a bushel.

In September, 1895, a major fire struck both sides of Main Street between First
and Second Avenues and destroyed all buildings with a loss of $80,000. The fire
was stopped from leaping First Avenue because the Gamble block (now the drug
store) was built of brick.

        Main Street

Another major fire erupted in October, 1898, when both sides of Main Street
between Alma and First Avenues burned with the exception of the Gamble block. A
total of 19 buildings went up in flames in the early morning fire. Pictures of
Main Street in 1900, show that the west side was quickly rebuilt using brick
construction.

CASH DEPARTMENT STORE

In 1908, the first movie theatre was located just north of the present one. The
projector had to be hand cranked. "The Birth of a Nation" was shown on March
28, 1917. It was advertised as the biggest thing in the history of Ipswich.

The following is the text of an article as it appeared in print on December 9,
1911, concerning the old settlers banquet at St. James Hotel:

The Old Settlers Meet and Eat

"About the year 1882, many from Eastern states journeyed to the territory of
Dakota. In 1883, this number was augmented by thousands of others, until
eastern Edmunds County claimed as great a population as it has at the present
time. Each year thereafter, until 1890, new settlers continued to arrive.

Two hundred of these pioneers, including their wives and children, gathered
around well spread, heavily laden tables, at the St. James Hotel, last Saturday
night; listened to speeches, telling of early day experiences and had a general
good time.

The hotel was inadequate to the proper accommodation of the crowd and banker
and capitalist jostled elbows with farmer, mechanic and laborer.

It was necessary to convert the hotel lobby into a dining room that the
overflow might be accommodated. Even this did not relieve the congestion, and
three times was the dining room cleared and refilled before C. H. Barron, the
toastmaster, rapped for order and announced that speeches would be listened to.


No set program of talks had been previously arranged and toastmaster Barron
called up George C. Meadows, W. E. Huck, Orson Cochrane, M. A. Slocum, R. F.
Finch, J. W. Jamieson, H. T. Dailey, F. J. Tracy, S. V. Arnold, Joseph Royle,
E. L. Cochrane, M. Plin Beebe, W. P. Wilson, W. H. Fessenden of Wetonka and
others, who, in turn, talked interestingly of their varied experiences since
landing in Edmunds County, between the years of 1882 and 1890. Rose Seely
Miller read an original poem, which was a gem. Gladys Slocum recited, and Griff
Williams sang beautifully, all of which added enjoyment to the festivities.

J. W. Parmley, S. V. Arnold and M. A. Slocum were the committee in charge of
arrangements and that they performed their duties well was evidenced by the
smoothness and clockwork precision with which matters moved.

A series of badges had been provided, colors indicating the year of arrival.
Red predominated, for that indicated the year 1883, when the influx was the
greatest.

At the close of the speaking, a permanent Old Settlers Association was
organized. F. J. Tracy was elected president and M. Plin Beebe secretary. These
officers were empowered to name other officers and necessary committees."

        September 1917 Ford ad

        Garage purchased to be used as a Ford garage by Sam Hagen. Formerly
        owned by John Thompson for a livery barn. 1916

        "The Barn" 1982

Seventy-nine years of transportation service came to an end on Saturday,
October 2, 1982, as the auctioneer sounded his gavel, and chatted his call
"sold", the barn in Ipswich ceased its era of transportation.

In 1903 the barn was built by a Mr. K. T. Lee and served as a livery stable
until 1919.

At the onset of the automobile, Sam Hagen purchased the livery stable and
started an automobile and repair shop business. Hupmobile was one of the first
cars offered, later adding several other makes, which included Buick and
Chevrolet. In later years it became a sole Chevrolet dealership. Sam's son
Earle took over the Chevrolet dealership in 1941 until his retirement in 1975.
At that time, he sold the dealership to Keith Dehnert, who ran the Chevrolet
dealership and garage until its close August 31, 1982.

        1911, K. L. Lee livery stable built by him in 1903 and used as a livery
        stable until 1919. Later purchased by Sam Hagen who started an
        automobile and repair shop business. His son, Earle, took over the
        Chevrolet dealership in 1941 and retired in 1975. Keith Dehnert
        operated the business until August 31, 1982.

Many changes in the transportation field have occurred during this time span.
With changes in transportation and people's driving habits, dealerships have
also changed and many have closed, just as Dehnert Chevrolet in Ipswich. We
will see other changes in the transportation field in the future years. But the
barn will always be remembered as the best in its field.

The Lux Candle Factory had its beginning in 1934, established by Father
Bormann. It was later owned by Theresa Heinz, and today continues to produce
100 percent bees wax candles under the management of Francis Heinz, Lawrence
Heinz and James Markovetz.

The Jon Schan Bakery burned in February, 1936, and was not rebuilt on that
site. We continued to have a bakery off and on until the 1970's.

The following businesses promoted "Sky High", a local talent play, about 1939.

Crissman Drug, H. S. Crissman; First State Bank; H. E. Beebe, real estate;
Engler Hardware, Bert Engler; Clarence Linden, stone and gravel; Nick Kasel,
undertaking: Frank Michalek, shoe repair; W.G. Woodward, dry goods; Owens and
Witz, coal; H. L. Woodworth, lawyer; Northern Seed and Nursery, John Taylor;
Farmers' Equity Elevator; Red Owl Food Store, Pete Moe; Ipswich Cash Store, J.
A. Juster; William Heimann, beer parlor; Ipswich Dairy; Leo J. Hoerner,
insurance; Germain Shoe Repair, Harry Germain; Atlas Lumber Company, L. C. Fix;
Hall and Schmidt Auto Company; Ipswich Creamery; Herrick and Jackson, general
merchandise; Lass Brothers Auto Company; Ipswich Truck Line, Howard Lass; C & C
Cafe, Lillian and Lloyd Cronholm; Engler Land Agency, Annie Engler; Wally's
Texaco; Northern Power and Light; M. Plin Beebe, bonds; Ziebarth's Market,
Martha Ziebarth; Brown and Barclay, variety and undertaking; Gamble Store
Agency, J. W. Jeffery.

Another major employer in Ipswich, the Ipswich Lumber Co. had its grand opening
in March, 1953, in its newly constructed facility on east Highway 12. The
business was organized by Howard O. Samp, John J. Beigler, and Henry Beck. It
is now owned and operated by Loren Leitzke and Herbert Wockenfuss.

Our largest employer and only surviving implement dealer is the Taylor
Implement Company. Organized in downtown Ipswich in 1955, by purchasing Wall
Implement, Harley Taylor, owner, in 1961 built a new and larger structure on
Highway 12. This John Deere dealership has been enlarged a couple of times, the
last major addition in 1980. Owners and managers include Harley Taylor, Miles
Taylor and Irvin Taylor. Harley Taylor purchased the John Deere dealership in
Aberdeen and moved there to manage it. Miles Taylor purchased the dealership in
Great Falls, Montana and moved there.

        1938, Len and Frances Engler in Engler Hardware Store.

In the late 1960's, Clarence Linden constructed a complex consisting of Linden
Lanes and Cafe and Phillips 66 Station. Later this operation closed, and the
structure is now occupied jointly by the Ipswich Farmers' Station and Ken's
Fairway.

Colonial Manor of Ipswich, a 59 bed nursing home, was constructed and opened in
1972. This business is owned and operated by C. M. Corporation of Sioux City,
lowa. Promotion and backing for this new venture came from the newly formed
Ipswich Community Development Corporation.

The Development Corporation purchased 180 acres one mile east of Ipswich and
erected a steel building. This building was leased to Dakota R. V. Corporation
with Alford Martschinske as owner and manager. This factory constructs toppers
for pickups. More recently they have added a polypropylene rope manufacturing
unit to their operation.

        St. James Hotel

                LANDMARKS

        Courthouse 1912

Edmunds County was organized July 26, 1883, by a meeting of the Board of
Commissioners appointed by Governor Ordway. A building from the townsite of
Edmunds served as the first Commissioners' office.

The first Edmunds County courthouse was built in 1889-90 of stone and brick. It
cast $11,000 and was of above average construction for its day. It also served
the community as a place of safety when there was an Indian scare and gave
security to the people of Ipswich when they suffered from destructive fires.

By the early 1930's, the courthouse had become too small to accommodate the
needs of the county offices. Talk began about building a new courthouse. Due to
the depression and many crop failures, citizens were reluctant to vote for any
bond issue at this time. When Legislature convened in special session, it was
found that some counties had accumulated a substantial building fund. Edmunds
County had a fund of $61,000 which put them in a position to build, provided the
matter of a bond could be facilitated.

On February 19, 1934, the Edmunds County Board of Commissioners consisting of
Thomas Hansen, Chairman; Michael Hauck, Tom Langan, Richard Lemke and William
Stafford adopted a resolution consisting of entering into and executing a loan
agreement with the United States of America. Agreement was to purchase $61,000
of Edmunds County bonds dated September 1, 1933 for the purpose of building a
courthouse and jail at Ipswich. The United States of America would then grant to
said Edmunds County a sum equal to thirty percent of the cost of the labor and
material.

        September 1920 Mabel Sinclair, Gladys Coulter and Levi Thomas, auditor.

        Courthouse

Before any construction could begin the county offices were moved to two
buildings on Main Street, the *American Legion Building and another small brick
building while the old courthouse was being torn down under the supervision of
J.D. Williams. The county hired a night watchman to safeguard the records in
case of fire while renting these offices.**

The new courthouse construction began in the spring of 1934 and was built in
accordance with the National Industrial Recovery Act. In regard to the general
provisions of the loan agreement, definite stipulations were set down. In
regard to labor, first preference was given to ax-servicemen with dependents,
then to citizens of the United States and aliens who declared their intention
of becoming a citizen and who were all bona fide residents of South Dakota
qualified for the work.

In regard to materials, all materials were to be produced in the United States
with preference given to the use of locally produced materials if they
qualified with the government requirements. No convict labor was to be employed
nor materials manufactured or produced by convict labor could be used. The
maximum of human labor*** was to be used in lieu of machinery wherever
practical.

Roland R. Wilken of Watertown, South Dakota was chosen as the architect and the
Gray Construction Company of Watertown, South Dakota was the general
contractor. The courthouse was built of stone, brick, concrete, steel and tile,
completely fireproof. The exterior of the building was constructed of brick,
trimmed with natural Bedford stone. The interior walls were finished with a
fine grade of pink Tennessee marble. The courtroom was designed for better than
average acoustics and a ventilating system. The architect stated that the
building was so constructed as to stand 500 years at an approximate cast of
$96,000.

On January 7, 1935 the new courthouse was accepted by the Board of County
Commissioners. The county officers moved into the new building on January 16,
1935 and the new courthouse was dedicated in June of 1935.

This building, as of 1983, provides fourth floor space for storage, third floor
provides space for the courtroom, the Circuit Court judges' offices, jury rooms
and the County Highway office. Second floor offices consist of the Clerk of
Court, Register of Deeds, Auditor and Treasurer. The first floor offices
include Director of Equalization, County Health Nurse, Extension and Social
Services offices, as well as the community room. The sheriff's office,
sheriff's apartment, janitor's office, jail and storage occupy the basement
space.

In the past ten years the building has been updated with the installation of
new windows, wiring, carpeting and air conditioners in each office, as well as
being repainted. During the summer of 1982, new air conditioners were installed
in the courtroom.

As of this Centennial year the following is a list of officials with offices in
the building. Auditor, Irene Rissmann; Treasurer, Edith Baus; Register of
Deeds, Edward Dosch; Clerk of Court, Jean Lingor; County Highway
Superintendent, Milton Aman; Director of Equalization, Betty Stevens Roesch;
Social Services, Donna Scott; Extension Office, Dale Curtis and Carole Sweeter;
Sheriff, Dennis Olson; State Area Health Nurse, Kathy Gerdes; Janitor, Joe
Martel; Commissioners, Ray Bachman, Ray Forkel, Robert Stoecker, Louis Thares
and Marvin Uhrich.

*burned in 1949
**rented for $16.50 per month
***labor, $1.20 per hour, skilled; .50 per hour, common

The Memorial Arch

        J. W. Parmley in front of Welcome Arch looking east, 1926.

This very unique structure has not only an interesting history, but is a
significant memorial with a double purpose. One of the reasons it is unique is
that it is believed to be the only arch which for many years rested over a
national highway. Its purpose was to honor the twelve men who lost their lives
in World War I, and it pays tribute to a noteworthy resident and pioneer,
Joseph W. Parmley, a man possessed of tremendous energy and who with his
co-workers founded the Yellowstone Trail.

When the Armistice was signed on November 11, 1918, ending hostilities in World
War I, the opportunity to honor those Edmunds County boys who fought in this
conflict presented itself. The ones who had not lost their lives would be coming
home. Citizens decided to erect a monument as a lasting tribute to their valor
and it should be constructed across Main Street.

Earl R. Perrin, architect, selected his design from many triumphal arches of
ancient, medieval and modern times. The arch spanning Main Street and First
Avenue was built of the best lumber and metal available. On each of the pillars
was painted a picture of a soldier of the Civil War and World War I. Overseeing
of the work was done by Mayor James A. Champlin and the City Council. John D.
Williams and John Thomas, also deserved credit for the fine piece of
construction. The wooden arch stood for many years.

Ipswich became a busy town and it soon became evident the arch was obstructing
traffic. A decision was reached to move the arch to a permanent location where
the travel of all on the highway would pass under. It was moved four blocks
north to span Highway 12, by then designated as the Yellowstone Trail. This was
later twisted and moved off its foundation in a severe windstorm.

It was at that time the citizens of the community came to the conclusion that
an arch of reinforced stone should be erected. No other location was even
suggested. The American Legion Post and the Lions Service Club were appointed
to take full charge of plans to construct a reinforced stone arch. Approval was
received from the State Highway Commission and C.J. Madison of Corpus Christi,
Texas was hired to do the stonework. Construction began on September 3, 1 928.

        Original arch located on Main Street

After the l-beams were purchased to be the basis for the horizontal part of the
arch, about four tons of scrap iron was assembled for reinforcing material. The
first six feet of the pillars were of granite in broken ashlar. Crazy masonry
for the next eight feet and rubble stone for the top twelve feet were laid with
artistic skill. Stones for the arch came from many parts of the world,
including Europe, Asia, Africa and the Philippine Islands, as well as stones
from nearly every state through which the Yellowstone Trail passes.

The two piers are capped with round boulders. One of granite was brought from
the very far north by the Wisconsin glacier and topping the other pier is a
boulder taken from the Cannonball River in North Dakota.

Two bronze plaques, one on each pillar were installed at a cost of $350. One
reads, "This pi liar of the Memorial Arch of Ipswich is dedicated as a lasting
tribute to the vision, foresight and tireless energy of Joseph W. Parmley and
his co-workers who in 1912 founded the Yellowstone Trail. The first portion of
the Yellowstone was built from Ipswich, its home, east to Aberdeen. From that
beginning that great highway grew to open our nation from Plymouth Rock to Puget
Sound."

The plaque on the other pillar has the following words: "This pillar of the
Memorial Arch of Ipswich is erected in honor of the valiant men of the
community of Ipswich who answered our country's call to arms in the World War
1917-1918 and as an everlasting monument to those who so nobly made the supreme
sacrifice." Underneath this statement was inserted their names: William C.
Dickerson, Clarence W. Elstad, Clifford Evans, Einar Jorgenson, William
Keppler, Adolph Forkel, Carl Weis, Benjamin J. Picton, Walter L. Stanard, Henry
A. Stroup, Andrew Schauer and Kirzinger.

        Welcome arch at present location in the park.

        Hiram Beebe in front of Arch when it was across Highway 12

In 1973, the city of Ipswich was advised that due to widening of U.S. Highway
12 through Ipswich, the Arch would have to be moved and could not be done with
highway funds. Through the efforts of the Ipswich Development Corporation, the
legislature appropriated funds for this purpose. Property was purchased just
east of the Charles Collier Park, and beside U.S. Highway 12 and this is where
the Memorial Arch now stands.

Raymond and Lester Forkel of Ipswich and Marvin Lout of Aberdeen started moving
the Arch on December 1, 1973. The south pillar toppled and broke into several
pieces during the moving. Darwin Diede, an Ipswich cement contractor rebuilt
it. This was completed in June of 1 974.

On March 1, 1973, word was received that the Memorial Arch had been approved
for nomination to the National Register of Historic Places. A rededication
ceremony was held on July 5, 1976 as part of the Bicentennial celebration.
Irene Trotzig, daughter of Joseph W. Parmley, was present for this special
occasion.

Yellowstone Trail

        This is the gumbo slough, the worst place on the famous Yellowstone
        Trail from Plymouth Rock to Puget Sound. Made passable for all time by
        courtesy of Gilbert Mfg. Co., Aberdeen, S. D. Gilbert Grader were the
        words on the sign being held by J. W. Parmley at the site of the gumbo
        slough east of Ipswich. Jake and John Zahalka in the cab.

Under the stimulus of the late J.W. Parmley, pioneer good-roads booster, a
conference of business men was called in Ipswich in the spring of 1912 at which
the proposal was made that an organized effort be made to promote a good road
connecting towns and cities all the way from Minneapolis to Yellowstone Park.
Following that meeting other gatherings were held in rapid succession in
Aberdeen, Mobridge, Miles City and other towns, culminating in a big meeting at
Lemmon where the final organization was perfected in October 1912. The Lincoln
Highway association was not organized until 1913.

Mr. Parmley was elected president of the Yellowstone Trail association and for
years thereafter he devoted much of his time and effort to the promotion of the
trail. His enthusiasm became contagious and the trail expanded until in 1916, it
adopted a slogan "A Good Road from Plymouth Rock to Puget Sound."

Each state west of Ohio was first thoroughly organized and a "trailman" was
selected in each town to be the representative of the parent organization. Each
year representatives from each town were sent to a state meeting where local
and state problems were discussed and a member selected to represent the state
on the executive committee of the association.

The association at once began the task of marking the trail with the insignia
of a yellow circle with a black arrow pointing toward Yellowstone Park. It was
marked from Syracuse, N.Y. to Seattle, Wash. via Buffalo, Cleveland, Ft. Wayne,
Chicago, Milwaukee, St. Paul and Minneapolis, Aberdeen, Miles City, Missoula
and Spokane, The present day U.S. Highways 12 and 10 roughly follow the
original route of the Yellowstone Trail.

        Concrete markers held by J. W. Parmley to be erected through Edmunds
        County on the Yellowstone Trail.

        Finished for the present. Travel and maintenance will make this a
        splendid boulevard five miles east of Ipswich. Fall of 1912.

        Trees one mile east of Ipswich were planted by M. Plin Beebe in the
        spring of 1904 when he was home from U.S.D. From the height of the
        trees this picture was token about 1912. 1912, Cheap ($100 00 a mile)
        graded road one mile east of Ipswich is caption under picture.

During the summer of 1917 the association established three tourist information
bureaus. The number was increased to seven in 1918, and by 1923 there were
thirteen bureaus maintained at the following points: Walla Walla, Spokane,
Missoula, Butte, Billings, Miles City, Aberdeen, Minneapolis, St. Paul,
Milwaukee, Chicago, Fort Wayne and Cleveland.

The purpose of the bureaus was to help the traveling public with information
and to encourage the American public to travel by automobile. Each bureau was
supplied with maps published by the association in addition to booklets and
folders on local points of interest and summer resorts. Each bureau exchanged
information on the condition of roads in his section. In 1923 more than 250,000
tourists utilized the services of these bureaus. In the first year of operation
they serviced only 800.

In addition, the association provided a road condition map service to hotels,
garages or restaurants who maintained membership in the association. The
service consisted of a large wall map of the entire 3,700 mile trail and a
weekly report on the condition of each section of the trail. The service was
supplied from May to September each year and from 500 to 1,000 business places
availed themselves of the service.

The result of thus organizing these local groups into a sustained national
program was that in each state more work was done and more money was spent in
building better roads along the route of the trail. Another result, of course,
was an enormous increase in tourist traffic over the route.

Cities and towns along the route began to make accommodations for the motoring
tourist. Free camp grounds were first provided to be followed later with cabin
camps. In 1922, it was reported that there were 126 civic free camp grounds for
tourists along the Yellowstone Trail. There were usually kitchen facilities,
plus water, wood, toilets and in some cases shower baths. In Walla Walla a
large city park was turned over to tourists and a "Greeters Committee" from the
Commercial Club called on the tourists in the park each evening.

The Yellowstone Trail received nation-wide publicity in 1915 when Mr. Parmley
undertook to carry a letter from the mayor of Chicago to the mayor of Seattle
in 100 hours by a series of relay cars. The distance was 2,611 miles and the
time consumed was 97 hours. This was largely over dirt roads, hundreds of miles
of which were only cowpaths three years earlier.

The following year the organization carried a letter from the Secretary of War,
handed by him to a driver at Plymouth Rock, directed to the Commandant at Fort
Lawton, Seattle. The trip of 3,754 miles was made in 121 hours and 12 minutes.
The bearers of this message faced a rainstorm for over 1,000 miles and at times
could not do better than 15 miles per hour. The best time was made across
Montana where an average speed of 34 miles per hour was maintained. Police
escorts were provided through the cities traversed en route.

It was largely due to the consistent efforts of Mr. Parmley and the Yellowstone
Trail association that Yellowstone Park was finally opened to automobile
traffic.

During the twenties the efforts of trail boosters was centered on getting an
all-weather graveled road across the state. Mr. Parmley lived to see this part
of his dream come true but he died in 1940, ten years before the final link in
the Trail in North Dakota was paved. The last stretch in South Dakota, west of
McLaughlin was completed in October 1950.
                Irene Parmley Trotzig

The preceding article was reprinted from the Ipswich 75th Anniversary book.
Irene Parmley Trotzig is the daughter of the late J.W. Parmley.

Improvements have been made through the years to maintain U.S. Highway No. 12
as an excellent coast to coast highway.

J. W. Parmley Home BUILT IN 1919

This home has been designated as a historical site. J. W. Parmley is known as
the Father of the Yellowstone Trail that now extends from Plymouth Rock to
Puget Sound and is known as U.S. 12.

J.W. Parmley was a notable man, a pioneer in the true sense of the word. He was
born in Mifflin, Wisconsin, educated in Platteville and Lawrence College, and
in ,883 started west for South Dakota. He first settled in Roscoe, but when the
railroad headed west and travel was opened from Aberdeen to LeBeau, he moved to
Ipswich. Mr. Parmley lived in this house until his death on December 12, 1940.

Several people of the community thought it possible to purchase and restore the
home and have it ready for tours during our centennial year, 1983. This became
a community project, and the response has been great.

The directors of this project are: Phyllis M. Herrick, president; James
Morlock, vice-president; Bonnie Hodson, secretary-treasurer. In addition to
these officers the other directors are: Arnold Petersen and Ruby Hembd.
Memberships will be taken at a later date.

The directors bought the Parmley home in October of 1981 through donations from
many people, including former residents and home town people who supported the
idea of restoring this structure. Plans are to have this the new location of
the Edmunds County Museum which is now located in the basement of the City
Library. A number of hours of volunteer time and energy have been spent in
preparation to have this home restored and ready for the museum by the spring
of 1983.

The directors hope to have the living room furnished with pieces from the era
of the 1900's. The other rooms will contain museum articles.

The house itself is constructed in an interesting manner. It is built of brick,
cement and plaster. The floors, stairway, as well as the bath tub are solid
cement. The woodwork throughout is cherrywood. There are two beautiful
fireplaces, the stones used in their construction are from all over the world.
In addition, many sea shells were used.

Thirteen miles east of Ipswich is the Parmley Dam that Mr. Parmley started
during the depression of the 1 930's. He also promoted the establishment of the
Peace Garden on the North Dakota-Manitoba border along with the Canada to Canal
Highway.

Eight years ago Mr. Parmley's portrait was among seven that were honored by
being hung in the South Dakota Hall of Fame located in the Highway Building in
Pierre.
                Phyllis M. Herrick

Marcus P. Beebe Memorial Library

The Marcus P. Beebe Memorial Library was dedicated on August 21, 1931. It was
built by the heirs of Marcus P. Beebe. These included his wife, Leota, and
their children, Inez Perisho, Gertrude Mears, M. Plin Beebe, Hiram E. Beebe and
Marjorie Beardsley. This building stands as a permanent memorial to one of the
first pioneer men that came to Dakota Territory in 1881. It was a dream come
true for the city and all the people that worked so many years to establish a
library.

As we review the construction and design of this unique building, we find it is
built of native uncut granite rock which is found on the surrounding prairie.
These rocks are a product of the glacier period that covered these prairies
thousands of years ago. , The interior of the building is one large room, has a
beamed ceiling and seven foot high book shelving. Most of the woodwork and
furniture is of oak and was finished by Aberdeen Dakota Sash and Door Company.
The floors and walls are of concrete reinforced with steel bars.

The walk up to the building, the lobby floor and stairs to the basement are all
laid with marble from Italy, Belgium and Austria. The steps into the library
room are gray Napoleon marble.

        The Prayer Rock in its original position on a hill top overlooking the
        Missouri River south of Mobridge. The story is that before the white
        man came, early travelers had worn a deep path leading to the rock and
        made the two sets of hand prints into its surface. Through the efforts
        of H. E. Beebe and the moving by Paul Cochran, this five ton boulder is
        now positioned on the south side of the sidewalk leading to the Marcus
        P. Beebe Memorial Library. The Prayer Rock is placed with the same
        incline as was in its original position overlooking the Missouri River.

A large native stone fireplace graces the west wall. Many Indian artifacts are
included in the design. These also came from the prairie land near the city.

In October, 1931, the Ladies' Library Association donated an electric
grandfather clock. A beautiful gift to the library; it chimes the hours out
each day.

In 1885, the women of this prairie town realized a need for a place to keep
books for reading and lending. The Ladies' Library Association was organized
and a small building put up to house this project. There were 25 members and
each one donated a book. Fire took this venture, destroying all of the books
and the building. The Ladies' Association reassembled books and housing and
again fire destroyed it and the early records of the library.

In 1889, the library was established in an almost fireproof room leased from
the Home Lodge. Here the ladies took turns volunteering to operate it. In 1905,
the Home Lodge association granted a 99 year lease for a room the library
occupied.

Miss Jennie Crissman was elected the first librarian May 1, 1901. Miss Helen
Smith followed her in November of 1901. Genevieve Tate accepted the position
August 2, 1905. At that time the City Council was asked to levy a one mill tax
on each tax dollar for library funding. It was granted and $100 realized from
it.

In 1910, Mrs. B.E. Allen became librarian and served until 1923. Mrs. Lillian
Witz helped her and took over when she left town.

By 1917, the library contained 1,542 books. This served 150 teachers in Edmunds
County and the Ipswich population of 900. There were eleven debating societies
and reading clubs. The operation of the library was funded by socials, fines on
books, library dues and dues of $1.00 from the 48 library associate members.

Several attempts were made to obtain a loan from the Carnegie Library
Foundation to build a library building, but the loan was never granted.

In April, 1918, the library was moved to the old bank building.

At this time the City Council took over the money funding and added a 2 mill
tax levy for the operation of the library. Then the Ladies' Library Association
disbanded and put $50 on savings toward a new building for the library.

In 1923, Mrs. Lillian Witz became librarian and served the public for the next
42 years. She was the first librarian to work in the new Marcus P. Beebe
Memorial Library. In 1965, Mrs. Carson Jensen became librarian when Lillian Witz
retired. Mrs. Jensen retired in 1975.

The Library Board has had devoted citizens directing the progress for many
years. Mrs. Willard Brown, Mrs. Frank Archer and Mr. Ken Herrick had each
served about thirty years. The present board is: President, Mrs. Robert Gross;
Vice-president, Mrs. Roland Meyer; Secretary, Mrs. Elmer Lass; Mrs. Phil Clemen
and J.W. (Wes) Jeffery.

Dorothy Shonrock was hired as librarian on November 1, 1975, and fills that
position today. The library has grown from the humble start of 25 books to
15,500 books in 1982.

Ipswich Pioneer Village

The Ipswich Pioneer Village had its beginning in the office of County
Superintendent of Schools, Nellie Weiszhaar, when the reorganization of the
county schools pointed to the demise of the one room country school. Florence
Hughes, secretary in the office, and Blodwen Richards, secretary to the Edmunds
County Board of Education, discussed the "now or never" preservation of one of
the schools left in Edmunds County. This idea was brought to the Ipswich Flower
and Garden Club on August 26, 1468.

On November 25, 1968, County Judge, H.W. Hembd, agreed to contact the necessary
people in regard to the possible location and repairs on the Harmony School
which the County Commissioners had offered to donate to the project. Later it
was decided to look for another building as the Harmony School needed too many
repairs. Dorothy Peterson, Rose Wagner, Jan Heinz and H.W. Hembd looked at the
Powell School and possible sites. On April 28, 1969, President of the Flower
and Garden Club, Jan Heinz, reported that the Powell School Building together
with some of its contents had been purchased by Mr. and Mrs. Jack Hodson, Mr.
and Mrs. Dean Hodson and Lila Hodson Goheen and donated to the Club as the
