beginning of a Pioneer Village. On July 9, 1969, Matt Forkel offered to sell one
lot and give two lots for a site for the village. H.W. Hembd offered to pay for
one lot and this offer was accepted.

Soil Conservationist, Ed Ensz, made a map of the proposed village; H.W. Hembd
and Raymond Hieb did the legal work; Ed Bachman surveyed the lots; Howard Samp
donated foundation blocks; the land was plowed, leveled, disced and seeded by
Glen Jones, Gordon Duvall, Roy Jones, Jack Hodson and Edgar Lohnes; Jack Hodson
arranged for moving and the Forkels moved the schoolhouse to its new location
and on its foundation by the spring of 1970.

Other buildings have been added as they became available. The Owens building,
which houses a museum, was moved in August, 1973 and was renovated by Matt
Evans. An outdoor toilet was donated by Mr. and Mrs. Melvin J. Schauer and was
moved from North Dakota in 1973. The Schwall house was donated by Ipswich City
and was moved in 1973. The Congregational Church replica was donated and moved
to the site of a possible church in 1976. The Oscar Fredrickson family bought,
moved and partially furnished the former Loyalton Post Office in 1976.

In 1978 Matt Forkel donated a building to house the former Ipswich Printing
Press, an 1865 model, which was donated by Mr. and Mrs. Jim Morlock, present
publishers of the Ipswich Tribune.

All the buildings are furnished and contain many items of interest. Donations
have been made by so many people that it is impossible to name them.

Many hours of work have been given to this project by the members of the
Ipswich Flower and Garden Club, their families and friends. During the summer
they keep the Village open to visitors from two to four each Sunday afternoon
and special tours are given to anyone interested.

The Ipswich Pioneer Village belongs to the Ipswich Community and it is hoped
that future generations will continue to appreciate the heritage it represents.

                OUR FAITH

First Baptist Church

On January 20, 1884, several families of the Baptist faith met in McNamara Hall
and organized a church with the following charter members: Henry C. Briggs,
Marcus P. Beebe, Leota F. Beebe, Dwight C. Pierce, Mrs. D.C. Pierce, Ella M.
Pierce, Elmer M. Pierce, Albert S. Wipple, Sarah A. Smith, Francis Gamble, Don
I. Woodward, John E. Thomas, Mrs. H.W. Lascelle. Shortly after this a Sunday
School was started which has been continuously maintained since that time.

The first pastor, W.T. Williams was ordained by the church.

Services were conducted each Sunday in McNamara Hall until the Methodist
people, who in the meantime had built, kindly offered the use of their
building. As time passed, the building was sold to the Baptists and was used
for many years.

Membership increased and between 1906 and 1910, the building was repaired, a
Sunday School room added and a furnace installed. A large parsonage was
completed in 1912.

In 1922, Mrs. Leota Beebe, a charter member, offered to give two-thirds of the
cost of a new $25,000 church edifice. The offer was accepted and $11,000 was
pledged by the membership. Work was started in June 1924. Much of the labor was
volunteered by the men of the church. The church was dedicated on April 4,
1926.

Ministers and the years they served are as follows: W.T. Williams 1885; A.C.
Keen 1894-96; R.B. Wright 1896-99; A.G. Hislop 1900; A.V. Dahl 1902-03; E.V.
Pierce 1906-10; Clarke Tibbits 1910-12; L.L. Mann 1912-17; A. Pierce Waltz
1918-28; W.O. Roten 1928-34; O.H. Haligrimson 1934-39; R.V. Ritter 1939-43;
Robert Williams 1943-46; C.A. Nuss 1947-54; Z.A. Kleinsasser 1954-61; Robert
Starburg 1961 -66; L. Odegard 1966-68; Richard Sutterlin 1969-70; Fred Cowles
1971-75; Roger Tracy 1975-78; Glen Epp 1978-81. Rev. Freeland was pastor at
large for several months.

A new organ was purchased during the leadership of Rev. Kleinsasser. A hearing
aid system was installed in the church sanctuary with several "stations"
available to those with hearing problems, sanctuary was carpeted, improvements
made to Dailey Hall, floor tiled & paneling installed, a Sunday School room was
renovated and a new piano was given to the church in memory of Wesley Schwoerer
during the years Rev. Cowles was pastor. During the time Rev. Tracy was pastor,
he conducted musical concerts in the park, which were enjoyed by the community.


In 1976, the fiftieth anniversary of "The Stone Church Built on the Rock" was
observed. Several former pastors attended. In 1977, the church purchased the
Jensen property for use as a parking lot.

Rev. Craig Weber is the present pastor.

Good Shepherd Lutheran Church

In the spring of 1963, a small group of interested persons held a series of
meetings to discuss the beginning of a Missouri Synod Lutheran Congregation.
Rev. Alva Pingel, Pastor of Our Savior Lutheran Church of Aberdeen, South
Dakota, helped this small group make preliminary arrangements for the beginning
of Good Shepherd Lutheran Church, a Mission Congregation of the Lutheran Church
Missouri Synod.

The first worship service was held April 14, 1963, at the Legion Hall. Rev.
Richard Brauer, rural Aberdeen, served as pastor for four years.

As the congregation became organized, the following members were recorded as
charter members: Mr. and Mrs. Harold Bauer, Rebecca and Roxanne; Mr. and Mrs.
Edwin Buechler, Loren, Cheryl, Cynthia; Mr. and Mrs. James Hanemann and
daughters; Mr. and Mrs. Frank Hamak; Mr. and Mrs. Casper Helgerson, Camille,
Candice, Michael, Reed and Brad; Mr. and Mrs. Leonard Nelson and Stuart; Mrs.
Arland Ranson and Allen; Mr. and Mrs. Norman Taylor and Julie; Mr. and Mrs.
Lawrence Wolff, Kenneth, Debra and Wesley.

In January, 1964, the congregation purchased land on the west side of Ipswich
and in 1965, the South Dakota District of the Missouri Lutheran Synod informed
the congregation that plans were being made to build a new church for the
Mission Congregation. Ground breaking ceremonies were held October 19, 1965 and
the church was completed February, 1966. The first worship service was held
February 27, 1966 and dedication of the new edifice was held April 24, 1966.

Numerous pastors and vacancy pastors have served Good Shepherd Congregation in
the past years.

Two charter members have passed away, Frank Hamak, June 14, 1966, and Edwin
Buschler, February 10, 1971. Both men played an important part in organizing
Good Shepherd Lutheran Church.

Sunday School has been active, classes held every Sunday and Vacation Bible
School held every summer.

Since the church has been organized 34 persons have been baptized, 70 have been
confirmed, 10 marriages have been solemnized and nine persons have been granted
a Christian burial. Presently, the congregation numbers 115, 85 communicant
members and 30 child members.

Rev. Richard Kothe is the present pastor of Good Shepherd Lutheran Church.

Good Shepherd LWML Organization

The ladies of Good Shepherd Lutheran Church became organized on July 14, 1966,
as a Ladies Aid. On May 18, 1967, the group was received into membership in the
Lutheran Womens Missionary League of the South Dakota District of the Lutheran
Church Missouri Synod.

This relatively small group of ladies of the congregation is very active,
diligently lending a helping hand wherever needed, contributing to the
congregation, missions and various charities. They keep busy making up quilts
and layettes as a high priority for Lutheran World Relief.

The organization meets every fourth Thursday of the month. Meetings include
Bible study and a business meeting. In addition, district zone meetings,
rallies, workshops and conventions are attended by members who display much
interest in the work and welfare of the LWML Society.
                Mrs. Irene Buechler

Holy Cross Catholic Parish

        Holy Cross Catholic complex: Holy Cross School (bottom left), Father
        Bormann Hall (top left), Holy Cross Catholic Church

Among the early settlers to come to the Ipswich territory were people of the
Catholic faith. The first settlers came from lowa, Wisconsin, Nebraska and as
far away as New York. They were Irish, German, French and English. About the
year 1890, the Germans who had previously immigrated to Russia came to this
county and settled here. They were known as the German-Russians. Around the
turn of the century people came from Bohemia, Russia and a few from Minnesota.

To take care of the spiritual needs of these first Catholic settlers, priests
from Aberdeen would come, at first irregularly, but as the population grew,
more frequently. The best known and most popular of the first priests to attend
to the Ipswich Catholics was Father Haire. The first mass was offered in what
was known then as McNamara Hall. Mr. N.J. Engler, grandfather of Leonard
Engler, served that mass. This was January 9, 1884.

According to available records, the first resident pastor here was Rev. Bernard
J. Hens. It was during this time, Holy Cross Parish was organized as an
independent parish. The Articles of Incorporation of Holy Cross Catholic Church
were dated May 28, 1887, and a frame building was built for worship.

In the year 1906, the German Russians built a frame church for themselves,
which was located about twelve miles northeast of Ipswich. This "New Strasburg"
church served as a Mission Church to Holy Cross Parish until about the early
thirties. At that time, the final unification of the people into Holy Cross
Parish of Ipswich was completed and the New Strasburg church was abandoned.

In 1908, a new rectory was built and a new and larger church was begun where
the Father Bormann Hall now stands. Finances did not permit the completion of
the proposed church, so only the basement was built. This was during the
pastorale of Father J. O'Hara. In 1911, Father Frederic Bormann became pastor
of Holy Cross. He was pastor until his death, December 1, 1947. On January 6,
1948, Rev. Clarence W. Trautner became pastor of Holy Cross Parish.

In 1951, with $125,000.00 on hand, construction of the present church and
rectory was started. The property on which the new construction took place was
acquired from Mr. and Mrs. E.E. Hubbard. Construction was completed June 2,
1952. The first mass was celebrated in the new Holy Cross Church on June 15,
1952. The formal dedication of the church and consecration of the Main Altar
took place on July 22, 1952, with Bishop William O. Brady officiating. Total
cost of the new church and rectory, furnishings included was $250,000. The old
rectory was remodeled into a Sisters' Convent and in 1979, it was sold to Mr.
and Mrs. Jerome Geditz and moved six miles southwest of Ipswich.

In 1962, Rev. L. Donald Grace was appointed as pastor for Holy Cross Parish.

In 1963, construction of a new hall was started and completed in 1964. It
includes a spacious modern kitchen for the ladies of the community to use in
preparing the many large dinners served on numerous occasions. The large
gymnasium is in constant demand by the entire community for meetings, dinners,
sports activities and the physical education needs of Holy Cross students.

At present, Holy Cross Parish has 250 units with a population of approximately
1,100.

        Original Holy Cross Church

ALTAR SOCIETY

The Altar Society of Holy Cross Parish is composed of the ladies of the parish.
The purpose of the Altar Society is social and financial; social in that it
affords a chance for the women of the parish to meet and get to know each other
while exchanging ideas and financial in that they raise money to help alleviate
some of the financial burden involved in running a parish the size of Holy
Cross.

In the early years of the society, it was difficult to put on dinners or
bazaars, because the facilities were very inadequate. In the days of Rev.
Father Bormann, the ladies worked very hard during the summer months in order
to have a bazaar in the fall, usually October or November. At that time, the
Opera House and St. James Hotel were the only available places. This was
difficult as all the equipment had to be borrowed.

After the completion of the new church, Rev. Father Trautner and many faithful
and willing men remodeled the old basement into a hall, with a spacious dining
room having a seating capacity of about 250 guests. In 1961, the old hall was
torn down and in 1963 construction of a new hall was started.

In the first years, the society was divided into seven bands, each with its own
leader, but later it was thought best to have the ladies meet as one band.
Different committees now take the responsibility of the various functions with
the officers as chairpersons.

C.O.F. Holy Cross Court No. 1805

Holy Cross Court No. 1805 of the Catholic Order of Foresters was organized in
April, 1916. The Charter for the twenty members was issued by the High Court,
June 12, 1916 and on the 25th day of June, 1916, the members were initiated into
the Court.

The charter members were: George M. Engler, Carl R. Blank, Christopher Fhay,
Albert M. Engler, Earl E. Blank, Leo J. Paule, Frank R. Edington, Peter J.
Meier, August W. Schumacher, Joseph L. Clemen, Mathew W. Morgan, John C.
Clemen, Bartholomew Meier, Martin L. Blank, Edward L. Cogley, Joseph L. Morgan,
Jay H. Morgan, Paul Meier, John T. Blank and John J. Meier.

In 1948 there was a special reactivation movement which resulted in more
members and a more active program. At this time, 1982, there are 125 members in
the Catholic Order of Foresters.

The officers for 1982 are: Spiritual Director, Rev. L Donald Grace; Chief
Ranger, Allan F. Braun; Vice Chief Ranger, Daniel Lacher; Recording Secretary,
Lyle Steinhoff; Financial Secretary, Vern Steinhoff; Treasurer, Adam Heinz.
                Allan F. Braun

Holy Cross Study Clubs

There are five study clubs in the Holy Cross Catholic Church parish. St. Ann
study club was established in 1951; St. Monica in 1952; St. Gerard in 1954; Ava
Maria in 1955 and the St. Therese of the Little Flower in 1981.

The clubs meet once a month for the study and understanding of their religion
and prayer. They also perform different services such as serving First
Communion breakfast, each club goes to the Colonial Manor once a month to lead
the Rosary for the Catholic members there and each club leads the Rosary for a
week during the month of May. Individual clubs also have their own projects
such as the Quarter Club for the missions, packing mission boxes, making
baptismal garments, furnishing and putting up the Advent wreath and other
miscellaneous things.
                Mrs. Delphine Watembach

W.C.O.F.

The Women Foresters is still a very active organization in the community after
65 years, with an increase in membership.

The Court now meets every second Monday at Holy Cross Hall with Father L.
Donald Grace as our Court Chaplain.

The basic purposes of this Society is to promote friendship, unity and true
catholic charity among members and foster patriotism and loyalty to our
country. The 1982 officers are: Chief Ranger, LaVerne Heier; Vice Chief Ranger,
Eleanor Markovetz; Recording Secretary, Lorraine Scheele; Financial Secretary,
Irene Stevenson; Treasurer, Mary Gillick.
                LaVerne Heier

Jehovah's Witnesses Church

There have been Jehovah's Witnesses in Ipswich since 1907. At that time, Mr.
and Mrs. C.N. Ferry took upon themselves the responsibility of preaching
publicly and from house to house. Many Bible lectures by traveling missionaries
were held in the old Ipswich Court House and in the Opera House.

In May of 1938, the congregation was formally organized and regular meetings
were held in the residence of Dr. and Mrs. O.F. Mater. Presiding minister was
John Huft, Roscoe, South Dakota, followed by George Sindelar, who became
interested in the work of Jehovah's Witnesses in Rapid City, South Dakota in
1939.

After the death of Mrs. Mater in 1955, the Mater residence became the property
of the congregation and was designated as the "Kingdom Hall of Jehovah's
Witnesses ".

There was an increase in the congregation, so in 1968 a new Kingdom Hall was
built.
                Raymond Rohrbach

Living Word Assembly of God

The Living Word Assembly of God began in November of 1980 as an independent
church. In June of 1982, however, they became a part of the fellowship of the
Assembly of God denomination.

The church building was purchased from the Zion Lutheran Church in December,
1980. It seats approximately 100 people. Pews were purchased in Bowdle.

Rev. Richard Apfel has been pastoring since the beginning of the church.
Richard, his wife, Marilyn and children, Jamie and Lisa, are presently residing
in Ipswich.

        Church of Living Word. Old Zion Lutheran Church building

Powell Presbyterian Church

The first settlers arrived in Powell, March 22, 1883, and put up the noted Big
Shanty, a 20 x 40 frame building, on a claim occupied by a leading member of
the Colony. This served as a hotel, church and meeting house.

More immigrants came in the spring of 1884, and built a large school house
which was used as a place of worship until the church was built a little later.
The church was built in 1886, and a Calvinistic Methodist Church was organized.
The Rev. John W. Morgan was the first pastor.

After the building of the Methodist Church, it became apparent that the Welsh
were not of a single mind and that the services would not serve the whole
community well. At that time a Congregational Church was organized and built.
Due to population shifts, the building was sold in 1902 and moved to Ipswich,
where it became a part of the Congregational Church and parsonage.

On June 2, 1911, the Methodist Church was completely destroyed by a tornado. A
new church was built and dedicated on Thanksgiving Day of that same year.

It was during the 1923-33 decade that the disbanding of the National
Calvinistic Methodist Welsh Church and the assimilation of the congregations
into the Presbytery of the Presbyterian Church, U.S.A. took place and with it
the last full time minister and the yoking with Roscoe and Java fields.

The church reached its modern era and the inevitable happened when a minister
was secured who could not preach the Welsh language. The Rev. J. Rhys Evans was
the last Welsh minister to serve the church.

In the period between 1941-45, the church became a member of the Aberdeen
Presbytery.

On October 11, 1953, the seventieth anniversary of religious service to the
Powell community was observed. On June 8, 1958, the church celebrated its
seventy-fifth anniversary of religious service to the Powell community and a
memorial to the "Pioneers of Powell".

On August 13, 1963, members of the Powell Church voted to share pastors with
the United Church of Christ of Ipswich. Rev. Douglas Ostlund served both
pulpits from September 1963 until September 1965.

The Powell Church became a member of the Presbytery of South Dakota in 1963.

In January 1966, Rev. Donald Stoebner served the Ipswich-Powell Parish. In the
fall of 1966, the Memorial Committee had a dedication service for a Pioneer
Memorial Plaque. Later, the Memorial Committee had a church sign made and
placed in the church yard.

Roscoe became a member of the parish in November 1969, but withdrew in 1977.

For the past several years, the Powell church members have joined with the
Ipswich United Church of Christ for services in Ipswich during the winter
months.

The thirty-eight present members and several friends of the church are getting
ready to celebrate their Centennial in June of 1983.

United Presbyterian Women's Organization

Times were hard in the 1930's, so the women of the Powell Church and community
decided to organize a ladies aid to help with the finances of the church and do
some sewing for missionary work. Their big donation was the food and fancy work
for the Fall Bazaar and supper. They also sponsored ice cream socials, served
at Republican and Democratic Rallies, sold cards, vanilla and lunches at farm
sales. Besides their yearly pledge to the church, the aid sometimes donated
money to the trustees to pay for coal or paint, etc. The big project of the
organization in the early 1940's was to buy the supplies so the men could put a
full-sized basement under the church.

The present day meetings are devoted mainly to Bible study or missionary
reports.

The bazaars and dinners have been discontinued, but members pay dues, give
"Love Gifts", have special offerings for missionary and medical projects, serve
lunches for funerals and farm sales and for the past several years have had bake
sales.
                Mrs. Ruth Richards

The Wesleyan Church

During the summer of 1931, Rev. W.C. Brannon, pastor of the Aberdeen Wesleyan
Methodist Church, came to Ipswich on Sunday afternoons to hold services in the
old schoolhouse. As meetings continued, interest and attendance increased. On
November 15, 1931, under the leadership of Rev. J.C. McGovern, the church was
organized.

The following twelve persons were charter members: Mr. and Mrs. Henry Albee;
Mrs. W.H. Tullis; Mr. and Mrs. Charles Linden; Mr. and Mrs. H.B. Engel; Wilbert
R. Engel; Lester Engel; Don Engel; Mrs. William Wagner and Mrs. Ray Mackey.

The first pastor was Rev. Ivan Calhoun, who came to take charge of the work
soon after its organization. He resigned in September, 1934. Preceding the
appointment of Rev. L.D. Harris in June, 1935, a movement was started to
procure a place of permanent worship Funds were raised and the lot where the
present church stands was purchased in 1934.

During the fall of 1935, work was started on the basement of the church, which
was ready for worship service on Christmas Day, 1935. Three years later, the
first service was held in the auditorium on Christmas Eve. The church was
dedicated on April 30, 1939.

The Ipswich Wesleyan Methodist Church completed a major remodeling and
enlarging program in 1969. Rev. R.J. Sausoman was pastor at this time.

The Wesleyan Methodist Church became the Wesleyan Church following a merger of
the Wesleyan Methodist and the Pilgrim Holiness Churches in 1971. The Women's
Mission Society carries the interest and support for foreign and home mission
projects and missionaries. The Wesleyan men undertake the development and
support of pioneer churches in growing communities, where there is a need for
evangelical ministry.

The Wesleyan youth ministers to the needs of the church youth. The Young
Missionary Workers Board, under the direction of Miss Marie Brannon, teaches
the children the importance of sharing in the missionary effort by contributing
funds and getting new members who are interested in mission work.

The following ministers served as pastors for several years each: Rev. W. J.
Leyh 1943-45; Rev. Dallas Wadsworth 1946-50; Rev. Robert Bateman 1950-54; Rev.
Daniel McCandless 1954-56; Rev. Richard Franklin 1957-60; Rev. and Mrs. W.J.
Leyh 1960-66; Rev. and Mrs. R.J. Sausoman 1966-81. The Rev. Rod Griffin is the
current pastor.
                Walter Peterson

United Church of Christ

                (Congregational Church of Ipswich)

The humble start of a Sunday School in 1883, with participants sitting on store
boxes and sawhorses in the Marshall store in Freeport, was the beginning of the
Congregation Church in Ipswich. The Rev. S. A. Belt, a Congregational minister,
brother of Mrs. H.T. Dailey and Hattie Fitch Rogers, and Mr. and Mrs. J.H.
Marshall were the organizers.

Later a union Protestant church was organized composed of Methodists, Baptists,
Presbyterians, Episcopalians and Congregationalists. It was necessary to have a
name to be eligible for aid from any Home Missionary Building Society. As the
acting minister was Congregational, the majority voted for that title. Baptists
and Methodists joined hands and started a second church.

The Congregational Church of Ipswich was incorporated on September 27, 1884.
Construction of the first building on lot 3, block 1, at the corner of Fourth
Street and Second Avenue, measuring 42 x 30, began that fall. The first
services were held January 4, 1885, led by Rev. J. Norton. Prior to being in
their own building, they held services in McNamara's hall which they rented for
$5.00 per Sunday. Rev. Norton stayed only six weeks and was followed by Rev. G.
W. Honey and James H. Kyle (1886-1889). Rev. Kyle later became actively involved
in politics, but was thought to be the Father of Congregationalism in this
area.

Ministers came and went, many not staying more than a year until Rev. E.B.
TreFethren came in 1899. He stayed until 1905 and built the first parsonage
during this time. These ministers followed him: Reverends D.L. Jones, Harry
Evans, Ralph E. Bayes (supply), C.H. Van Anken, Godfrey Matthews, Harry Evans,
George E. Green, B.G. Gallagher, Hugh T. Owen, E.C. Salter, M.C. Haecker, David
Graham, Lester E. Smith, George E. Hartz, John A. Northrop, Fred Sprenger, Doug
Ostlund, Don Stoebner, Del Gault, George Backer (interim), Douglas Barrows
(supply) and Jean E. Rosewall.

At the turn of the century, Rev. Treiber organized the Christian Endeavor for
the young people. An adult Welch class was started with John Owens (father of
Gladys Lee Williams) as the teacher.

During the pastorale of Rev. Godfrey Matthews from 1913 until 1918, the
beautiful stained glass windows depicting the life of Christ were installed.
The present location and description follows:

Angel to Shepherds, at the back of the church, given by Mr. and Mrs. George
Meadows (parents of Mrs. Beth Crissman) in memory of their son Earl, who was
drowned shortly after his graduation from high school.

Head of Christ, round window above the altar with the inscription "The Word is
a Lamp unto My Feet", given by Dr. and Mrs. Robert Hill.

Jesus the Shepherd, the northeast corner window with the inscription "Dedicated
to the Glory of God by the Junior Endeavorers". Emily Roberts and Elizabeth
Roberts Jensen recall starting with one dollar and increasing its value with
their talents. The idea probably being taken from the Parable of the Five
Talents.

Jesus Blesses the Little Children, southwest window given as a memorial for the
Rev. Harry Evans who died during the flu epidemic. Given by Mr. and Mrs. H.E.
Beebe, members of the Baptist Church.

The Resurrection, the northwest window given by Mr. and Mrs. Ed Barron in
memory of Mrs. Barron's mother, Mrs. J.C. Simmons of Aberdeen. Mr. Barron's
mother (Mrs. C.H.) was church organist for many years.

The Ascension, southeast window has a plaque engraved "Barron". We are unable
to confirm how this window was purchased.

Rev. Godfrey Matthews and his sister, Grace, were music lovers and Mrs. Ruth
Hill Nichols gives him credit for her interest in music. The two of them were
responsible for securing donations to purchase the first organ and Rev.
Matthews organized the first all girl choir.

As music plays such an important part in our worship services, it needs to be
recorded that Muriel Brown (Mrs. W.A.) was organist for 25 years and Marie
Bebermeyer (Mrs. R.W.) was organist and choir director for 28 years. These two
dedicated people served for 53 years of the life of our church. Carol Engel
Gunderson, Stacy Petersen Braun and Corinne Jameson have continued their
heritage and Ricky Benton adds her talent as pianist. Many talented and faithful
vocalists have given much of their time as choir members and soloists.

Stephen Williams, son of John D. and Emma Williams, was ordained as a
Congregational minister in 1935. He was the only son of the congregation to
enter the Christian Ministry through ordination and was pastor at the First
Congregational Church in Emporia, Kansas for 32 years. He died in 1981, at the
age of 73 years.

On December 12, 1943, the church observed its sixtieth anniversary.
Superintendent Rev. S.W. Keck of Huron was present and other special guests
were Mrs. Alice Caborn, Mr. and Mrs. George Jaquith, Mrs. John (Elizabeth)
Owens and Mr. and Mrs. George Meadows. They had been Ipswich residents for 60
years.

The basement and west addition of the church were built in the early thirties.
In January, 1948, extensive remodeling of the sanctuary was started and the
first service after its completion was held on October 29, 1950. Improvements
have continued through the years.

The seventy-fifth year was celebrated in services held on August 3, 1958, with
some 250 friends and members in attendance. The new pews were dedicated at this
time. The present parsonage was built in 1960 and the garage added later. A
dedication service for the new United Church of Christ hymnals was held on May
25, 1975.

The Congregational Church of Ipswich became part of a new denomination, the
United Church of Christ, in 1961. This denomination is composed of former
Congregational-Christian churches and Evangelical and Reformed churches.

Since 1963, the Ipswich United Church of Christ (Congregational) has been part
of a larger parish. At various times in the past, this has included the Powell
United Presbyterian, Roscoe Congregational and Roscoe Presbyterian Churches.
Currently, the Parish of Uniting Churches is composed of the Ipswich United
Church of Christ and the Powell United Presbyterian Church.

Many beautiful gifts have been given to our church as memorials to loved ones
as well as gifts of money enabling the church to purchase various items.

We have had devoted members through the years that have given unselfishly of
themselves to enable the work of the church to continue.

Present officers are: Secretary, Margaret Nelson; Financial Secretary, Lila
Johnson; Treasurer, Ruby Bosanko. Head usher is Ben Hoyle and Church School
Superintendent is Janice Kilber. Deacons are Jan Braband, Robert Pond, Dennis
Benton and Paul McGinn. Deaconesses are Shirley Williams, Ruth Lewis and Sally
Biel. Trustees are Charles McGinn, Loren Leitzke, Dave Nelson and Eleanore
Krokosh.

In December, 1983, our church will be one hundred years of age. Our Centennial
celebration is planned for August 28, 1983.

        Congregational Church in 1949-50 before remodeling. Pictured are Henry
        Beck and J.D Williams

        Congregational church interior 1949-50 before remodeling.

Women's Fellowship

Our Women's Fellowship meeting consists of devotions, a business meeting with
reports from the various standing committees, an educational program and of
course, lunch. There have been an average of 24 members in attendance.

Our programs consist of a variety of subjects. We have had guest speakers
telling us about Sweden, Ceylon, Honduras, Nigeria, Russia, Brazil, the Holy
Land, Jewish traditions, the Indians, mental retardation and the Redfield State
Hospital. A special Christmas guest night of music and worship began in 1965,
and is still a favorite night for many. We participate in the World Day of
Prayer services with other churches.

Throughout the years we have given to ten different missions which now is
dispersed through our giving to OCWM (Our Christian World Mission). We also
have given special donations to Yankton College and Placerville, our church
school and camp. We participated in the stamp saving project to purchase a bus
for Placerville in 1967. We pay a portion of our campers fees when they go to
camp. In 1980, we purchased a stereo for our local nursing home. We pledge to
the church and offer financial assistance whenever needed. In 1964, we began
providing coffee after church during the winter months. We give gifts to our
Church high school graduates and in 1980, began giving Bibles to the third
graders for their use and study in church school. We provide lunches after
funerals, help serve wedding receptions, anniversaries, auction sales, and
dinners to various groups whenever asked.

In 1971, our fellowship dropped one circle. We now have two circles, and
afternoon study circle and an evening circle concerned mainly of working with
labels, which became a very worthwhile moneymaking project piloted by Irene
Bosanko and still under her hardworking and dedicated direction.

Even though it was suggested by the State Association in 1959 that each member
of our church be an automatic member of our Women's Fellowship, our
organization did not make that change until 1981. Now, each and every woman of
our church is considered a member of Women's Fellowship.

Present officers are: President, Ila Davis; Vice-president, Helen Gross;
Secretary, Mary Jane Opsahl; Treasurer, Alice Jones.

Zion Evangelical Lutheran Church

The first Lutherans that moved into this area made requests for the services of
a Lutheran pastor. Their requests were answered by pastors coming from Leola
and Hosmer. In 1921, the first resident pastor, Rev. Walter J. Meier arrived.
Since its organization, it received continual services of pastors from the
Wisconsin Synod of which it was a member.

In 1927, Zion formed one parish with the congregation of Loyalton. Since that
time it has been united with the First Lutheran Church of Faulkton.

The church felt a deep need for a building to call its own, but not until 1931
could a used church be purchased and moved to its present location.

On January 17, 1963, the congregation voted to join the (C.L.C.) Church of
Lutheran Confession Synod of which it has been a member since.

The following pastors have served the congregation from the beginning: Rev.
W.J. Meier, Rev. E. Schaller, Rev. R. Gamm, Rev. W. Schmidt, Rev. H. Birner,
Rev. M. Eibs and Rev. Leland Grams since April 1954.

On October 1, 1974, a lot was purchased from Raymond Forkel, four blocks north
of the old church.

The old church was sold to the Living Word Assembly of God. On June 1978,
ground breaking was held and in July 1978, a building permit was received and
the building of the church started with all volunteered help. The first service
was held December 21, 1980.

        Zion Lutheran Church completed 1980

        Interior of Zion Lutheran Church

Ladies Aid of Zion Evangelical Lutheran Church

Not long after the Zion Ev. Lutheran Church was organized, a group of women of
the congregation formed a Ladies Aid Society.

This organization meets once a month for Bible study and concerns itself with
various works of the church. All its enterprises are supported and carried out
by its members and their free will offerings.

All ladies of the congregation are welcome and urged to participate in the
functions of the Ladies Aid.
                Mrs. Lillian Holsing

        Front row, left to right: Mrs. Steven Grams, Lillian Holsing, Martha
        Entzel, Mrs. John Wolff, Irene Holm, Verna Ochsner, Mrs. Darwin Diede,
        Back row: Mrs. Herb Wockenfuss, Gladys Rohrbach, Bernice Wietgrefe,
        Norma Schneider, Betty Habben, Marveline Schumacher, Irene Rissmann,
        Rev. Grams.

                EDUCATION

Ipswich Public School

        Ipswich Public School built 1886.

It is believed that the first term of school began in the fall of 1883 before
the town of Ipswich was platted. The teacher was a man from New York, and he
received his first license from J.W. Parmley, who was the first county
superintendent. The first schools were held in the upstairs in downtown
business places. The first kindergarten was held in the Nickle Plate Hotel (St.
James) in the school year of 1897-98.

The town of Ipswich was platted in 1884. Three schools were started at this
time, and were in use for two years.

The first brick school was built in 1886. A wide expanse of prairie on the west
and north of the building was the playground for many years before the town
began to move northward and westward.

As the population of Ipswich grew, the school became crowded. People began to
also feel the need for some higher education for the youth. An addition was
added to the east side of the first building. The first six grades were held
downstairs, and the seventh and eighth grades and a small high school upstairs.
The high school curriculum included geometry, algebra, Latin, English, physics,
American history, English history, bookkeeping and physical geography.

Four students graduated from the first four year course in 1907. Fifteen
students completed four years in 1920. The largest class to graduate from
Ipswich High consisted of sixty members in the school year of 1970-71.

In 1920-21, construction began again. A very fine building was erected south of
the old brick school. This new structure housed the first eight grades, a four
year high school, a gymnasium, an assembly, a lunchroom, a superintendent's
office and a lounge. In September 1934, the night before the school was to
start, fire destroyed much of this building. Classes were held in churches, the
Masonic Lodge building and the library most of that school year while the
damage was being repaired.

        Ipswich Public School 1908. Left side built, 1886; right side 1908.

The old building was used for storage, and for a brief time in the thirties it
was used as a dormitory and lunchroom. Eventually, the original structure was
torn down and in the late 60's, the east addition was also removed.

In the latter part of the 40's and the early 50's, the elementary population
began growing quite rapidly. The community again rallied behind the Board of
Education, and $90,000 of bonds were issued. A $100,000 structure was joined to
the main building to the south. It contained three grade rooms, a science room,
a Home Economics department, and a large music room with smaller practice rooms
to the side. It also contained restroom facilities, storage space and a furnace
room. This addition was completed in 1957.

There is no record of kindergarten being held on a regular basis, until 1959.
In March of that year, thirty-four youngsters enrolled for a six weeks period.
It was necessary for the third and fourth grades to move from the southeast
room in the new addition to the one time lunch room in the main building to
make room for the kindergarten class. For a number of years the class was moved
about from one place to another. The old lunch room, the basement of the United
Church of Christ, and finally, two of the houses west of the schoolhouse that
had been purchased by the district were used in different years. This situation
continued until the completion of the new elementary pod in 1971. The
kindergarten class then had its own room and could be held on a nine month
schedule.

The hot lunch program was initiated in November of 1960. The southwest room of
the main building was used as the dining area and the small adjoining room on
the east was used as the kitchen. Tickets were sold at twenty-five cents per
meal as compared to 65 cents for kindergarten through third grade and 90 cents
per day for fourth grade through high school in 1982.

Though the facilities were crowded, the program was successful and in 1971 a
new kitchen and dining area were made available.

Parent-teacher conferences were introduced into the school system in 1962. They
were met with great enthusiasm, It was found that the conferences aided both
the parent and the teacher in understanding the functions of the school. It
served to bring the school and the community into unity, and into a common
effort to educate the children and young people.

By the fall of 1969, elementary attendance had more than doubled. The heavy
responsibilities of the teachers were lightened when teacher aides were brought
into the system. At this time, cadet teaching was also initiated as an extra-
curricular activity in the high school. Interested students proved to be a real
help in the over-crowded elementary classrooms.

Reorganization of the school district, and the transfer of seventh and eighth
grade students from Holy Cross to the Public School were major factors in the
large growth of the elementary and junior high population in 1969.
Reorganization was not accomplished in a single year, but by this time, nine
buses brought students in from the following townships: Ipswich, part of
Adrian, Rosette, Belle, Pembrook, part of Huntley, Fountain, most of Cortlandt,
Union, Harmony, nearly all of Richland, part of Cleveland, Liberty, Powell,
part of Kent, part of Clear Lake, and some from the Loyalton School District.

The extremely crowded conditions in the school were alleviated by the use of
temporary units. Two of these buildings were located north of the main building
and one unit adjoined on the west side of the school. A house and a double
mobile home located just west were also used.

School board members and concerned citizens deemed it necessary that another
addition be put onto the existing facilities.

        Ipswich High School today

        Ipswich Elementary School and Auditorium 1983

Voters favored a school bond issue by a 68.1 percent vote in May of 1969.

The blueprint called for an eight-sided elementary building. a new gym and
stage with an ad joining lobby to be also used as the lunch area, locker rooms
for girls and for boys, equipment rooms and a kitchen area.

Sixty graduates received their diplomas in the beautiful new gym, May 27, 1971.


Kindergarten through grades six moved into the new building in the fall of
1971.

In September of 1971, the Ipswich Public School and the city of Ipswich entered
into a joint venture to develop the new school playground. Federal funds helped
to black-top the area, and to provide outdoor recreation areas which included
tennis courts, outdoor basketball area, horseshoe pitching space, swings and
other playground equipment. It was a happy day for the teachers and the
students when they no longer had to walk to the temporary playground on the
land surrounding the old shop building.

Some changes were also made in the main building. The old gym bleachers were
torn out and the space was used to make two large rooms on the first floor
level. Parking space had been very inadequate for many years, so the housing
area east of the school plant was purchased. Several houses were moved out or
torn down, and a spacious parking lot was made available.

A large quonset building was moved onto land located two blocks west of the
playground, in the fall of 1957. The agriculture and shop classes were held
there. Agriculture was dropped from the curriculum a few years later, but the
shop classes continued.

For many reasons this building was very unsatisfactory. The Board of Education
let bids for construction of a new two story shop building in June of 1976.
This structure was joined to the north side of the main building and covered
the site of the very first brick school. Construction began June 28, 1976, and
came to completion in February of 1977. Students gladly helped move equipment
from the old quonset and classes in the new addition were soon in progress.
Some of the courses taught were: drafting, woods, motor mechanics, power
mechanics and metals. These classes will again be given in the school year of
1982-83.

The new structure has two offices, two classrooms, a room for wood finishing,
and another room for storage all on the first floor. The second story is an
open area where wrestling is held. If it becomes necessary at a later date,
this space can be partitioned into two rooms for band and chorus.

This latest addition houses one of the finest and best equipped high school
shops in the state. Both boys and girls learn many practical skills that can be
used in later life. They turn out many fine products during the school term.

In recent years the library was moved from a classroom to the old study hall.
The old desks were removed and replaced with modern equipment. Carpeting cut
down on the inevitable noise caused by shuffling feet on a wooden floor. These
improvements made the library a pleasant, attractive place where students can
read, do research and check out books.

The English department recently initiated a new, innovative program open to
juniors and seniors. About sixteen nine week courses are offered in writing,
grammar, speech poetry, drama, short stories, novels and other subjects.

This department has also sent their one-act plays to state competition a number
of times.

        Ipswich Public School complex - elementary, auditorium, junior and high
        school.

Records show that Latin was taught from 1903-04 to 1929-30. At that time it was
dropped from the curriculum. A foreign language was not taught until 1967-68.
In order that the school might be accredited by North Central Association, this
became a requirement. German was taught one year. The next year, French became
part of the curriculum, and continued until 1971-72. It was replaced by
Spanish. The availability of a teacher was the deciding factor as to which
language was taught.

It is of interest to note that an orchestra was formed in 1930-31. With only
two year's experience, it received a superior rating in the state contest in
Vermillion. Several more superiors were won through the next five or six years
before orchestra was dropped.

Band became an extra-curricular activity in 1932-33. The Ipswich High School
band was entitled to participate in the National Music Contest at St. Paul on
May 16, 1941.

The records are incomplete for the next twenty years, but since the fall of
1964, the instrumental program has steadily grown under the supervision and
instruction of the present teacher. This factor has led to the continuity and
success of the program to this time. The cadet band of fifth and sixth grade
students and the junior high band program have led to consistent success in the
high school instrumental program. Contest results for all parts of the program
have been excellent. At the present time there is a 50 percent participation.
This should insure continued progress.

The first record of vocal music was that of a girls' glee club in 1928-29. It
rated a first in the spring of 1929 at the district competition for Class B.
Since that time, there have been boys' chorus, girls' chorus, mixed chorus and
various ensemble groups. The vocal department has also received many excellent
ratings at contests.

Many other changes and additions have been made to the high school curriculum
since the early days. Vocational business has made an on the job training
course available to seniors the last nine weeks of the school year. The
computer is the newest course to be offered, and has gained much popularity
among the students. At the present, it has had to be limited to juniors and
seniors to keep the classes from being overcrowded. Much more could be said
about the fine high school program, but space does not allow for all of it.

The seventh and eighth grades became departmentalized in 1969-70. Math,
language arts, science, social studies and physical education were the required
courses with typing, home economics and band made available.

Grades one through six were divided from two classes per room with one teacher
to the one classroom with a teacher for each grade in the early 60's. Each
teacher taught their own music, art and physical education for several years
until teachers specialized in those fields were hired. A full time elementary
principal also became a part of the system during this period.

The elementary teachers, with the help of their aides, have attempted to meet
the needs of the individual, rather than expecting all children of the same age
to fit into the same mold. This has been to the advantage of each student.

The elementary system has been fortunate in having dedicated, talented teachers
down through the years to the present, who have had the best interests of their
pupils at heart.

As we look back over the past one hundred years, we can view with pride the
steady progress that has been made in our school program. We can be proud of
the many fine graduates who have left Ipswich High and are out doing their part
in our world of today.
                Ruth Gibson

Holy Cross Catholic School

        Holy Cross Catholic School

Holy Cross School stands as a tribute to Father Fredrick Bormann. Father
Bormann dreamed of a religious education for the children of his parish. After
many years of hard work by Father Bormann and many members of the parish, the
school opened its doors in 1915.

The first classes were held in the basement which also served as the church.
Father Bormann continued his struggle to erect his school and his dream was
realized in 1921 when the present school opened its doors.

The school was first staffed by nuns from Louisiana and North Dakota; from 1934
until 1978 by the Benedictine nuns of South Dakota.

Many children have passed through the doors of Holy Cross and because of the
foresight of Father Bormann, some of the children were housed at the school.
These facilities were available until 1953 when the Sisters moved into the old
rectory. In 1969 the school closed its doors to the seventh and eighth grade
classes because of increasing enrollment and expenses.

The school is still funded almost entirely by the parish and at the present has
an enrollment of 74 students in grades one through six with 45 families
represented. The staff consists of: Mary Jo Stump, principal; Dianna Anderson,
Kathy DeGroot, Lisa Hammrich, Virginia Dehnert and Connie Wells as teachers.
Deloris Thares is the secretary. The school has its own hot lunch program with
two dedicated cooks, Margaret Hauck and Delores Schumacher, to provide the
delicious meals for the students.

Holy Cross School is in existence because of the dreams and hard work of Father
Bormann and the parish, and continues because parents want their children to
have the education which was provided by the dreams and hard work of these
people. With God's continued help and the support of the parish, Holy Cross
will remain the school that offers the four "R's".

                HEALTH CARE

Medical and Dental Services

In the early history of Ipswich, we find the names of several medical doctors.
Dr. W.E. Warner, an experienced physician came here from Freeport in 1883 as
did Dr. Seabright about the same time. From old newspaper clippings, there was
this: Dr. J.D. Jones left for his home in southern Minnesota in 1899 and Dr.
E.E. Barrett was in Ipswich in June of 1890. Found, too, are the names of Dr.
Diertz in 1903-04, Dr. Henry Foshager about 1912-1913 and Dr. Wickham in 1926.

Dr. Robert Hill was married to Bird R. Roe on September 18, 1895 in Leola and
immediately came to Ipswich to serve the community faithfully as long as health
permitted. He died in 1938. Their first home was where Mrs. Alma Heinrich now
lives. The writer was told Dr. Hill spent his wedding night delivering Chester
Doolittle. In 1902, the first X-ray machine in Ipswich was placed in his office
which was over Dr. Jim Engler's present office site. Dr. Kearns from Leola
often assisted Dr. Hill.

Dr. Walter E. White, an Edmunds County youth, returned as a physician and
surgeon about 1916. His wife, Pluma, was from Illinois and a registered nurse.
They served the community faithfully for many years.

Dr. G.J. Bloemendaal and his wife, the former Gladys Williams, a registered
nurse, came to Ipswich in 1928. Their devotion and service to Ipswich and the
surrounding area will be remembered the best by the majority of the readers of
this book. Gladys worked side by side with her husband, and when Dr.
Bloemendaal was called to serve in this country and Europe during World War II,
she provided valuable medical assistance to many.

In 1963, one-thousand people attended a program to honor Dr. Bloemendaal for
thirty-five years of dedicated service to the community. He received additional
recognition in 1972 when the city designated the street adjacent to the
hospital as Bloemendaal Drive.

From the beginning of Ipswich until early 1947 when the hospital was opened,
medical services in this community were provided in the doctor's office and
through their calls in the homes. Babies were born at home and when the time
came the neighbor lady was called and hoped the doctor arrived in time for
delivery. A few mid wives resided in the area.

Dr. G.J. Bloemendaal served as an active medical staff member of the Ipswich
Community Hospital until 1976. Other physicians holding active staff privileges
during the history of the hospital include Dr. Walter Kitzler 1966-68, Dr.
Eugene Gibbs 1971-73, Dr. Henry Shaw 1971-74, Dr. John L. McFee, Jr.
1974-present and Dr. Edward D'Sousa 1975-present. Several other physicians and
dentists have been or are now included as honorary, courtesy or consulting
medical staff members.

In 1974, the Ipswich Community Hospital was faced with the possibility of
having to close its doors because a resident doctor was not available.

        Dr. G. J. Bloemendaal and his wife Gladys on their 45th wedding
        anniversary, September 8, 1970.

Dr. John L. McFee, Jr. came to Ipswich at a time when he was desperately needed
and immediately plunged into the "always on call" routine of a small town
doctor. His faithful service to his patients includes a large area surrounding
Ipswich. Along with the duties of his office and the hospital, he goes to Leola
one day a week to see patients in the office there. His dedicated service to
the people he serves is greatly appreciated, and we arc fortunate to have a
doctor of his caliber in our community.

        Dr. John L. McFee, Jr., Ipswich community doctor, and patient Margaret
        Verselius.

Dr. McFee's family consists of his wife, Susan, a registered nurse currently
serving on the school board, and four children, John, Nancy, Kate and Phillip.

Dr. Mater, a chiropractor, came in 1907 and practiced until 1933. He left
Ipswich to reside in Alabama, but returned to his practice here and remained
until his death. His office was in his home located where the present Kingdom
Hall of the Jehovah's Witnesses stands.

Advertising in early papers indicate Dr. Bigger, a dentist, practiced here. Dr.
Albert Auchampach, born to pioneer parents in 1882, became a dentist and
returned to Ipswich to practice dentistry until near the time of his death in
1961. Dr. Otto Schnaidt from Leola opened his practice in the office of Dr.
Auchampach in 1959. He came three days a week until 1965. Dr. James Engler,
DDS, opened his office just south of the Crissman Drug Store in July, 1978. He
is the son of Mr. and Mrs. Leonard Engler.

Dr. Engler is a fourth generation Engler that has served the people of the
Ipswich community and information we have indicates this is the only family
that has this distinction. This includes his great-grandfather, Nick Engler;
grandfather, Bert Engler and father, Leonard Engler.

        Dr. James Engler, DDS, Ipswich dentist and fourth generation Engler
        serving the Ipswich Community, dental assistant Brenda Kolb (1) and
        Sharon Engler also work in the dental office.

Ipswich Community Hospital

The Ipswich Community Hospital was formally incorporated as a nonprofit
community association on November 22, 1946. Serving on the original eleven
person Board of Directors were J.F. Holdhusen, Carl F. Wall, H. S. Crissman,
W.K. Herrick, W.M. Hodson, Alfred Petersen, E. L. Friedrichsen, Lester Davis,
Joseph D. Lacher, Martin Schurr and J.W. Jeffery. When the hospital began
operations in early 1947, it was staffed by one physician, Dr. G. J.
Bloemendaal, one registered nurse, Gladys Bloemendaal, and two nurses' aides.
The hospital was housed in the Beebe mansion, now the Hein Funeral Home.

Plans for remodeling or replacing the hospital developed and were discussed
from the late 1950's on. The new hospital was constructed on a new site in
1964-65 at a cost of approximately $362,000. Over $181,000 came from a grant
under the auspices of the Hospital Survey and Construction Act of 1946,
otherwise known as "Hill Burton". Approximately $115,000 was donated from
various community members and organizations and the balance was taken from the
hospital's capital reserve. The new 19 bed hospital was dedicated on August 29,
1965.

        Ipswich Community Hospital in 1946.

In 1978, the Ipswich Hospital Board entered into a three party management
services agreement with Dakota Midland Hospital in Aberdeen, South Dakota and
Health Central, Inc., a nonprofit healthcare and management services
organization headquartered in suburban Minneapolis, Minn. Under the terms of
this agreement which was subsidized during its first three years by a grant
from the W.K. Kellogg Foundation, the hospital board buys administrative and
various management support services from Dakota Midland Hospital and Health
Central. The local hospital board still retains its same full range of
authority, responsibility and control over the hospital and its operations. The
agreement has been renewed every year since its inception.

In late 1980, substantive work began on the planning stages for an expansion
project. The project at a cost of $639,000, scheduled for completion in the
spring of 1983, will increase the licensed bed capacity of the hospital to 21
beds, increase its storage and work space, and correct several operational
deficiencies that have developed over the years. The project was funded by a
loan from the Farmers' Home Administration and by funds out of the hospital's
capital reserve.

Today, the hospital has a staff of 43 full and part-time employees. It is a
member of the American Hospital Association, South Dakota Hospital Association,
a member Hospital in Blue Cross and fully approved for licensure and
participation in the Medicare Program. Its 15 member Board of Directors include
Peter Geffre, president; Tom Holdhusen, vice-president; R.G. Gross,
secretary-treasurer; Mark Beyers, Earle Crissman, James Davis, Virginia
Dehnert, Matt Forkel, Gleva Kitchell, Daniel Lacher, Paul McGinn, Harvey Olson,
Reuben Pfaff, Don Wagner and Everett Williams.

The hospital serves over 43 percent of the inpatient hospitalization needs of
the residents of Edmunds County, over 15 percent of these needs for McPherson
County residents and approximately five percent of all Faulk County residents.
It has averaged an 80 percent plus occupancy rate for the past five years. Its
dedicated staff of physicians, dentists and employees, along with its concerned
Board of Directors, will help to ensure the continued level of high quality
health care that is available to residents of this area.

        Kenneth, Carol, and Keith (pictured above) were the first set of
        triplets born in Ipswich Community Hospital. They were born to Earl and
        Lila Johnson September 17, 1952.

In 1976, the hospital Board of Directors authorized construction of a clinic
building north of the hospital which is leased at the present time by Dr. John
L. McFee, Jr.

        For the week ending March 1, 1968, six babies were born in the
        hospital, including a set of twins. Staff, holding the babies, left to
        right: Iva Habeck, Patricia Kadlec, Dr. G. J. Bloemendaal, Mary Gauer,
        Ila Lass, Jewel Richards Hahler, Naomi Omland and Eileen Owens. Parents
        of the babies: Mr. and Mrs. John P. Schumacher, Mr. and Mrs. Larry
        Haar, Mr. and Mrs. Sam Woldner, Mr. and Mrs. Sam Ryman, and Mr. and
        Mrs. Eugene Rohrbach.

        Dr. Bloemendaal looks over his week's work, six newborns.

        Ipswich Community Hospital emergency operating room.

        Ipswich Community Hospital 1983.

        The Ipswich Clinic, headquarters far Dr. McFee.

Colonial Manor

        Colonial Manor nursing home of Ipswich.

The City of Ipswich contacted CM Corporation Sioux City, lowa in July of 1970
concerning the building of a nursing home in Ipswich, South Dakota. Shortly
thereafter plans began and information was obtained relative to population,
demographic area to be served and need for a nursing care facility in this
area. This information was then presented to the South Dakota Department of
Health.

Nursing and Retirement Home revenue bonds were sold in 1971 and 1972 to raise
$700,000 for the construction of a metal and brick 59 bed skilled nursing care
facility. The groundbreaking ceremony was held in May, 1971, with construction
beginning shortly after.

An open house was held at the Colonial Manor of Ipswich on March 26, 1972. The
first residents were admitted on April 6, 1972 and the home was filled within a
year from opening. Mrs. Maria Evans, a dedicated employee of the Ipswich
Community Hospital for many years, was the first resident.

Due to a shortage of professional nurses the licensure was changed from skilled
to intermediate care on January 1, 1979 and has operated as intermediate care
since that time.

                OUR ACTIVITIES

Commercial Club History 1933-1983

        1983 Commercial Club officers are, left to right: John Hein,
        secretary/treasurer; Jack Clarke, president; and Jon Braband, vice
        president.

A meeting of the business men of Ipswich was held at the C & C Cafe on March
21, 1933 with L.J. Thomas acting as chairman. At this meeting it was decided to
organize a commercial club in the city of Ipswich. A constitution was drawn,
and the officers elected the following men to take charge of the newly formed
club: H.J. Bebermeyer, president; L.B. Cronholm, vice-president; and E.L.
Engler, secretary-treasurer.

The club was started in the dust bowl days of the 30's and has stood the test
of time through the recession days of the early 80's. It is coincidental that
the club should celebrate its first 50 years at the same time Ipswich
celebrates its first 100 years.

It was the original intent of the club to improve the business environment of
Ipswich. But, because of the durability and longevity of the organization, the
intent of its projects switched from strictly commercial to more social,
recreational and educational.

In the earlier days, much time and effort was spent promoting new and better
roads throughout Edmunds County, especially U.S. Highway 12 as well as a road
north from Ipswich to the Edmunds-McPherson County line.

A great deal of time and effort was given looking into the possibilities of
building a new Edmunds County Courthouse in the city of Ipswich. Mina Dam and
Scatterwood Lake improvements were also among the many hard-working projects
undertaken by the Commercial Club. The original name, Mina Dam was later
changed to Lake Parmley through the efforts of the club.

On January 25, 1961 President Roy Lippert outlined a program to stimulate bode
in Ipswich. The plan was coiled Eze-Pickuns. This program is still in effect.

A hospital building committee was established for the purpose of soliciting
money for a new hospital. Stu Holdhusen and Bob Gross were very instrumental in
the success of the new hospital building.

In April, 1969 a special meeting was called to organize the Ipswich Development
Corporation. This was to be a special committee of the Commercial Club.

Other commercial endeavors have included: Farmers' Night, Trade Day, Turkey
Day, Big Hat Day, Harvest Festival, Krazy Days and Farm-City Day.

At one time the Commercial Club sponsored several educational programs
including: 4-H, 4-H Achievement Days, Boys' State, Boy Scouts, as well as
support for the new school addition.

Social and recreational activities that have been helped by the commercial Club
are: Gala Day prizes, Children' Christmas party, marble tournament, skating
rink, swimming pool, park facilities, baseball and softball fields, community
picnics, Christmas and Halloween movies and the High School Athletic banquet.

Special recognition should be given to Levi J. Thomas and Lyle Steinhoff for
the 14 years of service as secretary-treasurer that each of them gave to the
Commercial Club.

Arnold Petersen receives first Betterment Award

        Dr. Jim Engler presenting Betterment Award to Arnold Petersen

Arnold Petersen, right, was presented the first Ipswich Community Betterment
award by Dr. James Engler, president of Ipswich Commercial Club.

The award was presented to Arnold for outstanding community service, and the
promotion of Ipswich. This award will be presented annually to a person or
group that has helped promote and make Ipswich a better community.

Arnold has been a very active member of the Ipswich Commercial Club and Ipswich
Development Corporation, plus various other community functions. The award will
be on display at Ipswich State Bank. Each year the new recipient of the award
will have their name added to the plaque. The award is sponsored by the Ipswich
Commercial Club and Development Corporation.

The Ipswich Commercial Club was organized in 1933. Business and professional
people who have served as president are as follows:

1933, H.J. Bebermeyer: 1934-35, E.L. Engler; 1936, M.B. Bebermeyer; 1937, H.S.
Crissman, 1938, E.J. Williams; 1939, F.E. Lass; 1940, W.K. Herrick; 1941, J.F.
Holdhusen: 1942, T.G. Barclay: 1943, O.K. Prestegard; 1944, J.W. Jeffery; 1945,
H.W. Hembd; 1946, J.B. Taylor; 1947, Roy A. Engel; 1948, Howard O. Samp; 1949,
Leighton L. Jones; 1950, R.G. Gross: 1951, Carl F. Wall, 1952, R.W. Bebermeyer;
1953, H.F. Kasel; 1954, Mike Weber; 1955, C.A. Groseclose; 1956, Louis Thares:
1957, Don Williams; 1958, R.A. Meyer: 1959, J. S. Holdhusen: 1960, Earle
Crissman: 1961, Roy Lippert: 1962, Harley Taylor: 1963, R.W. Beadle, 1964, A.W.
