Development Corporation

The Ipswich Community Development Corporation was officially incorporated on
April 23, 1969. Prior to this formality, it was a division of the Ipswich
Commercial Club farmed in 1967 with the title of Progress and Planning
Committee. This group was formed as a task force to spearhead the social and
economic development of the community trade area, to pursue major projects and
act as a catalytic force in secondary needs.

        1983 Ipswich Development Corporation officers: Dr. James Engler, DDS,
        President (left) and Jock Clarke, vice president.

The first activity of this group was to pull together the area leaders and
concerned community-minded people for the purpose of building a new swimming
pool. This culminated in a very successful city election and a new swimming
pool in 1968. From this effort, another "core group" was organized to pursue
the School District's building problems. The District had suffered a serious
setback at the hands of the electorate in 1967. This core group pulled together
the necessary city and township leaders who redesigned the building addition
and remodeling plans and took the project back to the voters, and the school
bond election was won on May 5, 1969. The new construction and remodeling was
done in 1970.

This, then takes us to the official incorporation of 1969, wherein the group
became the Ipswich Community Development Corporation. The major effort was
directed at the financing, approval and construction of a nursing care center.
After securing approval from the State, arranging an agreement with Colonial
Manors of Sioux City and approval by the City Council, a successful city
election was held on November 10, 1970, approving necessary Industrial Revenue
Development bonds to finance the project. The care center was officially opened
in March, 1971.

The years 1969-71 were spent on many projects but were primarily to assist the
hospital in securing doctors. This effort was responsible in securing two
doctors and providing a suitable office area for their needs. The Corporation
continued their doctor and dentist placement efforts for several years,
responding to the Hospital's request for assistance.

The years 1971-72 were directed primarily toward three major projects. The need
for a good senior citizen low income housing complex was recognized. The
Corporation helped secure land, developed dialogue between interested citizens
and generally was the catalyst that resulted in a very successful housing unit
administered by the United Senior Housing Association. This was also a time
when the Corporation was forming a good industrial base and expertise. A very
successful full color brochure of Ipswich was produced and used in connection
with State advertisement. A heavy lobbying effort was mounted to convince the
State people of our need for a new highway 12 and 45 construction as well as
the "through town" project. This eventually resulted in the new Highway 12
construction and the storm sewer changes that came with the in-town
construction.

The years of 1972-73 saw the Corporation mount a major effort to move and save
the historic arch over Highway 12. In connection with other interested groups,
the corporation was able to obtain a site, secure State funds and move the Arch
to its present site just east of Charles Collier Park.

The years of 1974-75 were mainly concerned with industrial development. Many
contacts were arranged with jar and minor industries, including such names as
Cozy Cab and Herters. Major work consisted of an extensive Edmunds County labor
survey and work necessary to provide information for prospective industrial
concerns. Contacts with the State IDEA offices and the Area Planning Commission
were solidified and explored.

From 1976 to 1978 Ipswich saw a continuation of the industrial effort. The need
for an Industrial Park, the retention of the airport, and the revitalization of
East Main Street became primary concerns. The Corporation purchased the
air-strip and surrounding area as an industrial site, and much of the old
property on East Main was accumulated. In 1978 the Corporation built a new
10,000 square foot industrial building in the park. This building was occupied
by Dakota Recreational Vehicles, Inc., a builder of pickup toppers. The
Corporation also became involved in the National bicentennial celebration plans
for Ipswich.

The years 1979-80 saw the razing of many old buildings on East Main, the sale
of this property to a group that constructed a new Federal Building on this
site. The new building now houses the A.S.C.S., S.C.S. and FmHA. Efforts
continued in further development of the Industrial Park.

In 1981 the Corporation realized what will perhaps be its greatest
accomplishment. Member effort and expertise were able to persuade Sun Products,
Inc. to select a site just east of Ipswich for the purpose of constructing a
sunflower processing plant which will employ about 200 people and cost in
excess of 25 million dollars to construct. Ground breaking ceremonies were held
on April 25, 1981.

The years 1981-82 were also devoted to the revitalization of the Topper factory
which had suffered the bad economic times of the recreational vehicle industry.
Much time was spent working with the State of South Dakota to retain the
Milwaukee Railroad main line. Much effort was also devoted to the forthcoming
Ipswich Centennial celebration in 1983.

Many more projects were accomplished by the Corporation. Literally hundreds of
people worked as board members, advisors, officers and volunteer helpers.

The following men have served as presidents of the Corporation: 1969-70, John
Hein; 1970-71-72, Roy Lippert; 1972-3-4-5, Arnold Petersen; 1975-76, Earle
Crissman; 1977-78, Mick Hogan; 1979-80, Earle Crissman; 1980-81-82, Keith
Dehnert.
                Roy Lippert

Activities

American Legion

In August, 1919 a local post of the American Legion was organized at Ipswich
for the soldiers, sailors and marines who were or had been in the Great War.
Previous to this a picnic was held honoring all men who wore the uniform in the
late war. An aeroplane flight, baseball game, band, community sing, soldiers'
drill and foot races were the fun for the day. A dinner was served to every
soldier who was dressed in khaki.

The first officers of the Legion Post No. 67 were; Commander, W. P. Hardt;
Vice-Commander, F. E. Lass; Adjutant, J. A. Strom; Finance Officer, A. J.
Deschner; Historian, Deane G. Davies; Chaplain, Leo M. Fitzpatrick.

Ipswich Legion Post was named after Clarence W. Elstad who lost his life in
World War I. Elstad was born in Red Wing, Minn. and came to teach in Ipswich
High School in 1913. In 1917 he enlisted and died in June, 1918 at Belleau
Woods, France.

In 1947 the name of Archer was added to the post name making it Elstad-Archer
Post No. 67. Tom Archer, son of Mr. and Mrs. Frank Archer, was a graduate of
South Dakota State College in Brookings. He was a member of 12 Bomb Group 83rd
squadron and was killed in action and buried in Egypt.

Fire destroyed the American Legion Hall on Tuesday night, April 26, 1949, thus
destroying most of the records.

In 1949 the Legion and VFW organized the Vets' Memorial Association and built a
new structure at a cost of $30,800. In 1958 the Vets' Memorial Association
disbanded.

The Taylor Building was purchased in 1959 and was made into an 'on sale' bar.
The name "Fireplace Lounge" was suggested by Howard Williams because of the
fireplace in the building.

An old Ipswich fire truck was given to the Legion by the City for use in
parades. The truck with no box and no windshield was driven from Minneapolis by
E.J. Williams. The Legion restored and painted it, and it appeared in the 1962
State Legion convention parade in Aberdeen. Since then it has seen many
parades!

        Antique fire engine

        In 1982 the American Legion installed the Avenue of Flags. Donors
        purchased the flags and flag poles that were installed. Chairman of the
        project was Jim Morlock.

In 1975 the Masonic Lodge disbanded, and the American Legion Elstad-Archer Post
No. 67 purchased the building by donating $1,000 to the Masons. They in turn
donated it to the Ipswich Community Hospital to help purchase the X-ray
machine. Much remodeling has been done to the building. Two bathrooms have been
added, new tables and chairs, carpeting and tiling have been put in.

The old Veterans' Memorial Building was given to the Ipswich Development
Corporation for a $1,000 stock certificate. Later the property was sold to the
Ipswich State Bank who demolished the building and used the land for the new
bank building.

The Legion has helped the city, school, surrounding community and many
individuals through the years. During the 1920's they drove a team and wagon
and hauled gravel from the pit four miles east of Ipswich to gravel main
street. They have sponsored Junior-Junior, Junior, Teeners and Legion baseball
teams, Boy Scouts, Boys Staters, bowling teams, boys to Sportsmen's Camp, blood
banks, military funerals, grave markers for all vets at the Ipswich Cemetery,
oyster feeds, girls softball teams, wrestling teams and the Avenue of Flags at
the cemetery which were put in place for Memorial Day, 1982.

Some of the money-making projects have been: barn and square dances, minstrel
shows, boxing matches, benefit auctions, carnivals, raffles, turkey shoots,
bumper stickers and rabbit hunts.

The present officers are: Commander, Terry Braun; First Vice-Commander, Edward
Lacher; Second Vice-Commander, Jack Clarke; Adjutant, Harris Swanson, Jr.;
Chaplain, Chris Steen; Historian, Hiram Biel; Americanism Officer, Howard
Williams; Athletic Officer, Mike Olson; Finance Officer, Ken Trammell; Service
Officer, Gary Haar; Sergeant-at-Arms, Bob Olson,
                Howard Williams

American Legion Auxiliary

Ten ladies gathered in the G.A.R. building on October 4, 1921 to organize the
American Legion Auxiliary. Ladies eligible for this organization were mothers,
sisters, daughters and wives of servicemen, and since that time granddaughters
have become eligible for membership.

Officers elected were: President, Melissa Parmley; First vice-president, Mary
Herrick; Second vice-president, Margaret Mater; Secretary, Alice Fish;
Treasurer, Charlotte Strom; Chaplain, Fern Hartzell; Sergeant-at-Arms, Myrtle
Harris and Historian, Olga Herrick.

The charter bearing the name, Elstad Unit No. 67 was received in February,
1922.

Throughout the sixty-one years, the following work has been carried on:
honoring Gold Star Mothers and sisters; selling poppies, placing flags at
veterans graves; sponsoring two Girls State delegates; sponsoring the project
of blood donors; helping aid all veterans and their families by means of
rehabilitation, child welfare and community service programs.

The present charter bearing the name of Elstad-Archer Unit No. 67 was ordered
in 1946.

Meetings are held the second Tuesday of the month as was the custom in the
beginning, and the meetings ore held in the American Legion Post Home which was
formerly the Masonic Building, having been purchased from the Masonic Lodge in
1975. Membership stands at 135, with 118 adult members and 17 juniors.

There ore only two of the original charter members still living, and they ore
Olga Herrick and Beth Crissman.

Present officers ore as follows: President, lone Zahalka; First vice-president,
Fran Morlock; Second vice-president, Irene Buechler; Secretory, June Malsom;
Treasurer, Pot Gisi; Sergeant-at-Arms, Lena Braun; Assistant Sergeant-at-Arms,
Leota Bruckner and Historian, Mabel Hagele.
                Alice E. Hieb

Boys Scouts

The Boy Scouts of America is a World Brotherhood founded February 8, 1910 when
they received a charter by act of Congress. Fifteen years loser Boy Scouts was
organized in Ipswich under the leadership of Mr. Jones, Scout Executive for the
Ipswich troop. Thirty boys attended the organizational meeting Friday, July 10,
1925.

Cum Chat Awhile Club

On March 19, 1931 at the home of Mrs. George Hall, the name Cum Chat Awhile was
adopted for the club in Rosette Township. Previous to this time, it was known
as the Missionary Aid Society.

Church services had been discontinued at the Rosette Baptist Church eight and
one-half miles north of Ipswich; therefore, it was decided to change the name
and form more of a community club, including anyone in the community who wished
to join.

In the summer afternoons, meetings were held in the homes; in winter the ladies
met at noon for dinner with their husbands and pre-school children who wished
to come. Each summer a family picnic was held. A Christmas party with treats
for the children was enjoyed in December.

Money was raised by serving lunch at farm sales; quilts were pieced and raffled
off. Each member paid 50 cent dues. The money went to orphan homes as well as
anything that was needed in the community in case of fire, sickness, or other
tragedy.

Officers at the time the name was changed were: President, Bonnie Bowers;
Vice-president, Mrs. E. P. Jones; Secretory, Mrs. L. Barker; and Treasurer,
Marion Hummell.

Election in June, 1931 gave the organization the following officers: Fannie
Davis, president; Isabel Olson, vice-president; Nellie Bantz, secretory; Marion
Hummell, treasurer. At that time there were 28 members.

When the lost meeting was held, Ruth Auchampach was president; Mrs. Sidney
Wilcox, vice-president; Margaret Stabenow, secretory-treasurer.

Ruth Auchampach, Isabel Olson, Fannie Davis, Pearl Sanborn and Nanny Jones ore
the club's remaining members.
                Fannie Davis

Ducks Unlimited

The Edmunds County Chapter of Ducks Unlimited was organized in the summer of
1980 by a few of the area sportsmen and joining at that time literally
thousands of other chapters throughout the North American Continent. To
understand the nature and purpose of the organization, it is necessary to
review briefly its history.

Since the down of civilization, waterfowl hove held a unique and wondrous
fascination. Watching flocks of migrating ducks darken the sunrise of a crisp
autumn morning; seeing a graceful vee of geese silhouetted high against a yellow
Hunter's Moon; following a flight of mallards as they wheel and drop into A
wind-swept marsh, these ore memorable and pulse-quickening moments.

The seemingly endless flocks of waterfowl in flight was a noble heritage
graciously endowed upon man by nature a heritage for him to en joy, to protect
and to hand down as a legacy for generations to follow. Those who thrill at the
awesome sight of those countless thousands of waterfowl gave little thought to
any possibility of the skies ever becoming almost devoid of ducks. Yet,
fantastic as the possibility seemed, within a few fleeting years offer the 20th
century began, such grim prospects come dangerously close to reality.
Civilization rapidly sprawled westward across the North American Continent and
con jointly the waterfowl population began a downward plunge towards oblivion.

To combat the potential death of the waterfowl heritage, a group of farsighted
conservationists bonded together in January of 1937 to form what is now known
as "Ducks Unlimited". Through its 45 years of existence Ducks Unlimited has
lead the way in the perpetration of waterfowl, utilizing all facilities at its
command to restore, preserve and create nesting habitat for ducks and geese.
The majority of its projects ore built in the provinces of Manitoba,
Saskatchewan and Alberta as these areas contain the bulk of the nesting grounds
for waterfowl on our continent. In 1981 alone over 30 million dollars was
raised through contributions from concerned sportsmen through local chapters
such as ours.

Our local chapter has approximately 60 members. A fund raising banquet and
auction ore held in the foil of each year. Many of our local businesses make
donations for the auction. The proceeds from the event coupled with the
contributions from more than 406,000 other DUers are utilized to preserve our
priceless waterfowl heritage for future generations.
                Mick Hogan

Girl Scouts

Girl Scouting in the United States was organized March 12, 1912 by a woman
named Juliette Gordon Low. Their first meeting was held in Savannah, Georgia.

In tracing the Girl Scout movement to South Dakota, it is found that it all
began in a blizzard during the winter of 1926. As the story is told, Louise
Price, a staff member of Region X of the Girl Scouts, USA, was snowbound in
Aberdeen. Dr. Fought, President of NSTC, suggested that she talk with Jean
Helgeson, a physical education teacher at Northern State Teachers' College, and
Mrs. Harold Webb, on alumni of Northern about scouting.

Three things come of this meeting   a scouting training cross for college
students, the formation of Aberdeen's first Girl Scout troop registered in the
spring of 1927 with Miss Grace Baker as their leader; and the forming of Girl
Scout Council.

It was loser that year, during October, that first mention of girl scouting in
Ipswich con be found in the Ipswich Tribune. There were regular meetings on
Tuesday evening and their officers were: Ruth Evald, president; Berkley Berry,
secretory; and Grace Lucille Loomis, treasurer. Up until 1930, the Girl Scouts
baked their own cookies for the Girl Scout Cookie Sole and 1952 was the first
nut sole. In January, 1966, Ipswich become a member of the NYODA Girl Scout
Council.

The Girl Scout purpose has remained constant that being Girl Scouts we dedicate
ourselves to the purpose of inspiring girls to the highest ideals of character,
conduct, patriotism and service that they may become happy and resourceful
citizens.

Hospital Auxiliary

The Ipswich Hospital Auxiliary was organized in 1961 to assist the Community
Hospital in their building project and to meet the needs of the patients as
Auxiliary volunteers. The organization began with over 300 members and Mrs. Pete
Geffre as the first chairman.

The Auxiliary was divided into 13 units with each unit meeting every other
month excluding June, July and August. On alternate months the chairman of each
unit would meet as on Executive Board to give reports on unit projects and to
take bock the needs of the Auxiliary to their units. Since 1966 the Executive
Board has met on a monthly basis.

        Gleva Kitchell (left) and Irene Bosanko ore the current president and
        vice president of Ipswich Community Hospital Auxiliary

To raise money for the building fund, the Auxiliary held bake sales, turkey
raffles, style shows, bingo, donkey ball game, lunch stand at ball games,
breakfasts, coffee parties, auction sales, and a fall festival which netted
$7,500 in a five year period.

In 1964 the Hospital Board asked the Auxiliary to serve a dinner before the
annual meeting, hoping to increase attendance. The first dinner was served to
approximately 200 and has grown each year until the present time when 550
dinners are prepared.

On August 29, 1965 the newly constructed Community Hospital held an open house.
Coffee and cookies were served to 1,500 people by the Auxilians.

In 1969 Candy Stripers helped out at the hospital under the sponsorship of Mrs.
Bob Kitchell.

In 1972 it was decided to include the Colonial Nursing Home in the Auxiliary
work, and coffee and cookies were served during their open house on March 25
and 26.

In 1973 the Auxiliary helped with Meals-on-Wheels project. Mrs. Myron Fillbach
was in charge. They averaged three meals a day.

Dr. and Mrs. John McFee were welcomed to the hospital and community in 1974
when the Auxilians gave a reception which was attended by 225 people.

In 1975 new drapes were hung in all the patient rooms and $1,900 was raised to
help purchase an X-ray machine.

In 1977 the Auxilians painted the hospital interior.

From 1975 through 1979 Bar-B-Que chicken dinners were served on Achievement
Days to between 500 and 600 people.

In 1981 the group assisted in a state wide colon cancer test with a very strong
response. Each year National Hospital Week has been observed by serving coffee
and cookies. Tours were given for classes from the local schools.

The ground breaking for the new addition was held April 27, 1982 with the
Auxiliary again serving coffee and cookies.

Mrs. Alfred Petersen, Eva Klein, Mrs. Lester Davis and Mrs. George Hamak have
been faithful sewing and mending volunteers over the years. Approximately 175
gowns, 45 pajamas, 30 slippers, numerous bibs and dish towels, binders, mayo
table drapes and operating aprons have been sewn.

Nellie Weishaar served as corresponding secretary for 15 years.

Items that have been purchased include: 11 television sets, two radios, 10
clocks, EKG machine, dictaphone, drapes, mattresses and telephone jacks for
each patient room, hand rails for the halls, geriatric chair, baby scale,
folding crib, high chair, scale, heating pads, bedding, blood pressure bulb and
gauges, IV stand, humidifier, dehumidifier, copying machine, adding machine,
filing cabinet, nurses' reference book, dictionary, mirror for O.B. room, air
conditioner for the kitchen, mixer, blender, toaster, coffee pot and cart.

The latest project is to purchase a color television with remote control with a
nurse call button for each room and color television for the waiting room and
solarium which will come to over $5,000.

        The Ipswich Community Hospital presented these paintings to the
        hospital in honor of the service and dedication that Doctor G. J.
        Bloemendaal and Dr. McFee hove given to the Ipswich Hospital and this
        community. On hand for the presentation of the Gladys and Doctor G. J.
        Bloemendaal painting were the two sons, Doctor Robert (left) and Doctor
        Bill Bloemendaal. The pointing was done by Nancy Lovett Nigg, who was
        delivered by Doctor G. J. Bloemendaal.

        Dr. John McFee and Susan received recognition for their dedication and
        service to the community. This picture will also be displayed in the
        hospital.

Twenty ladies have served as Auxiliary chairmen since its organization.

The Auxiliary has been active in the District and State Hospital Auxiliary
meetings. Ipswich hosted a district meeting in 1981 with record attendance.

An attempt has been made throughout the years to make the hospital as cheerful
and pleasant as possible. Local art work hangs in the halls for the enjoyment
of both patients and visitors.

The Auxiliary has served the hospital and community well and has been a strong
and willing organization over the years.
                Ila Davis

Ipswich Cemetery Association

The first meeting of the Ipswich Cemetery Association was held September 18,
1942, and the by-laws were adopted January 26, 1943. The Association is a
nonprofit corporation organized for the purpose of care and maintenance of the
cemetery. Any one owning a lot is a member.

In April of 1943 a trust fund for perpetual care was established. Lot owners
who do not pay for perpetual care are assessed yearly dues. In 1946 the
perpetual care fund was $500, in 1966 it was $6,900 and now is over $22,000.

The east section of the cemetery was purchased by the city in 1952. The fence
and gate were built in 1954 at a cost of $800.

Paul Satzinger served as president of the Association from 1954 until 1977.
Olga Herrick was secretary from the beginning until 1967. Emily Roberts has
been treasurer since 1962. Present officers are: President, Bob Bosanko; Vice-
president, Robert J. Kitchell; Secretary, Shirley Williams. Three trustees and
five directors are elected to take care of the Perpetual Care Fund and the
business of the Association.
                Charlotte Petersen

Ipswich Flower and Garden Club

        Front row bit to right: Nellie Weiszhaar, Florence Hughes, Martha Steen
        and Clem Gabriel. Back row left to right: Fernella Grosz, Lydia
        Kukrall, Emma Lohnes, Ann Swalve, Loretta Thares, Ruby Hembd, Delphine
        Watembach and Edna Bebermeyer. Not pictured Laura Hodson. Rose Wagner
        in the inset.

The organizational meeting for the group that came to be known as the Ipswich
Flower and Garden Club was held in the Community room of the Edmunds County
Courthouse in Ipswich May 2, 1962. H. W. Hembd acted as temporary chairman.
Officers for the Club elected that evening were: President, Mrs. Howard Kusler;

Vice-president, Mrs. Elmer Lass; Secretary, Miss Ruby Gerdes and Treasurer,
Mrs. Pete Baer. Mrs. Kusler resigned at the next meeting, and H. W. Hembd was
elected to serve as president.

The Club's main purpose at the time of organization and through the years has
been the clean-up and beautification of Ipswich together with projects which
create interest in Ipswich and the surrounding community.

Interest and pride in the general appearance of the city has been enhanced by
the "Yard of the Week" award. The city has been divided into seven sections,
and each week a section is selected for judging by drawing a number at the time
of the survey. This is done each week during the summer months; then the yards
are judged, and a sign is placed in a conspicuous place.

In addition to the work done in helping to beautify the city, the Flower and
Garden Club has Pioneer Village as a project. This endeavor arose from a desire
to preserve a rural school. Much help has been received from individuals and
organizations.

An annual Harvest Supper has been an event in the Community for many years and
is held in either October or November to raise funds for the club's projects.
In addition to the officers President, Loretta Thares; Vice-president, Ruby
Hembd; Secretary, Edna Bebermeyer; and Treasurer, Martha Steen, present members
are: Clem Gabriel, Fernella Grosz, Laura Hodson, Florence Hughes, Lydia Kukrall,
Emma Lohnes, Ann Swalve, Rose Wagner, Del Watembach and Nellie Weiszhaar.
                Loretta Thares

Ipswich Senior Citizens

        The first Ipswich Senior Citizen housing complex was built like a
        figure "H". In the center there is a community room for various
        activities. There are now five Senior Citizen units.

On December 15, 1970, Elsie Bosanko, Ruth Hales, Margaret Evans, Rose Theis and
Pearl Blomster met to discuss the possibility of forming a Senior Citizens'
Club. Eleanore Krokosh, home agent, met with the group on January 8, 1971, and
on January 20, twenty-two senior citizens held a potluck supper and
organizational meeting in the Community Room of the Edmunds County Courthouse.

Officers elected were: Pearl Blomster, president; Margaret Evans and George
Hamak, vice-presidents; Elsie Bosanko, secretary; Ruth Hales, treasurer. The
group continued to meet in the Courthouse or 4-H building until 1972 when a 1
2-apartment low-rental Senior Citizens' apartment house was constructed with an
all-purpose room for the use of the Senior Citizens' Club and the residents
living in the apartments.

The group maintains a membership of nearly one hundred. Business meetings are
held the first Thursday of each month following supper at the Downtown Cafe. A
program is presented at the Senior Center after the business session. Two
suppers are held monthly -- one is a Sing-Along, and the second is bingo night.


Each Wednesday afternoon a lunch followed by various games or visiting is
enjoyed. On the last Monday of each month, a Game Day is held with lunch and
games of the group's choice.

Potluck noon or evening meals are enjoyed on holidays and special days. Two
sets of officers share in the responsibility of keeping the club active and
productive. Various committees are appointed by the officers.

This club has been helpful to older people living alone and needing social
activity.

Lions Club

        After one year in service these Lion officers have collected a good
        share of pins for their vests. (Back row all Directors, left to right)
        Jim Morlock, Raymond Forkel, Larry Stafford, Gary Zahalka, Roni Larman,

The first Ipswich Lions Club received their charter November 28, 1927.

There were 46 members. P. D. Selby was president and H. L. Woodworth was
secretary.

They met at noon the first and third Mondays of the month at the C & C Cafe.

One of the major activities in 1928 was the building of the Memorial Arch over
the Yellowstone Trail, now Highway 12. (The American Legion also helped with
the arch.)

A Farmer-Lion meeting was held to see if there was interest in having an
agricultural course in the Ipswich School under the Smith Hughes Act.

Over the years the club disbanded and in 1981 the Lions Club was re-started and
received a new charter. Charter officers of the newly formed club were Robert
Olson, president; Vince Jensen, first vice-president; Jack Clarke, second vice-
president; Ed Miller, third vice-president; Dave Nelson, secretary; Melvin
Morris, treasurer; Wilmer Deibert, tail twister; Merlin Heying, lion tamer.
Directors of the club were Jon Braband and Jim Morlock.

Jaycee Organization

Jaycee is a young men's organization that offers personal growth through
community development. The Jaycees welcome any young man between the ages of 18
and 35, inclusive, and the organization is designed specifically to meet the
needs of the young man seeking to make his place in his community.

The objects of the organization are: (1) The desire for self-improvement; to
learn how to accept responsibility, to make decisions, become effective
speakers, learn management techniques, to be better employers, employees and
citizens. (2) The desire for community involvement; to have a hand in searching
out community ills and then to plan and execute constructive action projects to
remedy them. (3) The desire for fellowship; to meet progressive young people in
their own age bracket with similar interests and similar goals.

Ipswich Jaycees held an organizational meeting on March 10, 1964. The first
officers elected were: President, Roger Munger; First Vice-President, Bob
Markovetz; Second Vice-President, Jake Hammrich; Secretary-Treasurer, Douglas
Eilers; Directors, Harold Bauer, Earle Crissman and Jim Hanneman; State
Director, Jack Christy.

A few of the community improvement projects the Jaycees have instigated in past
years are: installing street signs and selling house numbers, installing
playground equipment in the park and the swimming pool area, conducting bicycle
safety and safe driving programs, and organizing outstanding young farmer,
young educator and religious leader programs.

Ipswich Jaycee organization was rechartered in 1981 and is now called the
Ipswich Area Jaycees. The current membership includes: Darrel Davis, president;
Warren Heitz, internal vice-president; Craig Pfitzer, community vice-president;
Mike Horst, secretary; Calvin Carlson, treasurer; Jack Clarke, state director;
Wayne Vander Vorste, public relations director, and Tim Gillick, Dale Stoner,
Dave Penfield, directors. Other members include: Lauren Braun, Andy Clemens,
Darwin Davis, Mike Hammrich, Terry Heinz, John Hoffman, Cosmos Kappenman,
Eugene Kraft, Bruce Perrion, Mike Petersen, Kevin Stoner, Terry Stoner, Kenneth
yolk, and Darwin Wockenfuss,

Some recent projects the Ipswich Area Jaycees are conducting this year include:
outstanding business person, Jaycee week, muscular dystrophy telethon, snow
queen, honey bear sales for the mentally handicapped, shooter education, punt,
pass and kick contest, rabbit hunt and teen dance. They have also cleaned up
the softball field and helped clean the Parmley House.

The Jaycee Creed probably best illustrates what Jaycees represent. "We believe
that faith in God gives meaning and purpose to human life; that the brotherhood
of man transcends the sovereignty of nations; that economic justice can best be
won by free men through free enterprise; that government should be of laws
rather than of men; that earth's great treasurer lies in human personality; and
that service to humanity is the best work of life."

        Valette Horst, Jaycette president and Darrell Davis, Jaycee president.

Jaycette

Jaycette is a national service organization for young women. Most of the
members are spouses of Jaycees, although this is not a requirement.

In Ipswich a Jaycee Auxiliary was formed on October 4, 1965. This group
operated only on the local level until May of 1968. At that time they received
their charter and became a South Dakota Jaycette Chapter. Jean Oster was the
first president, and the membership numbered 16. The state project was cosmetic
surgery.

The Jaycettes were an active and vital group of young women. Many community
projects were undertaken by the group immunization clinics, babysitting clinics,
canvassing for the school bond, community art shows, children's flower shows
and newcomers' coffee parties are some of those projects. The organization
remained active until 1975 when membership decreased, and the Chapter
disbanded.

Fortunately in 1981 Jaycettes reactivated in Ipswich. Serving as their
president that year was Paula Torrence. The current leader is Valette Horst,
and the Chapter has 15 members. State projects now include Sioux Falls Jaycee
Camp, Juvenile Diabetes and Muscular Dystrophy. In addition the national
organization helps support St. Jude's Children's Hospital.

Masons

        Masonic Temple Ipswich S.D., May 6, 1957. George Reese, 50th
        Anniversary as a Mason, Levi Thomas, Willard Brown and J. D. Williams.

Devotion Lodge No. 76 AF & AM was granted a charter on June 11, 1885 from the
Grand Lodge of Dakota Territory with headquarters in Fargo, Dakota Territory.
Charter members were Daniel Rodman, Al Haines, Albert Smith, C.W. Huntley,
Charles B. Purdy, Homer B. Smith, Levi D. Gray, C W Stafford, Joseph S.
Stockton and Seth Jillson. Edgar L. Hopkins was the first person initiated.

In 1914, a committee consisting of L.C. Fix, J.W. Parmley and M.C. Westrater
was appointed to consider construction of a building. The first meeting in the
new building was held in February, 1923. After many years of service to the
community, the Masonic Lodge closed the door in 1974. Many of the members then
joined the Cresbard or Aberdeen Lodges.

The Masonic Lodge was happy to be able to turn the building over to the
American Legion. They have kept the building in good repair, and it continues
to be a center of activity for the good of the community.
                Harris C. Swanson

        The former Masonic Temple is now the American Legion Post Home.

Old Time Music Group

The Old-Time Music Group had its beginning several years ago at a party given
for Walter Bailey and Emil Simon whose birthdays are January 20 and 22. Then on
January 21, 1981, the group held a Jamboree, and since then they get together
every week whenever it is possible.

Members of the group are Walter Bailey, Roy Bailey, Glenn and Lucille Stafford,
Emil Simon, Willard Chambers, John Morgan and Dwain Gibson.

Walter Bailey began playing the fiddle when he was 11 years old. He has played
for area dances for many years. He has the distinction of being the first
fiddler champion of South Dakota and is a member of the South Dakota Hall of
Fame.

Emil Simon started on the violin at the age of 12. All of his knowledge has
come through a correspondence course.

Lucille Simon Stafford received a Hawaiian guitar when she was 12 and learned
from the book of instruction that came with the instrument.

Glenn Stafford became interested in the guitar through the Liberty Community
Club. He received a few pointers from Bill Lundeen, a talented fellow who
enjoyed passing on his talents to young people.

Roy Bailey played a banjo with Walter. When he was 16 he played back-up on the
piano for dances. He played the fiddle in public in 1976 and entered his first
contest in 1977. Since then he has won three trophies. Willard Chambers joined
the group in 1981 when he retired and returned to South Dakota. He played with
the Bailey Brothers while he was a student at Loyalton High School. Bill plays
a mandolin, bass fiddle, guitar and fiddle.

Dwain Gibson began playing his grandmother's fiddle in 1979. Since that time he
has played in the Yankton Fiddler's Contest and participated in the Black Hills
Blue Grass fiddling contest in 1981. Dwain was awarded second in the
Intermediate division in Yankton in 1981. He is now compiling a collection of
fiddle tunes that have not been put to music as yet but that are played by the
area fiddlers.

John Morgan joined the group in 1981. He has played the harmonica as a hobby
for many years. He now plays harmonica and some guitar.

The Old-Time Music Group has entertained many organizations in the area
including the Senior Center and nursing homes. They have appeared in parades
and community celebrations.

Since this group has dusted off their instruments to recapture some of the
enjoyment of their youth, they have succeeded in giving pleasure to many
people.
                John Morgan

Walter Bailey

Walter Bailey was a young lad of eleven when he started playing on a $3.00
violin his father had bought for him. When he was twelve, he played for his
first dance. Since then fiddling has become a big part of his life. In January
he will be celebrating his 61st year of playing.

Walter was the first South Dakota champion in 1973. It was the first state
contest ever held. It took place in Yankton, S.D. He has had the championship
twice since. In 1980 Walter was inducted in South Dakota Hall of Fame for old
time fiddling. It was a big thrill for him and something he never dreamed could
have happened to him 61 years ago.

Order of the Eastern Star

Ipswich Chapter, Order of the Eastern Star, No. 111, of Ipswich, South Dakota,
was organized March 5, 1915. The charter members were: Mrs. Camelia Barney,
Mrs. Alice C. Beebe, Mrs. Addie Bigger, Mrs. Mary K. Chubbuck, Mrs. Marie
Hartelt, Mrs. Ruby Jackson, Mrs. Mamie Lewis, Mrs. Melissa Parmley, Mrs. Lydia
Ring, Miss Jane Jones, Mrs. Maud 1. Jones, Mrs. Ruth Johnson, Mrs. Helen Smith,
Mrs. Louise Smith, Mr. Lockwood Smith, and Mr. Simon C.A. Ring.

The following were the first officers chosen to serve the Chapter: Worthy
Matron, Melissa Parmley; Worthy Patron, Simon C.A. Ring; Associate Matron,
Camelia Barney; Secretary, Addie Bigger; Treasurer, Helen A. Smith;
Conductress, Lydia Ring; Associate Conductress, Ruby Jackson; Adah, Jane Jones;
Ruth, Alice C. Beebe; Esther, Louise Smith; Martha, Ruth Johnson, Electa, Maud
Jones; Organist, Mamie Lewis; Warder, Mary Chubbuck, and Sentinel, Lockwood
Smith. Thirty-nine new members were received into the Chapter during the first
year.

The first Grand Officer to be appointed from Ipswich Chapter was Simon C.A.
Ring as Grand Sentinel in Aberdeen in June, 1914. The first Worthy Grand Matron
to visit the Chapter was Mrs. Florence Potter of Webster who came on December
16, 1914.

In 1956 Ipswich Chapter was honored by having Mrs. Muriel Brown elected Worthy
Grand Matron of the Grand Chapter of South Dakota. Members besides Simon C.A.
Ring, appointed as officers to Grand Chapter were: Melissa Parmley, Grand
Martha, in 1918; Nella T. Westrater, Grand Chaplain in 1924; Muriel Brown,
Grand Chaplain in 1928; Neva Williams, Grand Chaplain in 1946; Marie
Bebermeyer, Grand Organist in 1956 and Elva Groseclose, Grand Adah in 1963.

At Grand Chapter held in Yankton in 1961 Muriel Brown was honored on her 25th
anniversary as Worthy Grand Matron of the Grand Chapter of South Dakota.
Sixteen members of the chapter attended. Gloris Dehnert, Worthy Matron,
presented Sister Muriel with a gift of silver from the Chapter.

The year 1963 was the Golden Anniversary of our Chapter and the Diamond Jubilee
of the Grand Chapter. June 9 was the day chosen to observe our 50th
anniversary. It was a day to remember with the Temple beautifully decorated and
a very interesting and memorable program with many members and guests
attending. Six fifty-year members were present to receive their fifty-year pins
and Life Membership certificates: Alice C. Beebe, Helen Hembd, Lillian Witz,
Ada Walster, Ruth Auchampach and Willard A. Brown.

Mrs. Alice C. Beebe, Mrs. Louise Smith and Mrs. Marie Hartelt, all residing in
California, are the only living charter members.

Through the years the Chapter has received recognition as many of the members
have been appointed Grand Representatives, Grand Committee members, Grand Pages
and Grand Sentinels.

The final meeting of Ipswich Chapter, No. 111 was held on December 5, 1973.
After much careful thought and discussion concerning the matter, it was decided
to affiliate with Golden Rule Chapter of Cresbard.
                Ada Benton

P. E. O.

        Back row left to right: Hazel Engle, Dorothy Engel, Ila Lass, Sharron
        Swanson, Helen Herrick, Carol Holdhusen, Ginny Dehnert, Margaret Nelson,
        Myrtice Satzinger, Corinne Jameson, Helen Gross. Front row left to
        right: Sue Batteen, Neva Williams, Survilia Herrick, Beth Crissman,
        Gladys Williams, Betsy Holdhusen, Charlotte Petersen, Mary Jo Vander
        Vorste, Camilla Kolker. Very front: Dorla Kantack, Judy Steen. Members
        not pictured: Stacy Braun, Gloris Dehnert, Loree Swanson, Annette Taylor.

Chapter BB, P.E.O., was organized at the home of Muriel Brown, May 1, 1942.
Charter members were: Ruth Cronholm, Muriel Brown, Alice Beebe, Lucy Beebe,
Neva Williams, Edna Woodworth, Ruth Holdhusen, Survilia Herrick, Fay Hall,
Hazeldean Thielen, Lillian Thiel and Beth Crissman.

P.E.O. is a philanthropic organization that owns and operates Cottey College in
Nevada, Missouri. This two year liberal arts college draws students from every
state and many foreign countries. The Educational Fund offers low interest
loans to help worthy young women finance their education. International Peace
Scholarships provide for foreign women to study in the United States or Canada,
and the program for Continuing Education is a grant to aid women who need to
return to school. Other educational assistance comes from a State Scholarship
Fund and local chapter support.

On May 1, 1982 Chapter BB celebrated forty years of service. Former chapter
members and special guests joined the local group for a luncheon and program.

Thirty-two women are on the active membership -- of these three are charter
members.

The chapter's obligation to the Cottey College Anniversary Fund was met in
1982.

Present officers are Carol Holdhusen, president; Sharron Swanson,
vice-president; Virginia Dehnert, recording secretary; Camilla Kolker,
corresponding secretary; Sue Batteen, treasurer; Margaret Nelson, chaplain, and
Judy Steen, guard.
                Dorothy Engel

Progress Club

        May 25, 1979, 40th Anniversary. Back row: Mary Dolan, Bernice Engler,
        Bertha Lass, Orene Strom, Helen Jeffery, Blanche Van Schaack, Marie
        Bebermeyer (deceased), Lucille Meyers, Ila Lass, Ione Engler. Middle
        row: Survilia Herrick, Neva Williams, Faith Sampson, Ann Haeder, Alma
        Haux, Hazeldean Thielen. Front row: Mary Engler, Grace Arnold, Dorothy
        Engel, Alice Jones.

Progress Club was organized in March, 1939 as a study club by the following
ladies: Mrs. Ann Haeder, Mrs. Ella Oberlander, Mrs. Faith Sampson, Mrs. Dorothy
Engel, Alice Roberts Beadle, Mrs. Neva Williams, Mrs. Peggy Bach, Mrs. Fay
Hall, Mrs. Verna Barclay and Mrs. Grace Lucille Stevens.

The club has always met on the third Thursday of the month with the exception
of July and August. Dues started at 10 cents a meeting and have since raised to
one dollar a year. Afternoon meetings changed to evening meetings and light
refreshments to dinner meetings. Membership grew from ten to fifteen.

The club was a member of the National Federation of Women's Clubs from 1939 to
1958.

Besides studying and reporting on various books or topics of interest, member
travelogues and guest experiences, Progress Club has helped with many
charitable and fund raising projects. These projects include: the old hospital
was helped in the amount of $975, and $1,000 was contributed to the new
hospital together with $2,000 towards the equipment; Colonial Manor was given
$700 for equipment; a set of encyclopedias was presented to Beebe Memorial
Library; the sum of $100 went to Pioneer Village; a contribution was made
toward the public school choir robes and to Abbott House in Mitchell.

For many years a zinnia garden was planted and cared for in the downtown area.
Later petunias were planted in the Library Park and at the entrance of the
Community Hospital.

Since 1956 the December meetings were devoted only to packing and delivering of
Christmas boxes   consisting of cookies, candy and fruit, for shut-ins and
elderly people of Ipswich.

These projects were financed in various ways, such as food baskets passed among
the members, "Make It and Take It" project, cookie sales, a Christmas Fair,
Thrift Shop and a Bicentennial quilt raffle.

On May 25, 1979 the club celebrated its 40th anniversary with a banquet and
program at the Holiday Inn at Aberdeen. Five charter members were present: Ann
Haeder, Grace Lucille Arnold, Dorothy Engel, Neva Williams and Faith Sampson.

The club now gives interesting roll calls and plays bridge after potluck
dinners.

When a worthwhile community project comes up the members are there to help and
donate. The present membership includes: Dorothy Engel, Ione Engler, Mary
Engler, Alma Haux, Survilia Herrick, Helen Jeffery, Alice Jones, Bertha Lass,
Ila Lass, Lucille Meyer, Faith Sampson, Blanche VanSchaack and Neva Williams.
Bernice Engler is an honorary member.
                Ione Engler

Rosette Rebekah Lodge No. 195

The Independent Order of Odd Fellows, jurisdiction of South Dakota, was
instituted at Ipswich on March 4, 1926, in the old American Legion Hall, by
Sister Agnes Mathieu of Mercy Rebekah Lodge No. 158, Stratford, South Dakota,
as Special Deputy, under Grand Master, William Bernhardt, and Grand Secretary,
F.B. Raymond.

The charter members were Meriam Archer, Helene Tiggelaar, Lizzie Shew, Olive
Horton, Mina Brunkow, Louise Smith, Alice Fish, Grace Coulter and Cecil
McCleary who were admitted by card. Forty-six were received by invitation.

Since that date, 92 more members have joined. Transfer, withdrawal or other
card and death have caused the membership to decrease to 15. Of these, five are
non-residents.

The Legion Hall burned in the year 1949, and many of the records were destroyed
in that fire. Meetings were then held in the old school building or in the home
of A.L. Fish. On May 11, 1950 the new Veterans' Memorial Building became the
meeting place.

On June 9, 1965 Rosette Rebekah Lodge 195 of Ipswich received a petition, a
vote was taken and it was agreed to accept the members of Colonial Rebekah
Lodge No. 168, Cresbard, into membership by consolidation. Permission for this
was given and dispensation was granted on August 11, 1965 by Grand Master,
Ernie S. Severance.

For the convenience of a small group, members meet in the homes the second
Thursday of each month. The ill, elderly and bereaved are remembered with
cards, small gifts and memorials to the Home at Dell Rapids. A generous
response is made to the Odd Fellow Home, Assembly President's Project of the
year, and other worthwhile projects.

The first Noble Grand was Meriam Archer, Vice-Grand, Rebecca Beals; Secretary,
Grace Jones.

Present elective officers are Noble Grand, Edith Smith; Vice-Grand, Margaret
Evans; Secretary, Eleanore Krokosh; Treasurer, Alma Haux.

Sons and Daughters of Wales

The Sons and Daughters of Wales was started in 1976 when Ipswich was preparing
a program for the Bi-Centennial celebration. The Welsh people who live in the
Powell area were asked to prepare several musical numbers for the program.

About 22 people attended the first practice session. Among the songs that were
to be sung were: "The Bull Frog", "The Land of My Fathers" and "Men of
Harlech". This time spent was so enjoyed that it was decided to organize.

The group meets at least twice a year. Following a potluck supper, an evening
of singing is enjoyed. From 30 to 50 people attend each time and nearly
everyone is of Welsh descent. They come from Aberdeen, Bath, Wecota, Cresbard,
and, of course, Powell where it all started.

Ellen Pond was elected first president; Margaret Evans, vice-president and
Mary Jane Opsahl, secretary-treasurer.

Adding to the enjoyment of the group, have been visitors from Wales - Nansie
Own and daughter Enid of Carnarvon North Wales, cousins of David Griffith of
Wecota and Tom and Mair Jones from the Isle of Angelsey, North Wales, a cousin
of Margaret Evans.

In October 1980, through the effort of Dean Hodson and his cousin, Dai Davis, a
group of Welsh singers from Wales came to Powell and Ipswich. They were
professional singers who had won recognition at the National Eistetddfod in
Wales. Several enjoyable concerts were presented by this group.

These singers planned to be in America in 1982 to present concerts in several
cities.

Members of the Sons and Daughters of Wales want to keep their organization
alive because it is so enjoyable and it helps each of them to keep in touch
with the mother country --- Wales.
                Ellen Pond

Sportsman's Club

The forerunner of the Ducks Unlimited organization was the Ipswich Sportsman's
Club. The purpose of the club was to promote better public relations between
the people of Ipswich and surrounding area who enjoyed outdoor sports.

Lauren Stager of Cresbard was responsible for organizing the Club on May 20,
1955.

Gun safety courses for the area children and adults were held. Among the many
projects of this Club were: Snow Queen contest, horse races at Trail Day, trap
shoots and the maintenance of a boat loading dock on the west side of Lake
Parmley.

Tourist Club

The first study club to be organized in Ipswich was the Tourist Club. It was
founded on October 28, 1897 by Mrs. C. H. Barron, Mrs. Robert Hill, Mrs. M. K.
Reese, Mrs. S. V. Arnold, Mrs. J. L. Smith, Mrs. Edson Williams and Miss
Gertrude Williams. Their purpose was for mutual benefit and social
entertainment. The membership was limited to fifteen. The Tourist Club was a
member of the City Federation of Clubs in Ipswich, but chose not to join the
National Federation of Clubs.

Mrs. S. V. Arnold was chosen as the first president. She was appointed by
Governor Lee to serve on the Woman's Visiting Board, which checked on the
charitable and penal institutions. She named South Dakota, "The Sunshine State".
After 70 years as the "Sunshine State", it was changed to "The Proud State".
The members of the club planted an elm tree in the city park, helped with the
Zinnia gardens and supported school and civic programs. They had special
observances to commemorate the twentieth, twenty-fifth, fiftieth, sixtieth and
seventy-fifth anniversaries.

The Tourist Club met at the home of Mrs. H. S. Crissman on October 10, 1972 to
celebrate the 75th anniversary of the club. The following members were present:
Mrs. Ralph Bebermeyer, Mrs. Gar Griffith, Mrs. W. S. Hagen, Mrs. Raymond Hieb,
Mrs. J. W. Hodson, Mrs. W. H. Thielen, Mrs. Elmer Lass and one guest, Miss Jean
Lyken of Huron. Mrs. H. S. Crissman, Mrs. Helen Hassenpflug, Mrs. F.E. Lass and
Mrs. James Harris were second generation members and Mrs. W.H. Thielen was a
third generation member.

The Tourist Club continued to be active until the summer of 1974 when they
decided to discontinue because of small membership and ailing health of some of
the members. At the time the active members were: Mrs. R. W. Bebermeyer, Mrs.
H.S. Crissman, Mrs. Gar Griffith, Mrs. W.S. Hagen, Mrs. Raymond Hieb, Mrs. J.
W. Hodson and Mrs. Elmer Lass.
                Bertha Lass

Twentieth Century Club

        Twentieth Century officers, back row, left to right Deb Jones, Marge
        Marquette, Edna Bebermeyer, Ruby Bosanko. and Betty Fillbach.

Sixty-eight years ago a group of pioneer women met at the home of Alice Caborn
to organize the 20th Century Club, the purpose of which, as stated in the
constitution, was "mutual enjoyment and improvement".

The motto chosen by the group was "Through knowledge we behold the world's
creations". Green and white were selected to be the club's colors and the white
carnation was named the club flower.

During its first year of existence, the group joined the State Federation, thus
becoming part of a group of several million like-minded women.

Since its beginning the organization has held regular monthly meetings, except
for brief periods during World Wars I and II when club time was devoted to Red
Cross work.

In 1917, the organization entertained the District Convention. On this occasion
the ladies of the Congregational Church served a banquet for fifty cents a
plate. District conventions were also held in Ipswich in 1925, 1949 and 1958.

Our local Progress Club was sponsored by the 20th Century organization. This
group has proved to be a definite asset to the community and the 20th Century
is proud of them.

For many years the club planted and cared for the flowers in the city park.
Mrs. Minnie Krambeal directed this project. Zinnia beds on main street were
also planted and cared for by this group.

The monthly programs have been varied and have included debates, book reviews,
papers on timely subjects and musical evenings.

Each year 20th Century selects a special project to help with financially. One
recent guest night was devoted to the work done with crippled children by the
Shriners. Neil Kleppin and a fellow Shriner came out from Aberdeen to show
movies and present a talk on the work of the Shriners' Hospital. A generous
gift was made to this worthy project.

Our years in club work have added vital hours to our lives, and we hope much
good to the community.
  "Coming together is a beginning,
  Keeping together is progress
  Working together is success"
                Myrtice Satzinger

Yellowstone Trail CowBelles

Ten women affiliated with the South Dakota CowBelles when they organized the
first Cowbelle Association in Edmunds County on August 17, 1976 at the home of
Ervin and Willa Beitelspacher in Ipswich.

Mrs. Ronald Beiber, representing Circle L CowBelles of Leola, was present to
answer questions and to lead discussions on beef promotion activities.
CowBelles is an auxiliary to the Stockgrowers' Association and has as their
main objective the promotion of beef and beef products.

Officers elected at this first meeting were: Eleanore Krokosh, Ipswich,
president; Frieda Schurr, Roscoe, vice-president; Verna Oban, Loyalton,
secretary; Willa Beitelspacher, Ipswich, treasurer. Appointed officers
included: Ila Davis, Ipswich, parliamentarian; Sharron Swanson, Ipswich,
historian; Frieda Schurr, Roscoe, publicity chairman.

During the first business meeting, Yellowstone Trail CowBelles was selected as
the official club name. Yellow hats and blazers were purchased to accent the
outfits of black which were made complete with appropriate identification name
tags.

Throughout the year fourteen more members were enrolled, making a total of
twenty-four members.

Some of the club's major projects are: assisting the state officers at the
South Dakota CowBelle booth during the State Fair in Huron; in 1978 assistance
was given to Al's Meat Market at the Prairie Kitchen in promoting beef sausages
for sale and in serving hot beef sausage samples during State Fair week;
members judge contestants during the annual 4-H Special Foods Contest and
select winners in each division from the entries containing beef. National Beef
Cookbooks ore awarded to each winner.

In 1981 six members attended the National Beef Cookoff in Sioux Falls and were
responsible for the hospitality booth one afternoon. The club sponsored Carol
Holdhusen, Ipswich, as the first entrant from Edmunds County in the State Adult
Cookoff held in Mitchell in 1981; Rev. Jean Rosewall was one of ten finalists
who competed at the State Beef Cookoff in Rapid City in 1982.

An annual dinner-dance is held in observance of National Agriculture Day in
March, and the tickets include a chance on a quarter of beef. Beef Booster
Awards are given to those who have supported the Cowbelles in promoting beef
during the year. Al Hochalter, Ipswich, was the recipient of the award in 1980;
FEM Electric, Ipswich, in 1981; Carol Holdhusen, Ipswich, in 1982.

On a designated day during the legislative session, Cowbelles arrive early at
the Capitol building in Pierre to sit in on committee meetings. Topics
pertaining to the beef industry are discussed with the legislators. Long tables
are filled with beef tidbits for the legislative body to sample. In January,
1978 when twelve members were returning from this session, they were snowbound
at Gettysburg Junction for two days and nights an experience not soon to be
forgotten!

Fund-raising activities include the sale of beef cookbooks, instant beef broth,
stationery, hat tacks and bumper stickers; serving lunches at farm auction as
well as operating the lunch and concession stand at 4-H Achievement Days and
Horse Show have become the major fund-raising events of the year.

In October each member is encouraged to invite guests who might be interested
in joining the CowBelles organization. A special program is planned, and a
state officer is invited as a special guest.

Present elected officers are: Carol Perrion, Ipswich, president; Verna Oban,
Roscoe, vice-president; Marlene Stoner, Ipswich, secretary; Sharron Swanson,
Ipswich, treasurer. Appointed officers include: Sharron Swanson, Ipswich,
parliamentarian; Sylvia Ertz, Ipswich, historian; Diane Zahalka, Ipswich,
publicity chairman; Eleanore Krokosh, Ipswich, reporter.

Delores Lehr, Roscoe, and Eleanore Krokosh, Ipswich, serve as Directors for the
Yellowstone Trail CowBelles of Ipswich, Edmunds County, South Dakota.
                Eleanore Krokosh

Yellowstone Trail Saddle Club

The Yellowstone Trail Saddle Club was organized in 1967. A flag and badge were
designed by Duane Perrion, and Mrs. Duane Niles made the flag.

In 1968 The Ipswich Commercial Club helped financially with the construction of
an arena west of town. It was later enlarged and in 1971 improved with the
addition of lights, speaker system, lunch stand, rest rooms and other equipment
needed for horse shows.

The club holds about three horse shows and some trail rides during a year.

For three years a queen contest was sponsored. Contestants rode in the Trail
Day parade, and the winner was crowned at the horse show held that day. The
queens were: 1969, Mrs. Edwin Job; 1970, Mrs. Arland Ranson; 1971, Debbie
Hazelhorst.

Two polo teams were organized in 1968, and a league formed with Bowdle and
Hoven. An Ipswich team won the traveling trophy three successive years.

In 1970 a fund drive was held for Lee Hazelhorst's heart surgery, and a
dialysis machine provided for Marsha White.

The beard contest was sponsored for the Bicentennial in 1976 and the money made
from the sale of Brothers of the Brush buttons was given to the city for
playground equipment in Collier's Park.

There is an annual membership dinner and an annual dinner-dance held the
opening day of pheasant hunting season. The present membership is 35.

W. C. T. U.

From its beginning in 1884 until the organization disbanded in 1956, the
Women's Christian Temperance Union played an important role in the Ipswich
community.

For several years Ipswich was the largest Union in the State of South Dakota
with 145 members.

G. A. R.

November 3, 1888 a branch of the Grand Army of the Republic, known as the
Winfield Scott Post, Department of Dakota No. 48, was organized. Members of the
organization were war veterans.

This group was active until the death of S.V. Arnold when it ceased to
function. On Memorial Day, 1923, at the request of the three remaining members,
the flag and roster were changed to the Clarence W. Elstad Post of American
Legion.

The Women's Relief Corp Auxiliary

The Women's Relief Corp Auxiliary to the Grand Army of the Republic, also known
as the Winfield Scott Post, No. 48 was organized in Ipswich on March 3,1894.
This group played an important part in Ipswich social life during its
existence.

Royal Neighbors

Royal Neighbors Lodge, Camp 2689 was organized in March, 1906. Meetings were
held in the lodge hall above the opera house for many years until it was
decided to meet in the members' homes. This was continued until 1957 when the
camp membership was transferred to the Aberdeen Camp.

                RECREATION

Fun and Games

The history of the city parks, the swimming pool, and the sports fields is a
history of individual generosity, service-minded groups, a forward-looking city
government, and a people that believed in the importance of this aspect of
their community life.

The park adjacent to the Marcus P. Beebe Memorial Library became a part of the
city's recreation facilities also through the efforts of Mr. Beebe, who deeded
the land to the city October 4, 1937. Through the years the stone benches,
paths, trees and flower beds have provided a beautiful setting for many
activities. When a band shell costing $11,900 was erected in 1961, enjoying
music in the city park became a Saturday night tradition once again in Ipswich.
The band shell has been the site of many types of entertainment, including on
historical pageant celebrating the Bicentennial in 1976 and pep rallies for the
Gala Day Homecoming events. In 1982 in preparation for the Ipswich Centennial,
the band shell was rejuvenated after many hours of work by the Jaycees.

Charles Collier, a rural mail carrier for many years, served the community of
Ipswich in his off-duty hours as well. On his own land bordering Highway 12, he
developed and maintained a park that has been used with pleasure by Ipswich
people and by travelers passing through. After Mr. Collier's death the property
was deeded to the city in 1958. Although the park has always been
affectionately known as Charlie's Park, it is officially the Charles Collier
Memorial Roadside Park. Following Charlie's example of the devoted caretaker,
the city has added water and restrooms, playground equipment, a picnic shelter,
camping facilities, and safety fencing along the highway. When Highway 12 was
to be widened, the Memorial Arch had to be moved from its long-time location
spanning the highway. In 1975 a slow-moving process, plagued with difficulties,
finally took the historic arch to its new resting place in Charlie's Park. The
arch was rededicated in 1976 as a part of the Bicentennial celebration, and the
park had a venerable addition to its attractions, one which would be a source
of great pride for Charlie, since the arch has long been Ipswich's most
renowned landmark.

Mr. and Mrs. Sidney V. Arnold donated property to the City for use as a park
and it was named Arnold's Pork. It was located between First and Second Avenues
adjacent to Ninth Street and included walkways, trees and a drinking faucet for
the enjoyment of its users.

Its use as a park declined through the years as the trees died and upkeep was
not continued. In 1951, the City Council was approached by Ray Van Schaack and
E. C. Coddington relative to using it for a kittenball field. It was through
the efforts of the Better Ipswich Club and many other interested citizens that
a backstop bleachers and lights were installed for the enjoyment of the players
and spectators. It is still in use as a softball field.

Baseball and football have also long been popular in Ipswich. The baseball fans
of the early years spent many hot summer Sunday afternoons sitting on their
cars and cheering on the town teams. On Friday afternoons in the foil, the
school and many businesses simply closed down to attend the football games.
Then in October, 1961 plans for a new lighted athletic field were presented to
the City Council. The lights were first used on June 28, 1962, when the Ipswich
baseball team defeated Long Lake 5-4. These lights were later replaced through
the cooperative efforts of the American Legion, the City Council, and the
Ipswich Public School. The improved lighting system was ready to use for the
football games in the fall of 1982. During the Centennial celebration the field
is to be rededicated and named the Ken Herrick Memorial Field to honor one of
the city's most loyal and avid sportsmen.

Hiram Beebe was behind the planning for the first swimming pool in Ipswich. Mr.
Beebe and a committee appeared before the City Council April 4, 1927, with
their proposal for a pool. The stockholders of this association loser gave the
pool to Ipswich in 1948. The spoon-shaped oval pool with its eight foot diving
area was located across the street from the courthouse. One-meter and two-meter
diving boards provided challenges for divers, and many hot summer cloys were
enjoyed in the water, supervised by a succession of life guards, including Ed
Beebe, son of "Hi" Beebe. Roscoe, Hosmer, and other area towns often bused
swimmers to Ipswich for a few hours of fun and exercise. However, the
inadequate filtering system and the ravages of time finally brought an end to
the pool's use. Today the bathhouse stands, the only remnant of the happy
splashing that once took place in a pool that is now filled with earth.

Ipswich missed having a swimming pool for some years. Then on August 8, 1968, a
new pool in the northwestern part of town opened for use. The pool is L-shaped,
measuring 30' x 71' and 36' x 75'. The bathhouse includes an equipment room,
pool heater, filtering plant, shower and locker rooms and a checkout room and
counter. Although the first season was short, over 2600 people used the pool.
Swimming has continued to be a popular summer sport in Ipswich.

Golf

Shortly after World War I, local golf enthusiasts organized their first club
and rented a pasture adjoining Ipswich's westside south of Highway 12. Here
they built a nine-hole course with sand greens.

Some of the people most instrumental in building the course included Paul
Selby, Lyman Beardsley, Lefty Henderson, Dr. A. Auchampach, Vern Mater, Ed
Engler, Ralph Bartholemew, Ole Bruce, Ed Lass, Dave Hartzell, Evan Jenkins, Ken
Herrick and Herb Crissman.

Dr. Auchampach, a local dentist, was one of the all-time great local golfers.
The top lady golfer was undoubtedly Edna Woodworth who won the Ladies' State
Championship two years.

        The Ipswich Golf Club House and the first building to house 10 golf
        carts.

The present course is slightly different from the original nine-hole course,
but it still has sand greens, and the location is the same. The course has been
improved in recent years, and many trees have been planted on the fairways to
make play more challenging. The land on which the course lies was donated to
the city by Mrs. John B. Taylor.

Interest in the course by about two dozen ardent golfers kept golf alive in
Ipswich from the early days until 1968 when a club house was built. This was
possible through a $12,000 FmHA loan.

Numerous golf carts came into use in the early 1970's so a ten stall golf cart
garage was built in 1978. Golf leagues were organized. Many of the ladies
became interested, and in 1981 a second ten stall golf cart garage was
constructed.

The average number of members down through the years has been 85. With the
purchase of new equipment, maintenance and upkeep have been exceptional. The
gross receipts have grown to over $30,000 annually. Hopefully, plans for the
future will include grass greens.

The governing body at the present time consists of seven board members. From
that group a president, vice-president and secretary-treasurer are selected.
The officers succeed themselves on the board for the following year. Numerous
invitational, as well as local tournaments are held during the golf season,
including both men's and women's and the very popular Adam and Eve tourney which
is a one-ball twosome.

        From the second storage shed for golf carts, the golfer can enter to
        the number 1 tie box and enjoy a pleasant afternoon of golf.

Local Texas Best Ball was introduced in 1981-82 and is also well attended.
Potluck suppers conclude the day or evening festivities.
                Mary and Len Engler

Sports

Hunting and Fishing

Buffalo once roamed the prairie east of the Missouri River, but by the time
Ipswich was platted their numbers had begun to dwindle. John Erbe, a
homesteader west of Ipswich, shot one of the big beasts in July, 1884. He
brought the meat to Ipswich where he found a ready market for it at twelve and
one-half cents a pound according to the July 31, 1884 Ipswich Tribune. It was
thought to be the last wild buffalo killed in the eastern half of the state,
and its mounted head hung for years in J.W. Parmley's office.

Our early day sportsmen found prairie chickens to be abundant and shooting the
birds over a good dog was considered the tops in sports. The season usually
opened September 1 and on that day just about everybody took to the fields with
guns, dogs and food. Shotgun shells were considerably cheaper in those days, 65
cents a box for smokeless powder and 50 cents for black powder. Since people
had no modern methods to preserve their surplus kill, they had to drive about
the town after the hunt and give their extra birds away.

Duck and goose hunting has continued from the early days until now. Upland
waterfowl were so numerous in the early days that there was a spring season for
them, however, they were mostly hunted during the fall season which opened
September 1. Bold's Lake to the northwest of Ipswich and the Scatterwood Lake
area southeast of town have always been popular duck hunting areas. During wet
years sloughs and potholes around Ipswich produce ducks) but hunters like the
large migrations of mallards which come down from the north later in the fall.

The Game Department planted the Chinese Ringneck Pheasant in South Dakota and
the first open season was in 1919. Spink County was the site of the first
season in our area of the state and local hunters made many trips to that
county where shooting hours were from sunrise to sunset with a three rooster
limit. Over the years every county in the state has had open seasons and the
bag limits have ranged from three to ten birds depending on pheasant brood
counts before each season. Out of state hunters have been a boost to the local
economy over the years. When the pheasant population was at its peak in the mid
40's, as many as 100,000 out-of-state hunters took to the South Dakota fields
in search of the ringneck. Since then pheasant numbers have declined somewhat.

Deer have been available to Edmunds County hunters for the last several years.
The first open season was Thanksgiving weekend in 1948. Jackrabbit drives are a
popular wintertime hunting sport, while people like to sharpen up their rusty
shooting eyes on flickertail gophers the first few sunny days of spring. The
mourning dove is now a game bird again for local hunters, while fox, mink,
coons, badgers and other fur bearers are either hunted or trapped for their
pelts.

Several area dams have provided local fishing since their construction. The
most notable are Lake Parmley, more commonly known as Mina, Loyalton Dam,
Rosette Dam, Hamak Dam and Perch Lake. Most of these have been subject to winter
kill at one time or another, but the Game and Fish Department has always
carried out an active stocking program. Scatterwood Lakes, natural twin lakes
that are more like sloughs, have produced good fishing at times, particularly
through the ice. The past several years have seen many Ipswich fishermen pass
up these local lakes in favor of the Oahe Reservoir which has produced
excellent northern pike fishing and more lately, some of the best walleye
fishing in the nation.

Baseball

Information about baseball in Ipswich's early years is scanty. A pretty fair
local team was formed around the turn of the century. It lasted for several
years. Players on that team included George Llewellyn, Levi J. Thomas, Lou
Engler, Gilbert Roberts, Lynn Calkins, George Reese, Bert Engler, Ed D.D. Jones
and Ed Engler with L.F. Silverthorn as manager.

Ipswich fielded a professional team of sorts in 1909. Lou Engler was the only
local player to make that team which went around the state challenging all
comers. Playing around 40 games and winning over two-thirds of them, they were
proudly called "our champions" in the local newspaper. Aberdeen's team, which
featured a couple of ex-major leaguers, decided to challenge the champs one
more time. The local paper described what happened:

"The Ipswich ball team went to Aberdeen Tuesday to play two games with
Aberdeen, but at the game Tuesday afternoon a lot of tin horn hoodlums led by
the captain of the Aberdeen team started a riot, when their own umpire gave a
decision they didn't approve of and about one hundred of them started to mob
the umpire. The captain of the Ipswich team and police took the umpire away
from the mob and after about twenty minutes of rowdyism the game was finished
with a mob of boozy bullies along the lines threatening the umpire with a
pounding if Aberdeen lost the game."

        Ipswich baseball team in 1903. George Lewellyn, L. F. Silverthorn, L.
        J. Thomas, Lou Engler, Gilbert Roberts, L. Calkins, George Reese, Bert
        Engler, Ed Jones and Ed Engler.

        1948 baseball team that went to State Tournament. Back row, left to
        right: Marvin Bruce, Atlas Wendt, Don Petersen, Bill Bloemendaal, Fred
        Goodspeed, Manager, Stu Holdbusen, Victor Schlepp, Milbert "Shorty"
        Lammle. Middle row left to right: Johnny Cunningham, Loren Behselich,
        Eugene "Shorty" Pfaff, Len Engler, Gerald Davis, Pat Tracy. Front raw
        left to right: Leonard Hammrich, Albert Olson, Andy Kraft, Lawrence
        "Hip" Hammrich.

The Ipswich team decided it would never play Aberdeen again, but the team
disbanded anyway so the players could work the harvest.

J.S. Holdhusen pitched professional ball for Seattle, Spokane and Twin Falls in
1940. His professional baseball career was cut short when he entered the
service in 1941.

Amateur baseball teams were fielded in Ipswich off and on up through 1970.
Ipswich put together a team in the late summer of 1945 and for the next several
seasons its team participated in the Edmunds County Baseball League. Ipswich
won the league playoffs many times and has had entrants in the state amateur
baseball tourney.

When amateur baseball started to die locally in the mid '60's, youth baseball
programs were developed. The Teener team won its regional tourney in 1974 and
was third at the state tourney. Fritz Heinz was their coach. Elstad-Archer Post
67 organized a Junior Legion baseball team in 1960. Local teams waited until
1974 to win a district tournament, but were defeated in the regional
tournament. They went to the State tournament in 1982 and were defeated by
Tabor and Gregory in the double elimination tourney. Fritz Heinz is their
coach. Players on the 1982 Legion baseball team included Al Gauer, Mitch Olson,
Ted Hammrich, Rick Weber, Dave Palmer, Jeff Omland, Clark Thares, John Gillick,
Mike Heinz, Mark Rahrick, John Scarrott, Tommy Lewis and Danny Hodson.

        J. S. "Stu" Holdhusen pitched professional baseball for Seattle,
        Spokane and Twin Falls in 1940.

Football

Football was introduced at Ipswich High School in the fall of 1910 under the
coaching of Mr. Frank Jones of Groton. The local boys had the look of a
football team with new uniforms and helmets, but they were lacking the
fundamentals when they took to the field for the first time against Britton and
were soundly beaten. Players on that first team included Don Herrick, Leon
Prescott, Earle Meadows, Willard Bruce, Willie Chase, Leo Auchampach, Warren
Fish, Glenn Jackson, Buster Gentle, Charles Higel and Herb Crissman.

        Football team of 1920. Back raw: Dave Hartzell, coach. Emmett Mead,
        Ralph Aird, Oscar Brunkow, Donald Thomas, Ray Perry. Front row: Melvin
        Craven, Edward Conrad, Robert Mohr, George Coulter, Francis Fish, Henry
        Mohr, Earl Carlson. Back row standing left to right: R. M. "Bus"
        Walseth, coach, Ray Lent, Marvin Schuelke. Les Hodson, Stu Holdhusen,
        Sam McClain, Lawrence Preach Walz, Art Caborn, Earle Hagen, Alfred
        'Bud' Breen, Harrison Hartzell, Leo "Fat" Walz, Albert 'Smokey'
        DeNomme, Harold Frieborg, Vernon Bud Evald, Don Finley, Delbert
        Williams, Ken Williams, Alden Berner George Weninger, Parke Woodworth,
        Burton Zahalka, Loren Caborn, Francis Leahy, Leonard Bud Woodworth, Tom
        Archer, Bob Childers, Lester Jones, Howard Lester, Assistant Coach.
        Front row kneeling left to right: Everett Williams, Clarke Briggs,
        Gordon Thomas, Len Engler, Henry "Bronk" Kramer, Bob Aldridge, Henry
        'Firp' Scheele, Merlin Salter, Ernie Walz, Les Haugen. 1933 undefeated
        football team

By 1923 Ipswich football fortunes had improved enough to produce an undefeated
season. The 1932 team compiled an impressive record with Clarence Pierce as
quarterback. The team scored a 7-0 record in 1941 and the 1949 Tigers were a
potent eleven with Lefty Engelbritson as their coach. The 1959 team under Coach
Vern McKee gained a three-way share of the championship in the tough Central
Conference.

Football records took a nosedive in the early '60's as Ipswich teams found it
hard to compete against larger Central Conference schools. As a result Ipswich
left the conference in all sports and became a member of a newly formed six-team
Northern Conference in 1965.

Twenty straight football games failed to produce a win for Ipswich over a
four-season span in the early '70's, but a first year coach Rick Wahl righted
the Tigers in mid-season of 1974 and guided them to a share of the Northern
Conference title with Northwestern. Ipswich improved to a 6-2 record overall
and sole ownership of the Northern Conference championship the following season
under Coach Wahl. Mike Buckles came to coach in 1977 and guided Ipswich to
another Northern Conference title with a 5-1 record.

Faced with declining enrollments, Ipswich went to nine-man football. Ipswich
again found some tough sledding, but its present coach, Don Hotchkiss molded
the 1981 Tigers into Northern Conference champions in the final year of the
conference's existence. Ipswich was 6-2 overall that season, but missed a berth
in the first state football play-offs. The team also finished second behind
Faulkton in the newly formed North Central Athletic Conference that fall.

Basketball

Basketball came to Ipswich during the 1922-23 school year. Coaches Selby and
Lund came up with a starting five consisting of Lester Coulter, Dwight
Doolittle, John Cochran, Harold Bowers and Howard "Shorty" Lass. Subs were Eddie
Williams, Edwin Herrick, Almond Hall and Algeroy Reese. Ipswich took second
place in a six team tournament in Aberdeen to close the season.

        Basketball team of 1933-34. Back row left to right: Coach Howard
        Lester, Vernon Evald, Leo Walz, Gordon Thomas. Front row left to right:
        Robert Aldrich, Leonard Engler, Stuart Holdbusen, Henry (Bronk) Cramer
        and Ernie Walz.

        Pictured left to right are the 1952 State B basketball champions from
        Ipswich as they appeared 31 years ago: Gerald Sanborn, LeRoy Tuscher,
        Dale Fredrickson, Leo Hammrich, Roger Mohr, David Germain, Del Nelson,
        Delanus Mitzel, Harvey Hammrich and LeRoy Wolf.

The 1952 state champion Tigers under Coach Lefty Engebritson were the classiest
I.H.S. basketball team to date. They defeated Leola and Agar in the district
and region and defeated Hayti in the final game of the "Big B" to win the
championship. Players included LeRoy Wolf, Delanus Mitzel, David Germain, Roger
