Mohr, Leo Hammrich, Del Nelson, Dale Fredrickson, Harvey Hammrich, LeRoy
Tuscher and Gerald Sanborn.

        1977 Girls Basketball Team. Back row left to right: Student manager, Pam
        Lacher, Judy Crissman, Cherie Gillick, Lori Williams, Desiree Reil,
        Becky Frybarger, Lori Weber, Kristi Marquette and Coach Ed Miller.
        Front raw: Cherie Heinz, Janel Steinhoff, Rona Reinschmidt, Stacey
        Omland, JoAnne Heinz and Lori Taylor.

Ipswich had earlier won trips to state tourneys in 1930, 1934 and 1941. The 1941
team finished second in the State B at Aberdeen losing the title game to
Alcester. Harold Walz set a championship bracket scoring record of 61 points
which stood for many years. Members of that team were Walz, Foster Pierce,
Fritz Schumacher, Douglas Hickman, Johnny Harris, and sub Veryl Wilcox, LeRoy
Streit, LeRoy Dehnert, Ed Beebe and John Klein.

In 1976, the Tigers under Coach Dave Bauer defeated Leola in the District
finals and Mobridge in the Region finals to go to the State B again where they
finished fourth. Players were Lenny Braun, Wesley Wolff, Gary Williams, Bob
Davis, Randy Hein, Barney Williams, Tim Omland, Bob Hammrich, Tim Gillick, Jim
Morgan, Terry Stoner and Mike Hammrich.

Girls' basketball returned to IHS in the fall of 1974 after a long absence. It
has grabbed its share of Ipswich basketball headlines since. The 1974 team,
coached by Melissa Cook posted a 9-0 record. Members of that team were Becky
Klein, Cindy Marko, Cheryl Steinhoff, Cindy Braun, Joyce Oster, Roxie Williams,
Lina Holsing, Sherri KitchelI and Cheryl Thares. Statewide tournament
competition was organized the following season with the girls format identical
to the boys' except they play in the fall with the state tournament ending the
season in early December.

Ed Miller took the helm of the Ipswich girls' varsity basketball program in
1975 and has coached it continuously up through the present time. His teams
have compiled a 134-28 record through the 1981 season, and have not lost a
district tourney through the time of this writing. They won their first Region
2 title in 1977 by defeating Cresbard in the finals. Ipswich made their first
trip to the State B in Huron where they were defeated by a tall Beresford five
in their tourney opener. Desiree Reil, JoAnn Heinz, Becky Frybarger, Rona
Reinschmidt, Lori Taylor, Stacy Omland, Cheri Gillick and Lori Weber were the
top players on the team. An injury prevented Weber from playing in the
post-season tournaments.

The 1981 girls went all the way to the state championship game before falling
to Castlewood and bringing home the state runner-up trophy. Ipswich finished
the season with a sparkling 23-2 overall record. Starters and top reserves
included Lenae Reinschmidt, Julie Heinz, Carol Clemen, Lori Reinschmidt, Terry
Williams, Mary Ann Heinz, Susie Weber, Carmen Kolb, Theresa Heinz and Julie
Gauer.

Before leaving the subject of IHS basketball, a futility record should be
mentioned. Ipswich Tiger basketball teams lost 38 straight games spanning three
seasons in the early '60's. Ipswich finally ended the long drought five games
into the 63-64 season with a win over Garden City in a Northern State College
basketball preliminary in the Aberdeen Civic Arena.

Bowling

Ipswich bowlers had an opportunity to bowl locally for a few years in the
1960's. Otherwise they have had to compete in Aberdeen leagues. Ipswich has had
bowling teams in the Huff and Puff League in Aberdeen continuously for years.
Many bowlers from Ipswich went to the Legion Lanes in Roscoe during the winter
of 1962.

In November, 1963 the Linden Lanes opened in Ipswich. On opening day, Clarence
Linden, proprietor, who had never bowled in his life, rolled the first ball and
surprisingly scored a strike. Starting January 1, 1964, Bryce Herrick managed
the lanes until December 1969 and Ipswich took pride that it had six of the
best alley surfaces in the state.

During the first league season Bert Voegele rolled the highest series in
league, a 636 and Ralph Bebermeyer the highest single game of 259. The first
men's tourney in 1963 drew 134 bowlers. The first women's tourney was held in
April, 1964. Mrs. Art (Bessie) Smith generated excitement when she rolled a
single game high of 213 for the winners and led the 66 Cafe to the team title
in the tourney.

While the lanes were in operation here, Mrs. Robert (Gleva) Kitchell had the
distinction of rolling the highest single game in league play, a 276. In open
bowling, the late Ernest Friedrichsen had a 290 game for the best single score
on Linden Lanes.

An international bowling match between Ipswich, South Dakota and Ipswich,
England took place March 1, 1964. The match began simultaneously in the two
cities, 4:00 p.m. locally and at 10:00 p.m. in England. Each city had two
teams. The results were compared via the telephone line. They showed a
5867-5724 win for the local bowlers. Stu Holdhusen rolled a single game high of
213 for the winners and Lee Hopkins added a 211. The Ipswich, South Dakota
teams were awarded ten English pounds for the win over their British
counterparts.

        Top: Bert Voegele, Ralph Bebermeyer, J. Stuart Holdhusen, Darold Owens
        and Bryce Herrick. Bottom: Earle Swanson and Butch Henderson. Henderson
        and Bebermeyer were alternates.

        Morris Dehnert, Lee Hopkins, Bob Owens, Walter Brick and Tony Thares.

The local bowlers won the international match two more years before the English
team beat them in 1967. The crew from Linden Lanes avenged that loss with
another win in 1968, the last time the match was held.

Linden Lanes closed down permanently in December of 1969 and Ipswich bowlers
were once again forced to return to Aberdeen for their bowling opportunities.

Wrestling

Wrestling was added to the Ipswich High School sports curriculum during the
1967-68 school term with Marv Seyer as coach. The 1970-71 school year was
probably the most exciting for Ipswich wrestling fans. They were coached by Ed
Miller and their only loss was to number one ranked Faulkton. Coach Ray
Geditz's 1976 team was also outstanding with a 9-1 dual meet record. Over the
years Ipswich has qualified several wrestlers for state tournaments. Bob
Taylor brought home a championship trophy in 1971 and Curt Clemen in 1976.

        Bob Taylor, 1971 105-pound state B champion, cradles his rival's head
        in a headlock as he attempts a pin.

Ipswich has developed quite a number of its wrestlers through the AAU youth
wrestling program which follows the high school wrestling season. They finished
third in the 1973 AAU State tourney. Ten local youth wrestlers qualified again
the next year for the national western regional AAU championships in California
and four returned home with medals.

Track and Cross Country

Track and field competition was organized at Ipswich High School in 1927 after
earlier attempts at establishing the sport had failed. Standout teams in recent
years include the 1966 team which won the region and qualified seven boys for
the state meet. Jim Lass was the state high jump champion that year.

The Tigers sent several boys to the state meet each May during the mid '70's.
Lenny Braun brought home a first-place medal in the 880-yard run in 1976. Blake
Hoffman led his 1978 team to a sixth-place finish in the state, capturing firsts
in the mile-run and the two mile run to cap an undefeated season in both
events.

Hoffman, also, had an undefeated cross country season the previous fall. He was
the brightest IHS star in the demanding sport which was introduced to the
Ipswich sports curriculum in 1969.

Girls' track came to Ipswich in 1972 with Mrs. Theresa Hoff coaching that first
modern-day team of 29 girls. The 1982 girls' team has probably been the class
of all teams to date. The girls won several meets during the season and won the
regional title over several larger schools. Nine girls, led by Lenae
Reinschmidt's record breaking first place in the metric mile run, captured 22
points and fourth place in the team standings at the state meet. Bill
Reinschmidt returned to coaching here in 1980.

        Curt Clemen, 1976 105-pound state wrestling champion.

        Blake Hoffman

Softball

Softball has always been a popular summer recreation in Ipswich. Fast pitch
dominated the local scene for years, and city leagues included local teams such
as Powell, Craven, the Foresters, Co-op, Taylors, the American Legion and
others. Neighboring communities such as Mina, Loyalton, Tolstoy, Roscoe and
Hosmer fielded teams for Ipswich fast pitch leagues.

One interesting game that some people might recall was played in 1960 between
the Co-op Oilers and Taylors. Co-op won the game 35-26. it was probably one of
the longest softball games ever played in Ipswich, as the final putout wasn't
made until nearly midnight.

An exhibition slow pitch game between the Ipswich Jaycees and the American
Legion in 1967 foreshadowed the present softball picture in Ipswich. Slow pitch
softball has come to the forefront here as it has elsewhere. Ipswich has had a
slowpitch league for several years.

The Ipswich Bad News Bears, a high school girls' slow pitch team, has had
several good seasons, including a fourth place showing in a 32 team field at
the 1978 State Women's Slow Pitch tourney in Sioux Falls.

        Barb Hein, a member of the Bad News Bears girls' softball team, slides
        into second base during a local tournament.

1982: Rare Double

Last year, 1982, was a good one for Ipswich school aged athletes. They had two
teams make it all the way to state tournaments.

The 1982 Junior Legion baseball team, mentioned earlier, won its first regional
championship and a berth in the State B tourney in Gregory with power hitting
and clutch pitching.

        1982 Ipswich Legion baseball team. Standing left to right: Coach Fritz
        Heinz, Dave Palmer, Jeff Omland, Clark Thares, John Gillick, Mike
        Heinz, Mark Rahrich. John Scarrott and assistant coach Doug Heinz.
        Players kneeling left to right: Al Gauer, Mitch Olson, Ted Hammrich and
        Rick Weber. Batboys are Brian Morgan, Brad Lacher and Jimmy Kolker.

The 1982 girls' high school basketball team, not to be outdone, represented
Region 2 in the State B tournament in Watertown. It was the second straight
year for Coach Ed Miller and his girls to play in the "Big B".

The Tigers rewrote the IHS record book, setting nine new team and individual
marks. They averaged nearly 65 points per game, while allowing their opponents
only 47. They compiled an impressive 24-1 record winning 22 straight regular
season games.

Their lone loss came from top ranked Armour in a first round game of the state
tournament.

The Ipswich girls, who were ranked third in the state poll nearly all season,
rebounded quickly from their loss and won their final two games to claim the
consolation championship.

        1982 IHS girls basketball team. Back row left to right: Mickey
        Williams, Lori Bobby, Tracy Walter, Julie Gauer, Barb Hein, Theresa
        Heinz, Lisa Penfield, student manager Shirley Heinz, Julie Seyer. Front
        row left to right: Dawn Lincoln, Mary Ann Heinz, Julie Heinz, Carol
        Clemen.

Julie Heinz was the individual leader and floor general of the 1982 Tigers. The
5-6 senior guard landed a spot on the Class B all-state first five basketball
team for her fine season. She was also named to the State B all-tourney teams
in both 1981 and 1982.

She was one of six finalists for the 1982 Miss Basketball in South Dakota
award, sponsored by the Hansen-Anderson Basketball Promotion.

Heinz averaged 14 points per game her senior year with five assists and five
steals. She sank 14 of 17 free throw attempts in the 1982 state B tournament.
She was also her team's defensive ace.

        Julie Heinz

Outstanding Athlete Award

In 1972 an Outstanding Senior Athlete Award was initiated at Ipswich High
School. It was developed to give recognition to the athlete that had
contributed the most in his or her four years at IHS. It was also an extra
incentive for seniors to compete in track their final season.

Mark Wietgrefe was the recipient of that first award in 1972. Among his
accomplishments in football, basketball and track was a second place finish at
167 pounds in the State B wrestling tourney his senior year.

The 1973 award resulted in a tie between Tom Fischer and Earl Steinhoff.
Fischer was an all conference pick in basketball and football two years and
also competed in track.

Steinhoff was a steady performer in football, basketball and track.

Dale Stoner won the award in 1974. He was an all conference football player and
competed in two state track meets. He also lettered in wrestling.

Todd Oster was the Outstanding Senior Athlete in 1975. He was a four year
football regular, competed in two state track meets and developed into a good
basketball player.

The 1976 award went to Tim Omland, who was a third team all state basketball
player his senior year when his team went to the state tourney. A four year
starter in basketball, he also starred in football and track. He was twice
picked to all conference football teams. He competed in two state track meets.

Lenny Braun won the award in 1977. He played in four different sports, but
excelled as a runner. He went to four state cross country meets. He was a
winner of the 880-yard run in the state meet as a junior.

Another ace runner, Blake Hoffman won the 1978 award. He competed in three
state cross country meets and three state track meets including a first place
in the state A mile run his senior year. He was also the state Class A record
setting cross country champion that fall.

Neil Wietgrefe received the award in 1979. He was a four year varsity wrestler,
going to the state meets his junior and senior seasons. He won a sixth place
medal for 145 pounders his senior year.

Doug Heinz won the award in 1980. He was an all conference football player his
senior season. He also competed in track and in basketball where he was an
outstanding defensive player his senior season.

The first lady recipient of the award was Stacey Omland. She was a four year
varsity basketball player including the 1977 state tournament entry. She also
participated in two state track meets.

John Gillick and Lenae Reinschmidt shared the award in 1982. Gillick was a
third team all state basketball player as a senior. He started all four years
in basketball and his last three in football. There he was twice
all-conference. He also competed in two state track meets.

Reinschmidt went to state track meets six different years. She was state
champion miler as a sophomore and as a senior, when she set a state class A
metric mile record. She also went to two state cross country meets winning first
place as a sophomore.

She was a third team all state basketball player her senior year when she
guided the Tigers to a runner-up spot in the State B tournament. There she was
an all tourney pick.

        Mike Steen beside the Street Stock Class race car in which he races at
        Brown County Raceway in Aberdeen. In 1982, he won the Brown County Fair
        Feature race, Championship race and finished second in point standings.
        The car is owned by Doug and Les Holland, Sr. and the pit crew was Rick
        Holland, Bob Gillick and Paul Doeling.

                OUR RURAL AREA

Adrian Township

Adrian Township, 124-69, is located in the central part of Edmunds County. Ed
Jackson, an early settler, named it "Adrian" for a township in Michigan by that
name. It was organized sometime between 1898 and 1902. Others who helped with
the organization were: Ed Cochran, George Swalve, Carl Sanborn and James
Champlin.

Early pioneers also included: John J. Kimmitt, Mr. Boe, John Peterson, Joseph
Brown, Helen Eggleston, H.T. Laper, Tom W. Jones, Lockwood Smith, Maude Ames,
Champlin, Joe and Will Kimmitt, Mr. Rowland, John Borgan, Arthur Zempel, Carl
Sanborn, Norman Howe, L. Belle Morehouse, Katherine Brennen, Frank and Joe
Edgington, and Charles Peterson.

        Mrs. Sanborn, Delmar and Alice, 1918

Gertrude Beebe Mears, Mrs. Ed Jackson and Ed Cochran were the early school
teachers. The first schoolhouse was located two miles west and half a mile
north of the Jackson ranch. There were no churches built, but Sunday School was
sometimes held in the homes or in the schools.

In the past twenty-five years there have been several changes in land
ownership. But Adrian is still full of rocks, too many blizzards, too many
grasshoppers, and still an occasional prairie fire. We have only eight families
remaining in the township. Below is a brief history of the families residing in
the township at the present time.

George Swalve settled in Adrian in the early 1890's. He bought some of the land
and homesteaded some in 1908. As time went on, George and his son, George R.
Swalve, bought additional land. George R. Swalve continued the ranch until the
time of his death on October 14, 1974. Since that time his son, Robert, and Ann
Swalve have continued the operation. Robert and Debra have a son, Dustin, who
is the fourth generation of the Swalve family to live on the ranch.

Edwin and Alvinia Mahoney moved to Adrian in the early 1930's. Since the death
of Edwin, Alvinia and their son, Glen, have continued to own and operate the
farm. Alvinia now lives in Ipswich, and Glen and his wife live on the farm.

Mr. and Mrs. Don Lincoln now live on the farm that Clarence and Leon Zempel
lived on for many years. This ranch has recently been purchased by Dr. John
McFee, Jr. Clarence Zempel passed away in 1973, and Leone now resides in
Ipswich.

Tom Kolker owns the farm previously owned by Henry Schneider. Henry and his
wife now reside in Ipswich.

Mr. and Mrs. Robert Beyers now own and operate the farm they purchased from
Edward Fischer. Ed Fischer and his wife, Nelda, now live in Roscoe.

Mr. and Mrs. Marvin Pietz now own and operate the former Charles Peterson farm.


Mr. and Mrs. Loren Johnson also own and operate their farming and ranching
operation. They purchased their farm from Jake Klein in 1958.

Mr. and Mrs. Lloyd Taylor also own and still operate their ranch. As of this
writing, Lloyd Taylor is the oldest resident in Adrian Township. Taylors moved
to Adrian in 1936 and purchased the ranch from Art Silver.

Others who have resided in Adrian and moved away include the John Grace family
and the Phil Clemen family. Richard Russell of Aberdeen now owns and operates
these two ranches. Gordon Koepenick still owns his ranch, but now lives in
Aberdeen. Gordon and his family have owned and operated this ranch since the
early 1920's. There was also the Hamak Brothers Ranch now owned by Chet
Gullickson of rural Bath.

The Adrian Township rural school closed its doors in 1963 at which time the
township was split between the Roscoe and Ipswich School Districts.
                Ann Swalve

Belle Township

Belle Township got its name from Belle Slocum who later married L.E. Evans. She
and her brother were the children of Mr. and Mrs. M.A. Slocum who lived 5 1/2
miles north and one mile west of Craven. Some of the buildings are still there.
Mr. Slocum was one of the first assessors.

One of the first telephones was installed on a barbed wire fence between two
neighbors who used to play checkers over the wire in an evening.

There were 39 pupils in the North Belle school one of the earlier years with
Donald Rogers as teacher. The families represented were Engle, Reed, Fessenden,
Krambeal, Bales, Zelenka, Hall, Konopasek, Krueger, Johnson, Morgan, Ruenzel,
Curry and Neihaus. One of these still living in Ipswich is Wavie (Hall)
Krueger. In 1909 the teacher was Miss Ellen M. Bray in the North Belle School.
She had 33 pupils. The school board members were W.H. Fessenden, A. Krambeal
and Jacob Fischer.

        North Belle School 1948.

The schools in those days were quite different. They were all one room with an
entry, about 24 feet long and 10 feet wide, with a coal heater to keep it warm.
In the warmer part of the season, the coats and dinner pails (usually syrup
pails) were left out in the entry; but in the winter all of this had to be up
by the stove, including overshoes so they would be warm. The children generally
walked, rode horse back or came by horse and buggy. When the snow was deep they
came by horse and sleigh. Teachers roomed with some of the parents and had to
pay board and room out of their $40.00 per month salary. The teachers had to
get to school early in the morning to get the fire started so the building
would be warm by nine when school started. Wood and coal were kept by the stove
with a larger supply in a shed. Some brought a cream can of water from home.
Two children would be on a water committee, and it would be their duty to keep
water there. The water sat in an open pail with a dipper and all drank out of
the same dipper. In later years they bought a water cooler with a faucet.

        Lizzie Ruenzel, Mrs. Leo Krueger and children, Viola, Irene, Walter and
        Rosella.

Committees were appointed to different chores at school. One was to take the
erasers outside and pound them to get the chalk out.

Programs were held on Halloween and Christmas. Some times a pie social or box
social was held where they auctioned off the pies or boxes to raise a little
money to buy a few things for the school. Often they would have spell-downs.
Several times a year there would be a baseball game between the two schools.
Some of the games played at school were Pump, Pump, Pull-away, Hide and Seek,
Last couple out, Kick the can, and in the winter Fox and Goose. The school
districts were reorganized in 1964, and the children are now bused to the
Ipswich schools.

        Nora Ruenzel and Lillian Krueger

        The Stephan Hammrich family

Horses were used in those days to do the farm work so every farmer owned a lot
of horses. Each morning before breakfast the horses had to be fed and the cows
milked. Then the breakfast would usually consist of potatoes, eggs, and cooked
cereal. There was not much cold cereal; if any, Corn Flakes or Shredded Wheat.
Coffee beans were ground at home. Then after a day in the fields, they came
home to evening chores which included feeding and watering the pigs, usually
carrying the wafer to them or hauling it on a stone boat in a barrel. In the
very early days some had to haul their water and drive their cattle to the
neighbors for a drink. Every farmer seemed to have horses, cows, pigs,
chickens, geese, ducks and turkeys. They also had big gardens with lots of
potatoes. They canned vegetables for winter, and put carrots and rutabagas in
big cans of sand. When they butchered the meat would have to be canned because
they did not have refrigerators or freezers. Some pork was fried and put in
crock jars with lard over the top to keep it, and some was put in a salt brine
to keep. There wasn't much money so the eggs, cream, butter and cottage cheese
were sold at the store to buy the groceries and other necessities.

Dr. Hill and Dr. White made house calls to the sick people in the township.
Mrs. Ford Ayres, who lived nine miles north and two and one-half miles east of
Craven, would help them. When babies were born there was a midwife in the
neighborhood who would take over.

For entertainment they used to have barn dances which would last until the
early morning hours with some from the community playing the music. Often six
families would join together for a whist club and meet every Friday evening,
going from place to place, taking the family with them. Once a group of the
young folks put on a three act play at the schoolhouse, going to practice in
the winter with team and sled for four miles or so. The women in the
neighborhood would go from place to place quilting quilts. Families would go up
to the Elm River to pick choke cherries to make jell. I remember being told of
a family who had gone visiting in the winter. When they got their team and sled
ready to go home it was blizzarding. They started for home but couldn't see
where they were going so finally just let the horses go the way they wanted and
the horses took them home.

Electricity was brought to Belle Township in 1949. Present residents of Belle
Township are the families of Jr. Hammrich, Cap Malsom, Andrew Scheele, Les
Dutenhoeffer, Frank Heinz Sr., Frank Heinz Jr., Gerald Noble, Val Malsom and the
Deerfield Hutterite Colony.

We must give the pioneers credit for all the hard times they lived through.
                Irene Holm

Cleveland Township

        Victor and Christina Strom

Victor and Christina Strom both homesteaded in Cleveland Township in 1885. They
had immigrated from Sweden with their oldest son, Gustav. Their first home was
in Belle Township where their oldest daughter was born. Two sons, John and
Frank, were born on the homestead in Cleveland Township. Frank died at age one
and one-half and was buried on the homestead. Their sod house was built on the
line that divided the two homestead quarters.

The Stroms had a horse and an oxen to start their farming. Pioneers that they
were, they survived the hardships of droughts, prairie fires and blizzards. My
grandmother told of seeing five buffalo about one-half mile north of the
homestead. They also told of Indian scares, but they weren't afraid so they
didn't leave their homestead. During the winters, grandfather twisted hay for
heat. During the blizzard of 1888, grandfather twisted hay for three days.

Elizabeth became a teacher. She taught in Cleveland Township and in Liberty
Township. Mary Stulc Kadlec, Mary Kub, John and Jenny Sindelar were some of her
pupils. These people all live in Edmunds County.

In 1910 Victor and Christina moved to Ipswich and their son, Gustav, and wife,
Emma, took over the family farm. They had six children: Neva, Ernest, Vivian,
Edward, Leonard and Glen.

Elizabeth married Harry Bailey in 1910. They had three children: Walter, Roy,
and Stella. In 1944 the Baileys moved to the Victor Strom homestead. Walter and
Ardis and Roy and Myrtle still operate the Strom homestead. Stella married and
lives in Faulk County.

Albert Kub homesteaded in Cleveland Township. He married Marie Staska. They had
four children: Frank, Marie, John and Rose. Frank and his wife, Mabel, operated
the family farm until Frank's death. The son, Tony, and wife, Paulette, are now
operating the farm.

The parents of Charlie Simon homesteaded in Cleveland Township. Charlie and his
wife, Anna then operated the family farm until Frank's death. Their son,
Lillian, Emil, and Lucille. Emil, his wife, Ruth, and sons are living on and
operating the family homestead.

        Albert Kub

        Mrs. Albert (Mary) Kub

        John and Katherine Sindelar

John Sindelar lived in Cleveland Township for many years. He married Kate
Staska. They had eight children:

Marie, Josephine, Christine, Johnny, Albena, Ernest, Tony and Helen. Johnny and
Tony are living on the family farm.

Joe Markovetz homesteaded the Markovetz farm. Wilkes lived there for a time.
Later another Markovetz family moved to this location. Jim Markovetz farms it
now.

Other early day homesteaders known of were: Oliver Christianson, The Staskas,
the Novotnys, Vincent Stulc, Anton Stulc, the Jaquiths, two families of
Andersons, the Balattas, Emil Simon (brother of Charlie Simon), Joe Sindelar,
Vince Sindelar, Charlie Sindelar, Willie Sindelar and Jane Jones.

In later years, a farm built by the Luttons was purchased by Martin Schurr. His
grandson now operates the farm. Jerome Blanks live on an Anderson farm. Ed
Kirschenmann lives on an Anderson farm. The Picton place is still inhabited.

Charles and Anna Simon

The Steens lived in Cleveland Township for many years. In the late teens or
early twenties, John Sitter built a farm in Cleveland Township. They lived
there for many years. The Malsoms live there now. In the early twenties, Henry
Sporer built a farm. There is only a shelter belt there now. In the 1930's,
Glen Higgins started a farm which is owned now by Elmer Kirschenmann. Ben
Walker and Harold Howard had farms which are gone now. Gus Job lived in the
township for many years, and his farm is occupied at the present time.

As the township grew, three schools were built. The names of the teachers are
unknown except for Jane Jones who taught for many years. Elizabeth Strom taught
in later years, and her daughter, Stella Stevenson, taught there in the 1940's.
There are no schools remaining as the children are bused to Ipswich.

There is a cemetery on the Sindelar farm. Early day pioneers are buried there.
It is called St. John's Cemetery.

In 1915 a group of farmers from Cleveland and Montpelier Townships went
together and bought a threshing outfit. It was called the Company Rig. In 1918
Elmer Lass came to the Sindelar farm and sold them a Maxwell truck.

The years go by and much of our precious history is gone forever. This history
is written to the best of my knowledge from the stories of the old pioneers and
their descendants.
                Walter Bailey

Fountain Township

In 1884 two settlers, Theodore H. Stratten and George W. Fiddler, filed claims
in Fountain Township in SE one-quarter six and NE one-quarter ten,
respectively. At that time land was selling for as much as $4.00 an acre. The
prices were high, but it was a chance to own land and grow with the country.
That is what many thought, but unfortunately some could not endure the
hardships of the Dakota prairie and so left. Those who stayed made history and
left a legacy for their children.

There are several farms in Fountain Township with the third generation living
on them. Among those is Howard Fischer, son of Wendelin and Cecelia Fischer, on
NW one-quarter three. Howard lives in the original house built by his
grandfather, Michael Fischer.

Casper Burgod and son, Larry, farmed together on NW one-quarter eight until
Cap's death. Now it is owned by Mrs. Kate Burgod and farmed by Richard and
Bernie Burgod Henderson. Andrew D. Schumacher is the second generation on his
farm on SE one-quarter eight. A new farm home was constructed in 1942. In 1956
Adam Schumacher bought SE one-quarter twenty from Josephine Fehr and William
Langen, and some buildings have been moved on that place.

The late Jack Gauer was also a third generation on his farm, SE one-quarter
twenty-two. His wife, Henrietta, now owns the farm that is operated by her son
Bruce. Their farmstead lies beside Highway 12, the Yellowstone Trail. Jack was
a veteran and active in the American Legion. In 1971 Jack purchased the Ludwig
Usselman farm NW one-quarter eleven, and that is also operated by Bruce.

The Ben Kessler farm, SW one-quarter twelve, is now owned by Randolph Bauer of
Brown County. There are no buildings left on the farm, and the Kesslers reside
in Aberdeen. Their farm home was moved into Ipswich, remodeled, and now owned by
Raymond Geditz.

Daniel M. Lacher is the third generation to reside on farmstead NE one-quarter
fifteen. A new home was built in 1962 replacing the original house which was
sold to Spring Creek Hutterite Colony at Forbes, N.D. Like his father and
grandfather, Danny is active in community affairs, among them township clerk,
member of the Ipswich Community Hospital board, and the Edmunds County weed
board.

Mrs. Walter Schumacher is the owner of NW one-quarter eighteen. Lester Jones
and then Pius Gauer were previous owners. All buildings are now gone, and Pius
lives in Ipswich.

In 1951 Peter Krokosh came to the United States from Canada, and in 1960 bought
40 acres from Jacob Gauer in SW one-quarter nineteen. This was previously owned
by D.D. Jones and then John W. Dosch. In 1975 a Wausau home was constructed to
replace the original house.

Richard Engle was the third generation living on the NW one-quarter twenty for
many years. His grandfather, C.H. Rebrud was the original owner. In 1982 the
farm was sold to Allen and Gary Braun who also operate the Richard C. Braun
farm on the SE one-quarter fifteen. Allen is a township supervisor, and Gary is
on the Ipswich Farmers Elevator board.

Joseph A. Braun was also the third generation on his farm. His father, August,
had been a carpenter along with his farming operation. Joe lives in Ipswich,
owns and operates a school bus. Jack Braun lives on SW one-quarter twenty-seven.
He is actively farming and is well known for his mechanical work and skill at
inventing.

Frank and Armella Braun bought their farm, NE one-quarter twenty-two, from the
Baer estate in 1947 and built a new home in 1954.

A second generation now farming NE one-quarter twenty-four is Mike Dosch who
lives in Aberdeen. Joe and Regina Dosch have lived on NE one-quarter
twenty-three all their married life. Regina teaches school at the Plainview
Hutterite Colony north of Ipswich.

H. H. Hoffman owns the O.R. Hales farm, another vacant farmstead. Dave Hales
built a home on NW one-quarter twenty-nine which is now owned by Tom and Vicky
Kitchell.

Duane Ertz is the third generation living on NW one-quarter thirty-two. He
bought the farm in 1964 from Mrs. George Ertz. The original house is still
there but has been remodeled to accommodate the changing needs of a family.
Duane has a custom business along with his farming operation. He is a Korean
war veteran and is active in community affairs, among them being township
supervisor.

        School-family picnic at Fountain Township in 1928. Eva Ertz was the
        teacher in first row left. Families are Will Engle, Adam Braun, Frank
        Van Kirk, Ellis, Kramer and Pugh.

NE one-quarter thirty-three had many owners, but in 1950 Cliff Ragels purchased
the farm from Joy and John Clark. It is now owned by Edwin and Gayle Ragels
Job. Lloyd Larson owned SE one-quarter 33 until 1960 when he sold to Loretta
and the late Francis Gauer. Loretta still resides on the farm and is married to
William Reinschmidt who teaches at Ipswich Public School.

Harold Artz is the present owner of NE one-quarter thirty-four and also owns
Artz Equipment in Aberdeen. Jerry and Judy Adler purchased their farm NW
one-quarter four from their father, Jerome Adler. Joseph and Marie Dutenhoeffer
still farm and reside on NE one-quarter twelve.

A dairy farm along with land farming is carried on by Stan and Diane Malsom and
sons on NW one-quarter one. They bought the farm in 1962 from Joe and Elizabeth
Malsom.

In 1912, Craven had a grocery store, lumber yard, blacksmith shop, post office,
saloon and elevator. The Craven elevator merged with the Ipswich Farmers
Elevator in 1970. The present manager is T. J. Christopherson.

The other businesses have closed. Oscar Schumacher built a night club at Craven
Corner in 1950. It is now owned by Richard Braun. Oscar and his wife,
Marveline, live in a mobile home on NW one-quarter twenty-one.

In 1905 the people of the Catholic faith of Fountain and bordering townships
built the New Strassburg Church. Even though the church is no longer in
existence since 1950, the cemetery has been kept up by several farm families in
Belle and Fountain townships.

        Fountain Township School Program, 1900

We must not forget to mention our country schools and what happened to them.
Some of the early teachers were Catherine Hirch, Caroline Rebrud, Mr. Taylor,
Lilly Jones, Eva Brossart. The four schools were closed in 1960. Ben and Louis
Thares owned a school bus and were hired to bus the children to the Ipswich
city schools. In 1963 the Fountain Township School District No. 15 consolidated
with the Ipswich Independent School District No. 42. At that time Delores
Hales, Daniel Lacher and Duane Ertz were on the Fountain Township school board.
The school buildings were sold to Fountain residents to be used as granaries.

This brings us to our Centennial celebration in June, 1983. Without a doubt,
progress has left a deep mark on our land. There are eight farms with empty
buildings or no buildings at all. The price of land is in excess of $300.00 an
acre. On the bright side, we have many farms with either a second or third
generation on them. New homes have been built and others remodeled, farmsteads
have taken on a new look. Whatever the future holds for Fountain Township, the
good people who live there will take it in stride and keep right on making
history.
                Duane and Sylvia Ertz

Harmony Township

The pioneers of Harmony Township came early in the spring of 1883. The majority
of the people came from Wisconsin by rail as far as Aberdeen. Wagons drawn by
oxen and horses were used for the rest of the journey west. Harmony was settled
by a variety of nationalities: Welsh, German, Irish, English and others. The
summer of 1883 was a busy one with breaking a little sod, planting small
patches for food, building a shanty, gathering fuel and locating water holes.

On October 7, 1883, a son was born to Martin and Theresa Blank in Harmony
Township, Section 19. They named the baby, Martin, and he was the first white
boy born in Harmony, the second in the county.

A very early letter written by Theresa Blank tells how serious the water
shortage was until an underground spring was located on Section 7. Pioneers
hauled the water from this well for 10 to 12 miles around. Later another well
was dug where Leroy Gauer now lives and another in Section 23.

On February 4, 1884, a petition was signed by 23 names asking that Township
122, Range 68 be organized as a civil township and that the name be Harmony.
The petition was granted on February 19, 1884, and the first town meeting was
held at the house of Mr. K. Kirk, NE one-quarter of Section 15. The very
earliest families included: Martin Blank, Albert Smith, D.C. Pierce, George H.
Loomis, Fred Johnson, Ezra Drew, Job Jillson, N.W. Kirk, John P. Hughes, George
McEachran, Tom Doonan, Dow E. Lewis, and others.

The first assessor book of 1891 shows the family names of Ira Dibble, T.L.
Davis, T.M. Callahan, J.W. Chase, D.D. Jones, Peter J. Evans, Lee and Roll
Loomis, Thomas Evans, John Griffin, Owen and William Rowlands, Charles E.
White, Jane Jones.

The Charles E. White family settled on Section 14. Dr. Walter White, a son,
practiced medicine many years in Ipswich. Lilla White taught school several
years in the township.

Several living in this area today can tell about the unforgettable character of
Thomas Evans, known as "Tom-Fifty". This Welsh pioneer was very popular with
all the children, teaching them Welsh songs, games and stories. He was a
bachelor and had a shanty south of the Pfaff farm.

Harmony township grew very fast the first twenty-five years. Township expenses
were mostly for organization: paying the clerk, supervisors, and overseers;
road and bridge work; plowing fire guards; erecting and maintaining guide posts.
$1.50 an acre was allowed for plowing fire guards. The supervisors received
$2.00 a day, the town clerk $1.50, and the assessor $3.00. The town treasurer
was paid a salary of $15.00 per year. In 1897 the total township expenses were
$156.15; in 1898 they were $124.89; and in 1899 they were $147.89. From the old
minute book the following was recorded: June 7, 1897, T.M. Callahan gave the
report that the board could not compel the land owner to fence the open wells.
If it was done, the town would have to meet the expense.

Family names kept changing as new ones moved into the township, sons and
daughters began taking the places of the older ones, and many became
discouraged and went back East. From 1903 on, the family names of John Pfaff,
Joseph Brady, Peter Morgan, J.C. Stolsig, R.R. Buikema, Peter Lass, J.P. Jones,
Peter Geditz, A.J, Leech, Aaron Gsell, the Streit brothers, H.B. Engel, E.E.
Hubbard, Ed D. Jones, Boon Flesher, Tom Shew and others appear in the records.

During the 1930's when one dry year after another came, and Harmony Township
was a regular Dust Bowl, the Public Works Administration came to our aid and
constructed the Blank Dam, which is located on Section 7. All local labor was
used. This gave the people some income to tide them over. When the dam was
completed, it was stocked with fish. For a time it was quite an attraction for
the ones who liked to fish.

In the fall of 1883 the County Board designated Harmony School District as No.
19. The first judges were: F.M. Wright, M.K. Kirk, and Ezra Drew. Albert Smith
was the first treasurer. The district received their first apportionment of the
General Fund of $55.11 in April of 1884.

        The Zahalka Brothers breaking sod in 1911.

There were five schools in Harmony Township at one time. Harmony Center School
was built in the early 1880's. A tar paper shack across the road from where Bob
Geditz lives now was used in the early 1900's. There was no heat so there was
no school when it was real cold. Then in 1907 a new school was built one-half
mile south of there. Another school one-half mile north of Walter Wietgrefe
called the Leech School, one across from Mike Schumacher called the Johnson
school, one and one-half mile north of Daryl Stoner called the Jones School.
There were two rural schools operating in the township in 1958 with Mrs. Harold
Markuson and Mrs. Albert Kadlec as teachers. A few years later Harmony Center
was the only school open. In the fall of 1963 Harmony Center was closed, and
all the children were bused into Ipswich. The last teacher to teach in Harmony
Center was Mrs. Helmuth Kirschenmann. The last school board members were: Ruben
Ochsner, Daryl Stoner and Walter Wietgrefe, Jr.

On July 7, 1970, a Hutterite Colony from Mitchell bought the land of Gilbert
and Allen Roberts and settled on the Gilbert Roberts farm. They moved Allen's
house over and built more homes. They are the Pembrook Colony. They have their
own school, and teachers from the Ipswich school come out to teach.

After a few meetings and discussions, the township had on election on
disorganizing the township. The last township meeting was held at Attorney
Raymond Hieb's office and then later at the Community Room of the Court House
to finalize their last business of the township. Bob Geditz and Gilbert Roberts
were supervisors: Ruben Ochsner, clerk; Mike Schumacher, treasurer.

Harmony township is one of the few townships to have irrigation. The Pembrook
Colony and Gary Gisi have irrigation systems from deep wells on their land in
this township.

A new crop has become very popular with the farmers the last few years, and
that is growing sunflowers. There are two kinds of sunflowers: the confection
flowers and oil flowers. Most people plant the oil as there is a better market
for them.

Twenty-five years ago there were twenty-four farms in Harmony township. Now
there ore sixteen families living on farms, but only eleven families actually
farming. The tradition still holds true where children are taking over the
family farms.
                Marlene Stoner

Huntley Township

Huntley Township is located west of Ipswich, S.D., Township 123, Range 69.

In the beginning, Huntley and Adrian were one township. In 1890 they became a
civil township with board members John Meyers, R.H. Christians and John
Kimmitt. The present board members are Norbert Jangula, Carl Unser, Dennis
Sauer, Gordon Kilber, treasurer, and Mark Beyers, clerk.

The price of land in 1884 was $1.25 per acre. It now averages $300.00 per acre.


There was a cheese factory located on the SE one-quarter of section 21 in 1890.
John James was the first cheesemaker.

The town of Beebe was located eight miles west of Ipswich in Huntley Township.
Some of the businesses located in Beebe were: Woodman's Hall, post office,
Schneider's Cafe, lumber yard, blacksmith shop, stock yard, cream station,
elevator, grocery store. Now all businesses are gone, but one place of
residence is there being occupied by Mr. and Mrs. Robert Hieb.

Some of the early teachers were: Ed Cochran, Mattie Lovett Hoyle, Peter J.
Brady, Myrtle House, Alma Hutchinson, Mrs. J.C. Scullen, Amy Doten, Mrs.
William Kimmitt, Clara Rettig, Ethel Coulter, Hobalt Hilton, Alin Moore, Rose
Finley, Lula Standard, Thomas Kinney and Mrs. Fred Kimmitt. The schools were
located in the NE one-quarter, Section 23, SW one-quarter, Section 8 and NW
one-quarter, Section 32. The last of these schools were closed in 1965. Some of
the pupils now go to Roscoe, and some go to Ipswich. The last schoolboard
members were: Esther Finley, Neva Kirschenmann and Irene Rissmann.

In 1958 the elevator at Beebe was moved to Ipswich and has now burned down. In
1959 the store in Beebe was closed and torn down. This was the last place of
business in Huntley Township.

        Sam and Earle Hagen in 1918.

The BBB Extension Club is still in existence, but now is a card club.

In 1959 the roads were graveled which was a great improvement.

There have been about a dozen farms vacated, leaving only the land to farm. The
present farmers located in Huntley Township are: Norbert Jangula, Oscar Zambo
and the families of Jake Volk, Kenneth Rissmann, Glen Hoyle, Gordon Kilber, Paul
Hoyle, Helmuth Kirschenmann, Richard Opp, Bruce Opp, Carl Unser, Mark Beyers,
Raymond Beyers, Dennis Sauer, Adolph Finkbeiner, Lester Finkbeiner, Art Zambo
and Art Nehlich.

An unforgettable event which took place in Huntley Township was a three day
sale in September, 1982, by Art and Oscar Mambo which consisted of antiques
which were collected by them over a number of years.

Even though many farms are vacated and gone, Huntley Township is still a
prosperous township. The roads are maintained on a regular basis.
                Irene Rissmann, Mildred Hoyle

Ipswich Township

        Early plowing scene

        The Zahalka Brothers threshing at Homewood.

        17 car train wreck at Ipswich, August 31

Ipswich Township No. 123, Range 68, was named for the home town in England of
Charles Prior, who was superintendent of the C.M. and St. Paul Railroad.

The school district was organized October 1, 1883. J.W. Parmley, the first
county superintendent of Edmunds County, was ordered to plat the school
districts. Two school houses were built in the township. One was on Section 8
and the other on Section 14. The first apportionment of $297.13 with which to
run the schools was made in February 1884 for District No. 14 to A.B. Allen,
the treasurer of Ipswich Township schools. The school building on Section 8 was
later sold to Mike Schweich to be used as a house. The school building on
Section 14 was sold to Julius Habeck for a granary. Schools were consolidated
in 1921 or 1922. Ipswich Township itself was organized on July 11, 1890.

Some of the first settlers were: Lester E. Jones, Arthur Chubbuck, James
Crawford, Samuel Sherman, A.L. Jones, Henry Briggs, Mayo Jacquith, W.P. Tate,
Lewis Chase and Joseph Brown, who was the father of Willard Brown, the first
white child born in Edmunds County.

In 1979 Tony Heier bought 16 acres one mile east and one and one-half miles
north of Ipswich in Section 14 and built a storage building for Heier's Body
Shop. Tony Hammrich bought ten acres of land from Raymond Forkel and moved his
buildings from the city of Ipswich to the southeast corner of Section 20.
Walter Schumacher bought five acres in Section 16 and built a house and
quonset. After Walter passed away, Mrs. Schumacher finished the buildings and
moved from Rosette Township.

The present families living in Ipswich Township in 1983 are - Reuben Bender,
Lester Forkel, Ed Gillick, Joe Geditz, Larry Geditz, Franklin Hammrich, Eugene
Hammrich, David Holland, Arnold Holsing, Elmer Habeck, Ivan Habeck, Louis
Jones, Donald Jones, Ronnie Kienow, A.T. Schumacher, Art Schaub, Wesley Stern,
Tony Hammrich, and Delores Schumacher.

Present township officers are Reuben Bender, Clerk; Wesley Stern, Franklin
Hammrich and Louis Jones, Supervisors; Sylvia Stern, Treasurer.

Ground breaking was held May 4, 1978, for a Topper Factory on the southeast
corner of Section 22. In March of 1982, Frontier Caps purchased Super Rope
Company of Bowdle, S. D. Super Rope, which is used mainly as a tow rope and
made of polypropylene, will be manufactured in con junction with toppers for
pickups.

Plans are being made to build a sunflower processing plant on the N one-half of
Section 25. This plant will process Sunflower seeds for oil, and the
by-products will be used as cattle feed.
                Lillian Holsing

        At the Homewood Farm in 1912.

Liberty Township

        Harvesting with header on Griffith Farm in 1910's.

        Combining in 1982 on Jack Griffith's farm.

As Ipswich celebrates its Centennial, we find residents in the township whose
ancestors homesteaded here, with members of the families having lived here
throughout all of these years.

John Griffith has the distinction of owning land that his paternal grandparents
(John and Margaret Griffith) homesteaded in 1883, and also owns land in the
same section (26) where his mother was born. The NE one-quarter 26 was
homesteaded by her parents, Oscar and Amelia Viertel. This homestead is now
owned by Owen and Richard Williams and remains the same as was built up by the
Viertels.

John and Walter Griffith reside on the SW one-quarter 26 where they lived with
their mother, Mrs. Lydia Viertel Griffith; the Griffith homestead on Sec. 23
being abandoned a few years ago.

The "Stafford" name has been in the township since its settlement and has the
distinction of some member serving on the township board almost continually
since its organization. David A. Stafford, a Civil War veteran, settled in 1884
and was elected to the first township board, succeeded by his son, Walter,
grandson, Glenn, and presently a great-grandson, Larry.

Two granddaughters of another early homesteader, Frank Hamak, reside in the
township: Mrs. Glenn (Lucille Simon) Stafford -- daughter of Anna Hamak Simon
who was born on the homestead in Sec. 18, and Mrs. Ivan (Delores Wagner)
Holsing, daughter of Mrs. Lee (Rose Hamak) Wagner.

Liberty Township is an agricultural area with diversified farming, with a
tendency toward rough land, some areas unfit for cultivation due to rock, and
best suited for wildlife and grazing. Several large natural lakes afford duck
hunting, and an artificial lake constructed under WPA in the 1930's provides
fishing facilities. In the late '40s and early '50s many shelterbelts were
planted, adding a great deal to this area.

People began migrating into this area as early as 1881. Claims may have been
filed in 1883, but records indicate that proving up did not take place until in
1886 through 1890. Willard Brown and Edmund Griffith were the first white
children born in Edmunds County, and both were born in Liberty Township.

        Walter Stafford, Della Hodson, Delbert and Ethel Hodson.

        The parents of W. A Brown lived on this ranch.

The first township organizational meeting was held at the home of Evan Jenkins,
Powell, Dakota Territory, on October 23, 1886. The first verifiable record
indicates David A. Stafford, John Getsman, William W. Thomas, C. S. Poole and
Evan Jenkins on the board. Addresses of all residents were Powell City and
Vermont City. The following were listed as personal property owners in 1891:
H.D. Andres, H.F. Barton, Joseph Brown, Thomas Clayton, John Getsman, J.H.
Griffith, Ludwig Havel, Frank Hamak, W.J. & O.O. Jones, E.J. Jenkins, Frank,
Joseph and Albert Kub, Christian Kraft, George Kuble, D. J. Morris, Jacob
Novotny, C. S. Floyd and J. A. Poole, V. Pletka, Griffith and Richard Prichard,
E. R. R. Jones, S. C., D. A. & W. M. Slattery, E. E. Stearns, Hugh Roberts, A.
J. & D. A. Stafford, J.E. Thomas, J.B. Truscott, W.W. Thomas, Oscar Viertel,
E.S. Williams, and S.E. Young.

The first school meeting recorded in 1887 indicates C.H. Roberts as treasurer
but no record of other members until in 1890's which indicate Oscar Viertel,
Mrs. E.J. Jenkins and Don Goodale on the board. Sessions were held as early as
1885, and three school buildings were erected. The Getsman school on NW
one-quarter 11, destroyed by a tornado, was rebuilt on the SE one-quarter 11.
The Viertel school on Sec. 34 was destroyed by a prairie fire in 1908. The
Goodale school was located south of the Joe Kub farm. For a time in the 1890's
school was held in a room of the Frank Hamak home, that being a central
location for all of the pupils at that time.

To complete the life of the settlers, Sunday Schools were organized by Rev.
Kyle of Ipswich, and religious meetings were held in the schools. The Welsh
settlers in the eastern part attended the church they built in Powell City. The
English settlers in the central part attended Sunday School within the township
and later attended the Free Methodist Church in Powell Township. The Bohemian
settlers in the western part, mostly Catholic, were periodically ministered to
by a Bohemian priest who came to the Cleveland township community. In the
meantime, they gathered at the Vincent Stulc home (parents of Mrs. Joe Kadlec)
for worship services on Sundays or walked or traveled with horse and buggy a
long distance to Ipswich to attend Mass. Rev. Roberts helped conduct religious
meetings in the area in the Dunkord religion. With the advent of cars and road
improvement, Sunday Schools were abandoned and church services attended in
Powell, Loyalton and Ipswich.

        Oscar Viertel's threshing outfit.

        Oscar Viertel's Farm

Steam power began to replace some of the horse-powered equipment. and more land
was put under cultivation. Oscar Viertel owned the first steam engine and
threshing outfit in the area. It included a cook car, with his daughters, Emma
and Lydia, doing the cooking for the crew. Horse drawn binders and headers
bound the grain into bundles or stacks. This had to cure or go through a
"sweat" before the threshing procedure could take place. When Oscar Viertel
retired, his son-in-law, Austin Griffith and Walter Stafford took over with a
Minneapolis thresher and cross-motored 15-27 Case tractor.

The decade of the teens saw a movement of new families into the township. Among
the families coming in were Harry Bailey, Joe Vogel, George Stern Sr., Ira and
Ernest Beecher, Peter Kukrall Sr., Ira Aldrich and sons, Ernest Shore and the
Thorells.

Three modern school buildings were erected in 1920. Liberty Pride replaced the
Viertel School; Liberty View replaced the Goodale School; and Liberty Bell the
Getsman School. Liberty Bell still serves as the township hall. Telephones were
installed in many of the rural homes. Joe and Mary Kadlec, Matthew and Margaret
Evans, and Lee and Rose Wagner became landowners and residents.

Steam power also meant grading of roads. The Hamak brothers, with their 110
Case Steam engine, graded many roads in the area and surrounding townships and
broke native sod. Horse power was being replaced by tractors, and the Aldrich
brothers purchased the first 15-30 tractor sold by the Dehnert Implement Co. of
Ipswich.

The Liberty Community Club was organized. The families gathered monthly at the
Liberty Pride School for fellowship, potluck suppers, basket socials and
sometimes put on three-act plays for public entertainment. An annual club
picnic was held each summer in the Hamak grove. It also lent a helping hand in
time of need within the community. During the depression years, it was the only
means of diversion for the younger generation. The young men organized baseball
clubs, playing teams from surrounding area.

        Many historical pictures were taken by Kammers and Viertel.

New land owners in the 1940's were Glenn and Lucille Stafford, William and
Clara Kadlec, Pete and Rose Kukrall, Clarence Morris, Don and Olga Wagner, Mrs.
Maggie Williams and sons, Darrel Walker, Wilfred and Orlean Kadlec, Albert
Weitgrefe, George and Frances Stern, Edward and Christine Stern, Ernest and
Mary Shore.

The 1950's brought REA, providing city conveniences in the rural homes. These
years also saw the first graveling of roads. Ivan and Delores Holsing bought
the Ira Beecher farm, and Floyd Hilden and Henry Morris moved into the township.
The Hamak brothers sold their holdings to the Hutterites. Their holdings now
total 4800 acres of Liberty land.

The school district was consolidated with Loyalton in 1958, and the last
one-room school was closed. The Loyalton school was closed in the
reorganization in 1970, and Liberty was taken into the Ipswich Public School
District.

Several grazing corporations were organized where residents outside the
township purchased large tracts of land, with cultivated land restored to
grass.

Better roads and school facilities in the 1970's brought a return of "native
sons" to Liberty. These included Frank and Carol Kadlec, Ron and Patty Kadlec,
Larry and Dianne Stafford, Robert and Donna Wagner. Others remaining in the
township at the present time are: Loren and Pam Seidschlaw, Ivan and Delores
Holsing, Terry and Douglas, Wilfred and Orlean Kadlec, Joe and Mary Kadlec and
Louis, Owen and Richard Williams, John and Walter Griffith, Marion Charles and
Shannon, Donald and Olga Wagner, William and Clara Kadlec, and Glenn and
Lucille Stafford.
                Lucille Stafford

Powell Township

The first Welsh settlers arrived in Powell March 29, 1883, under the leadership
of William E. Powell, General Immigration Agent of the Chicago, Milwaukee and
St. Paul Railroad. They put up the noted Big Shanty (Shame Fawr, as the
Welshman says) 20 x 40 ft. frame building on a claim occupied by the late Hugh
Griffith, who was a leading member of the colony. This was the first frame
building erected on the prairie west of Aberdeen, and served as both hotel,
church and meeting place. Here the settlers dined, slept and worshiped the God
who had brought them to this vast wilderness. They were all musically talented,
some coming directly from Wales.

There was a great deal of coming and going the first summer, with folks staying
in the Big Shanty until they had built some sort of shelter on their own
claims. Lumber, groceries, everything was hauled that long 36 miles from
Aberdeen that first summer. Shanties were going up on every claim, some tar
paper covered, and others of the sod variety.

On May 16, 1883 a past office was established by the members of the colony, and
Morris Jones was chosen postmaster. November 1 of the same year, the township
was organized as a school district, and the first school board members were:
John J. Reese, Hugh Griffith, and J.D. Jones. The first school was built in
1884 where the town hall now is, and Rev. J. Williams was the first teacher.
That winter the Big Shanty was moved two miles further south and established
the center of town, now Powell Township. The Big Shanty was then sold to Morris
Jones, and Daniel Williams who homesteaded on NE one-quarter of Section 21 gave
him a lot to build a house, also post office and store on same lot next to the
Methodist Church, the present Presbyterian Church.

On August 13, 1883, the Board of County Commissioners met, and appointed the
following from Powell: John H. Hughes, sheriff, John J. Reese, Justice of
Peace, and J. Morris, Notary Public. On August 27, 1887 J.D. Jones was appointed
Constable. There were two members of the early legislature from Powell, the
late Hon. John J. Reese and Hon. A.H. German. Thomas A. Evans served as County
Commissioner from the district. Two sons of Rev. and Mrs. J.T. Lewis were
elected to county offices, A.W. Lewis, Superintendent of Schools in 1908, and
Emrys R., County Auditor in 1922.

        Dr. Hill and Earl McAllister on Powell Rood one mile north of Powell.

The first death was Nellie Reese, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. John J. Reese in
January, 1884. John Richards and Mary Williams is reputed to be the first
couple married at Ipswich, on April 23, 1884, by Judge J. Rice. The first deep
well was dug by J. J. Reese assisted by Hugh Roberts. It was 100 feet deep and
walled with Dakota stones. The first artesian well was drilled on the place now
occupied by Benjamin Richards.

Powell had, during its existence, these postmasters: Morris Jones, John T.
Llewellyn, Henry and W.A. Hodson. On November 1, 1908, the Rural Free Delivery
routes started out of Ipswich with W.S. Barnwell as our first carrier, followed
by Elmer Lewis, W. Richmond, Charles Collier, Gerald Taylor and Walter
Peterson.

Mrs. Seth Lewis was mid-wife for the entire community during those years, and
presided at the birth of nearly all of the babies. In the fall of 1904 typhoid
fever raised a fearful havoc in the community taking the lives of Mrs. Henry
Hodson and two sons and Nellie Williams, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Evan J.
Williams. In the spring of 1907, dyptheria broke out resulting in the death of
two young people, Mary, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. John Richards, and Hannah,
daughter of Mr. and Mrs. W.R. Williams. In the fall of 1918 the flu epidemic
swept through taking one member of the R.R. Williams family and three members
of the Richards family. It left its mark of suffering in many homes. Dr. Robert
Hill of Ipswich was the community doctor during these epidemics.

On July 1, 1937, the first day of the fishing season at Hamak's Lake brought a
drowning tragedy which took the lives of four people: Evan Williams, his two
sons, Glen and Kenneth, and Dale Jones, son of Mr. and Mrs. Hugh Jones. The
only survivor was Alice Benton.

On June 2, 1911, a tornado struck this area and destroyed much of the townsite,
several farms, schoolhouses, and the church. While no one was killed, property
damage was great. Roland Williams and Mrs. David J. Morris were seriously
injured.

Every quarter section of land that was available for homesteading was taken,
and little "shanties" were built of various sizes according to the needs of the

family. With the advance of larger and more efficient machinery the farms are
growing larger. Now, one hundred years later, there are only fifteen farm
residences, but four of these are relatively new. There are a few of the second
and third generation farming or owning the land of their forefathers including
Cassius Pond, John Richards, John B. Evans, Mrs. Ann Griffith, Evan J. Williams
and maybe others. Many of the younger generation have moved to Ipswich or
larger cities, and many pioneer names are to be found in the cemeteries of
Powell and Ipswich.

Some of the farmers specialize in certain phases of agriculture such as feeder
pigs, finishing hogs, pure bred cattle and such, but most of the farmers are
highly diversified with sunflowers being an important crop of the past decade.
Recently the "no-till" method of farming was demonstrated on the Dean Hodson
farm.

Two thriving businesses were recently established by farm wives in Powell. Mrs.
Duane (Janice) Perrion has established an upholstery and custom drape business
at her farm home known as Jan's interiors. She has recently added the making of
buffalo vests to her business. Mrs. Dale (Myra) Hodson has a former rural
schoolhouse on their farmstead where she makes artificial floral bouquets and
has various arts and crafts classes.

Many students were educated in one or more of the five school houses located in
Powell Township, but some years only two or three were open as the school
population decreased. In 1958 the voters of Powell Township decided to
centralize the school system by moving two of the school buildings near the
center of the township on the land where the Township Hall was located. Some of
the families voted themselves out of the Powell School District and elected to
take their children into Ipswich. In 1967 the voters elected to join the
Ipswich School District, and the children are now bused into Ipswich to attend
school.

        M. C. Church and Mrs. L. Lewellyn's residence offer cyclone on June 2,
        1911.

        Blodwen Richards and children of Powell school in Welsh program.

Present township officers are: Dale Hodson, clerk, Randy Williams, treasurer,
and Everett Williams, Benjamin Richards, and Dennis Benton, supervisors. The
township board has charge of the Powell Cemetery. There is also a cemetery
association which takes charge of the upkeep of the cemetery.

It is hard to imagine that the next century could possibly bring as many
changes as the past century has, but we can only hope the changes will all be
for the good of our country and civilization.
                1883-1958 Jennie Griffith
                1958-1983 Mrs. Benjamin Richards

Rosette Township

Edmunds County was divided into school districts on October 1, 1883. By special
election on December 3, 1883 the following officers were elected in Rosette:
Mr. Knapp, S.D. Beth and H.T. Daily, George Wilcox was elected treasurer. Among
the first teachers were: Jennie V. Pulling and Ervin Barrows.

A petition was presented to Rosette Township on March 26, 1891 asking to have
the township organized for civil purposes. The following officers were
appointed supervisors: George Wilcox, A.G. Auchampach, H.T. Daily; clerk, George
Williams; treasurer, A.A. Rizer; assessor, R. Barrows. The assessed valuation
of the township at that time was personal, $164,645; real estate, $332,606.

On July 10, 1910, the Rosette Baptist Church on Section 9 was dedicated.
Services were held in this church for several years. Members here were: the
Holmes, Duvall, Jones, Smith and Swanson families. The Rev. Pierce played a
major part in getting this church started. In the early 1940s this church was
sold and moved to Norbeck, S.D. The American Sunday School Union organized a
Sunday School which was held in the Northeast School House for several years.

Several prairie fires have gone through our township. The most publicized was
on April 2, 1889 at which time the town of Leola burned.

In 1930 the Rosette Dam was built, and since that time Rosette Dam has
furnished a lot of good fishing for the town and country people.

In 1957 the Spink Colony of Hutterites purchased several hundred acres of land
in Rosette. Since then it has grown and divided into another colony in Belle
Township called the Deerfield Colony. Two of the three school houses built in
1929-1930 were moved to the Hutterite Colony for their children as a part of
the Ipswich School District.

For a few years prior to the reorganization of school districts, the children
were bused to Ipswich by the township. Since the reorganization of the school
system, the children of the township have had bus service and have received a
good education.

The Richard Schumacher family lives on the George Williams homestead, which is
the fourth generation of Schumachers.

The Davis and Kindelspire farms are being owned and operated by Jim Davis and
sons, which is also a fourth generation.

Some of the residents who have left Rosette Township since 1958 were: Mr. and
Mrs. Alfred Olson, Mr. and Mrs. Lester Davis, Mr. and Mrs. Victor Lueb, Mr. and
Mrs. Francis Lueb, Mr. and Mrs. George Duvall, Mr. and Mrs. Delmer Sanborn, Mr.
and Mrs. Francis Bobby, Mr. and Mrs. Ben Weigel, Mr. and Mrs. Walter
Schumacher, Mr. and Mrs. Tony Hammrich, Mr. and Mrs. Jake Mardian, Mr. and Mrs.
John Schlepp, and Oscar Kindelspire.

February 1978 after a bad storm.

The following families living in Rosette Township at the present time are: Jim
Davis, Darrel Davis, Darwin Davis, Clarence Hammrich, Tony Gross, Joe Gauer,
Richard Schumacher, Tom Weigel, Carl Sanborn and the Plainview colony which
consists of twelve families.

Since 1958 we have had some severe winters. The blizzards of 1966, 1972, and
1978 will be remembered by most of the residents.

We have seen continued improvement on our roads and conservation with more
tree, water and land conserving projects. We are hoping to see the WEB Pipeline
and Sunflower plant completed which would benefit our townships.
                Delores Schumacher, Del and Carl Sanborn

Union Township

Homesteaders began settling in Union Township in 1883, and by 1885 there were
two to three on every section. The scarcity of water caused many to leave until
only thirty remained in 1901

In 1958 there were twenty-five homes and now, twenty-five years later, we have
twelve farming operations and five farm sites occupied, but the residents are
not engaged in farming.

The early settlers who established permanent homes here were: Mr. and Mrs. F.
Drager and Agusta, Charley, Emil, Herman and Edward who came in 1883; Mr. and
Mrs. A. W. Olson and Arthur in 1889; Hans Tobiason in 1893, and the Royles. The
Olson homestead is the only one that is still owned and operated by a
descendant. Larry Olson, great-grandson of Andrew Olson, is the fourth
generation farmer. Union Township also has two three-generation farmers, Robert
Olson and Harris M. Swanson. There are three two-generation farmers, Norman
Bosanko, Leonard Gauer and Philip Sporer. We also welcome all first generation
farmers.

In 1886 a church was built one-half mile north of the Center on a ten acre plot
given for the church and a cemetery by the Reeves family who homesteaded that
quarter in 1883. Mrs. Reeves was the Sunday School superintendent there for
over twenty-five years and drove out to attend church for many years after
moving to Ipswich. The church building was moved to Java in 1936 or 1937.

Two school buildings served the township until more settlers moved into Union
in 1904, making it necessary for two more schools to be added before 1908.
These four were used until about 1940. In 1962, as schools were being
reorganized in the county, the children in Union Township were bused to school
in Ipswich.

In the last twenty-five years, six new homes belonging to Pat Fonder, Raymond
Forkel, Paul Palmer, August Arlt, Gary Gisi and Lenny Gauer improved the
township. The homes of August Arlt and Mrs. Sylvester Gauer were moved to
Ipswich in the late 1970's. The former Alva Wolter house was moved to the
Harris Swanson farm and remodeled. The majority of other homes have also been
remodeled and farmsteads well kept.

Although the population in our township has dwindled from one hundred three in
1958 to sixty-four in 1983, the milking of cows and the raising of chickens has

drastically declined. Union township now has more horses than twenty-five years
ago.
                Harris and Loree Swanson

        New mobile home of Lenny Gauer.

        New home of Gary Gisi.

Town of Loyalton and Vermont Township

The town of Loyalton was at its peak in 1919. This was because the cattlemen
from Montana and western South Dakota came to this vicinity to make hay which
at that time was in abundance. Men and equipment in great numbers descended on
this small but busy little town. Every available room in the hotel (which yet
stands) was taken. Private homes also were occupied during this never to be
forgotten, and much talked about, episode in the history of Loyalton.

Men of various characters, men of means, inhabited the town for several weeks.
When the haying was finished, it was loaded into box cars on the C. M. St. Paul
& P. Railroad and shipped to its most needed destination.

When Vermont Township was organized is not definitely known but assumed to be
in 1886. Not long after, a group of hearty pioneers from Vermont and New
Hampshire homesteaded. Some lived on homesteads all their lives and died there.


Israel Oban and family were not known to many of us, but his son, T.L. Oban,
and family lived on the homestead, and some of the family still live here in
Loyalton, namely, Mrs. Edna Oban (Ralph Oban's wife.).

Arthur Barton died on his homestead one mile south and two and three-fourths
miles east. There are no buildings there now.

Time nor space will not permit referring to all who had a part in settling here
and building the community in which we live. Time and elements have changed the
countryside. Those who once lived here and were a part of the township have long
since passed on. One generation and then another moved in and out. The
landscape changed along with the population.

Vermont Township today has only thirteen remaining farms out of the thirty or
more, if our memory is correct.

Darrell Bukaske owns and lives on what we know as the Jim Stark farm. S.O.
Streit also owned and lived there.

Norman Hettich now owns and lives on the T.L. Oban homestead. Chet Oban, Tom
Oban, Vernard Schmitt also lived on this farm. Vernard Schmitt owned it for
years until sold to Hettichs.

Johnnie Obans and Loren Vargasons live on what we know as the Oban home. In its
early days it was known as the Zander farm.

Stanley Lester lives on the last farm that T.L. Oban built. Stanley Lester
owned this farm for years. He sold it to Floyd Hilden and later bought it back.
T.L. Oban had in mind to build one more farm just half way between the
Oban-Vargason and Stanley Lester farms. The grove of trees there is a reminder
to all who knew him of his intentions that never materialized.

Robert Allbee now lives on the farm he and his dad built. Clyde lived there
before Robert.

Clyde Allbee farm was known as the Newman farm. Bill Norton and Bill Chase also
lived there.

Clarence Kukrall's farm was built by Hiram or Herman DeWolf. Ole Mattison,
Lester Duvall, and Roy Loudon were past owners of the farm.

Curt Sylte owns the Mart Friedrichsen farm formerly owned by a Mr. Lot. Mart
and his family came in 1908 from Nebraska. His son, Emil, now lives in Ipswich
as does Edna Friedrichsen (Ernest Friedrichsen's wife).

The Harold Anderson farm was the Silas Alexander homestead. Henry Bode, Clay
Steen, Bob Scandrett and Bill Chase lived there. At one time it was owned by
Bill Mohr.

Richard Lester lives on a new farm built by his dad, Stanley Lester, one and
one-half miles south of Loyalton.

Marvin Hettich's farm on which he lives was called the Whistler farm. The
following ones lived on this place until Marvin Hettich became the owner: Oren
Soward, Louie Walter, Oscar Fredrickson, George Nipp, Earl Allbee, Bun Barton,
Lloyd Walker and Mike Petersen. Marvin Hettich also owned the Dave Soward farm.
This was homesteaded by Andy Short. He also owns the Nipp farm known in its
early days as the Chicago Ranch along with the Mohr farm at Loyalton known as
the Handcock farm.

John Beyers lives on the farm known as the Harry Cole farm built by Herman
Tscherley.

Robert Baer has built a farm in Vermont Township adjacent to his farm in
Hillside Township. He operates a dairy farm on this place.

All the other farms that once were in Vermont Township have been destroyed by
man and elements. Only the mute foundations tell the tale of their location.
The locations of these farms are known to us, and also to the people who once
lived and operated them.

The Syltes came to Loyalton on October 12, 1919. Our first landing was on the
steps on the old hotel where we met Tom Hines, E.P. Waller, Bill Hines and Roy
Loudon.

The main street was lined on both sides with business places of nearly every
description, a really prosperous prairie town. The drought in the 30's was the
beginning of the downfall. Farmers moved, business places locked up, the
railroad was torn up, storms and fire also took their toll. We have seen the
town at it's best and as it is now today.

In November, Loyalton will be only a memory, and it's memory cherished by those
of us who remain and still call it home. C.E.
                (Al) Sylte

Kent Township

Kent Township was surveyed in 1882. The surveyors saw no sign of any settlers
at that time. Soon after settlers came, and many built sod houses. By 1885
elections were held, and two school houses were built. Later, two more schools
were built. Wages at that time were $25.00 a month. Kent's first school
apportionment was $26.23.

A post office was started in 1886, called St. Herbert, and the first post
master was Charles Rehfield. This post office was discontinued in April of 1908
and mail service moved to Cresbard, S.D.

In 1958 there was only one school operating in the township. All the children
attended this school. Wages that year were $235.00 a month, and Mrs. Ruth
Dornbush was the teacher. The township levy was $2000.00. This was a modern
school with running water, electric lights, furnace and bathrooms. It is used
now for township meetings and elections. When school reorganization was held,
all rural schools closed. Buses were used to transport the children to town
schools each day. The township was divided, and the north half went to Ipswich
Public School and the south half to Cresbard Public School.

There is a small cemetery in the township named St. Herbert Cemetery where
several of the early settlers are buried.

At one time Kent township was well populated with at least one farm home on
each section. Now in 1982 there are only twelve occupied farms.

        This is Kent School, one of the last years that the school was open.
        Children of Gordon Petersen, Harold Roemmich, Lauren Stager and L.
        Harris. Mrs. Ruth Dornbush was the teacher.

        Merritt and James Jones farm.

        Dan Anderson and John Anderson stacking hay in 1912 on the present
        Melvin Palmer place.

        Selmer Anderson family with their Morgan Gelding "Tip Top" are ready
        for a parade in 1978.

Several in the township are living on farms built up by their ancestors.

D.M. Jones built up a home and farmed on Section 19. Later his son, George
James, and wife, Christine, farmed the land. Today their sons, Merritt and Jim,
live with their mother and sister and farm the land once owned by their
grandfather.

A.P. Anderson farmed many years on Section 13. Later on his son, Daniel M.
Anderson, farmed it. Today it is farmed by Dan's son, Selmer, and now his son,
Ron, and wife have a trailer home and help with the farming, making it a four
generation farm.

        A. P. Anderson Homestead in 1905 or 1906.

        A picture of the farmhouse where Gordon Petersen lives. It was built by
        Gordon's grandfather, John Davies in 1911.

        1918 35 H.P. Minneapolis. Fred Achenbach on engine and Erwin beside
        wheel.

Gordon Petersen farms the land once owned and built up by his grandfather, John
Davies. His son, Levi Davies, operated the farm until Gordon took over. Now
Gordon's son, Michael, helps, making it a four generation operation.

The Stager farm on Section 12 was first farmed by Albert Stager, then by his
son, Lauren, and is now farmed by Lauren's son, Larry Stager.

Leroy Wegner owns part of Section 33 that he bought from his uncle, Otto
Wegner. This land was formerly owned by Leroy's grandfather, William Wegner.

Arnold Petersen lives on land that was built up by his parents and was owned in
the early days by his grandfather, John Davies.

Farming is done on a larger scale due to the larger, more modern machinery.
Most farm homes are as modern as the city houses with heated water,
electricity, television, refrigerators and freezers, all taken for granted.
They were unheard of in the early days. We wonder what is in store for those
living the next one hundred years.
                Beulah Petersen

                SERVICES Rural and City

Fire Department

        Ipswich Fire Department

Ipswich, with its many wooden frame buildings, had disastrous fires that wiped
out large portions of the business section more than once in its early history.
It was after one of these that an ordinance was passed banning the erection of
frame buildings on Main Street.

As early as 1886 or '87 the town had sunk an artesian well near the railroad
tracks and hopes ran high that the city had adequate protection. A pipeline was
laid east of the well and then north reaching into the business section. To
this pipe a hose could be attached which afforded what appeared to be a fair
stream with considerable force. All went well for a few years, but then the
well ceased to function and the city again was forced to go back to the former
bucket brigade method.

The city did not have relief from this situation until after the city sunk
