another artesian well to provide sufficient water supply. It was after this
that the townsmen decided a fire company should be organized for the purpose of
having fire drills. On April 7, 1911, a group met and formed the first
organization. The following were the first officers: W.E. Herrick, L.E. Smith,
Nick Kasel, L.E. Bigger, Roy Smith, H.E. Beebe, C.L. Jackson, A.L. Jones and
George McEachran.

In the years that followed the firemen took active steps to organize the
company so effective fire fighting could be established. They began to add
additional equipment and in 1923, a new truck was purchased. This fire truck
has been retired for many years and is now in storage and used only by the
American Legion for parades.

The 1950 fire truck has been taken out of service. In 1959, a fire truck was
purchased and this unit is equipped with regular fire fighting equipment. This
unit is used mostly for city fires and is only taken into the country on calls
when it is a house fire, or in an emergency when all other units cannot handle
the fire.

In 1974, the Fire Department purchased a one ton Dodge and this unit was
equipped for the purpose of fighting grass fires. Both the 1959 and 1974 trucks
were purchased with money that was received by the assessments of the townships
for fire protection. In 1981, an army truck was equipped with an 1800 gallon
water tank and a pump for the purpose of transporting water to the fire.

The Fire Department was reorganized into a nonprofit organization known as the
Ipswich Volunteer Fire Department, Inc. This took place in 1980. All townships
were assessed the amount set by law to establish a fund to make the payments on
a new fire station. The new fire station was built in 1981 at a cost of
$85,000. The new building houses the ambulance, four fire trucks, the city
office and a large meeting room for the firemen and the City Council.

A new fire truck should be in service the early part of 1983 at a cost of over
$50,000. At the present time there are twenty-five active volunteer firemen
with Robert Gauer acting as Chief.

FEM Electric

        FEM Board of Directors, 1951. Back row left to right: Byron Jones,
        Martin Schurr, Wendell Miller, Michael Bowar, Albert Hauffe and
        Manager, K. A. MacMillan. Front row left to right: Paul Hausauer,
        Edwin Haselhorst, Matt Bachman, T. T. Martel.

The desire of farm families in Faulk, Edmunds and McPherson Counties to come
out of the darkness of the past and enjoy the conveniences available to city
residents led to the formation of a rural electric cooperative. FEM Electric
Association, Inc. became an official entity on December 10, 1945, with the
filing of the Articles of Incorporation with the Secretary of State. The
original incorporators were Matthew Bachman, Edwin E. Hubbard, Victor Holsing,
Michael Bowar, Wendell Miller, Theodore T. Martel, Paul Hausauer, Albert C.
Hauffe and Robert Schurr. Edmunds County Agent, Oscar E. Prestegard, assisted
with the origination of the association. Five years later he stated, "Out of
the darkness into light in five short years has been the unique experience of
over 2,000 farm families served by FEM Electric Association in Faulk, Edmunds,
and McPherson Counties. The rapid growth of this association since its first
meeting held in Roscoe in 1945 has been phenomenal."

The first lines were energized three years later at the Hillsview Substation on
November 30, 1948. On December 12, 1948, farms in the Ipswich area received
electricity from the Ipswich Substation. By Christmas Eve of that year, service
was available to slightly over 200 members. A celebration on December 14, 1948,
at the Long Lake Legion Hall observed the completion of electric service to the
town of Long Lake. The first annual meeting of the membership was held on June
5, 1949, in connection with Ipswich's "Trail Day". Over 3,700 people were
served beef barbecue sandwiches. Conservative estimates of over 5,000 people
were on hand to participate in the day's activities and the first annual
meeting.

Construction of the distribution system continued throughout the three-county
area to provide central station service to more of the members. By February 28,
1949, the number of members receiving electric service had reached one
thousand.

The need for an additional source of electric power for the new cooperative
resulted in an agreement that provided exchange power from the United States
Bureau of Reclamation. On May 25, 1949, a small group gathered at the Ipswich
Substation to recognize the first official entrance of USBR power in South
Dakota.

An old building on Main Street served as the first headquarters for the
cooperative, but the need for a permanent location, designed for the purpose,
resulted in the construction of a building on the edge of the city. Ground
breaking on July 24, 1950, began construction with completion the following
year.

The last contract line construction was completed on December 23, 1950,
bringing electric power to all the farmers qualified by Christmas Eve of that
year. In a period of five years and 31 days, 2,114 miles of line had been
constructed to bring the dream of electricity to the farm families of the three
counties. Energizing of the 2,000th consumer occurred on August 31, 1951, when
the Elmer Feiock farm near Leola was connected.

FEM Electric Association is a cooperative organized to provide electric service
at cost. The first patronage refunds, called capital credits, were paid on June
10, 1959, at the annual meeting in Bowdle. A total of $26,000 in checks was
issued.

The use of electricity on the farms continued to increase, requiring the
addition of several new substations in the service area during the 1960s and
1970s. By 1982, the number of substations had increased from the original three
to eight. Sales had increased from one million KWH in 1950 to over 40 million
in 1982.

In the fall of 1969, the first underground circuits were installed in the
Eureka area. During the past 12 years, many more miles of conductor have gone
under the ground to replace the old overhead wires and poles.

FEM Electric has had two board presidents since its inception in 1945. The
first, Albert C. Hauffe of Leola, also served many years as the president of
the South Dakota Rural Electric Association and two terms as the president of
the National Rural Electric Association. Mr. Hauffe was succeeded by Norman
Batteen of Chelsea in 1976. He is currently serving in that position.

K. A. McMillan, an Indiana native, was selected to manage FEM Electric in 1948,
following the coordinator, Robert Schurr. Following Mr. McMillan's resignation
in 1956, Line Superintendent, A.W. Grage, was promoted to manager. S.G. Fischer
was named manager in 1967 after the passing of A.W. Grage. Fischer has served
as manager since that time.

        First Bureau of Reclamation Power in South Dakota reading the meter at
        Ipswich Substation. Left to right: R. W. Beadle, Glen Brown, K. A.
        MacMillan, Albert Hauffe, Unknown, Unknown, Joke Lippert, L. S. Harmer,
        Unknown, R. W. Bebermeyer.

The use of electricity and the facilities to distribute it have changed
dramatically over the lifetime of the association. The most significant
changes, however, occurred in the demographics of the three-county area. An
overall consolidation of farms into larger units has been a constant process of
evolution. The causes have varied, but the result has been constant and on
going. Statistics from the late 1940s indicated that over 2,000 farm families
occupied Faulk, Edmunds and McPherson Counties. Current information shows that
the number has decreased to under 1,300 in the same geographic area. Despite
the decrease, the use of electrical energy has steadily increased until 1981.
The cost, a difficult economic situation, and milder weather have resulted in a
leveling of consumption.

The past 37 years have brought the farm families, still living on the land, a
living standard equal to that of the most modern city. A long list of
electrical equipment from video discs, computers, automatic washers, to
microwave ovens can be found on many farms. Items that did not exist 37 years
ago, in addition to light bulbs, motors, water pumps, refrigerators, etc. have
made farm life easier and more productive. Those fewer producers that still
remain are producing more with this modern technology. This advancement has had
the opposite effect on many social aspects. Many farmsteads have been wiped
from the countryside. Small communities and towns, once the source of identity,
enjoyment and pride are gone. All that remains is the mute evidence of concrete
that formed the foundations.

        FEM Office in Ipswich.

These changes have brought an entire new set of operating circumstances to bear
on the association and the people who control and operate it. The future will
reveal whether the lessons learned from the great accomplishments of the past
will be adequate to chart a course in the unknown waters of the future.

Edmunds County Conservation District

Interest in an Edmunds County Conservation District developed over a period of
years. West side operators were concerned about water erosion, while flat-land
operators' concern was wind erosion. Prior to the organization of the district,
conservation work was done mostly by construction of dugouts and drainage, but
Soil Conservation Service technical assistance depended upon local men who were
inadequately trained to offer assistance of a professional quality. Many people
had a feeling more conservation work was badly needed.

An educational meeting, conducted by the Agricultural Conservation Program, was
held early in 1954 at Bowdle. This is the time when Art Loecke, office manager
for Agricultural Stabilization and Conservation Service, Matt Bachmann, Bowdle
and Wilbert Blumhardt, Bowdle, discussed circulating a petition to organize a
Soil Conservation District so that full time Soil Conservation Service
technical assistance could be obtained. Many others in the county were having
the same thoughts.

The names of Arnold Petersen and Harris Swanson are on the records. There were
many others not named who were active promoters. Our county agent at that time,
Oscar Prestegard, spread the doctrine that there was a need to conserve our
soil.

On November 28, 1955, signed petitions for formation of a district were filed. A
public hearing was held on December 13, 1955, and an election was held March
14, 1956. Of the 788 people who voted, 73 percent voted in favor of
organization.

With the formalities mostly completed, a meeting was held on May 14, 1956.
Selection of the first supervisors were as follows: Chairman, Wilbert
Blumhardt; Vice-chairman, Arnold Petersen; Secretary, Mervin Goehring;
Treasurer, Melvin Borkirchert and Stanley Lester. The Edmunds County Soil and
Water Conservation District was born.

The Edmunds County Conservation District derives a major portion of its
operating funds from custom tree planting and grass seeding. Money budgeted by
the Edmunds County Commissioners, also contributes to the operation of the
District's conservation programs.

Since the District was organized, approximately 4,540 acres of trees have been
planted in Edmunds County, including 281 acres of field windbreaks.
Approximately 70,870 acres of grass has been seeded in the county, including
both tame and native seedings. Some of the major conservation measures
installed with district assistance are: grass waterways, terraces, contour
farming, animal waste control facilities, wildlife area development and
livestock watering facilities.

The most recent endeavor by the district is the promotion of no-till farming.
In the spring of 1982, approximately 1,000 acres of cropland was seeded using
no-till drills. Approximately 2,500 acres is planned for the fall of 1982.

Current members of the District Board are: Dwayne Leboldus, Onaka; Gideon
Schuh, Bowdle; Wilbert Blumhardt, Bowdle; Duane Holsing, Cresbard and Jerald
Braun, Wetonka.
                David R. Karst, District Manager

Cooperative Extension Service

        Edmunds County 4-H Building in Ipswich

Extension Service began its first service to Edmunds County in April 1918 when
R.R. Buchanan was installed as an emergency agent. During the spring of 1919,
Mr. Buchanan was called to Roberts County and he was followed by W. C. Boardman
as emergency agent. Following Mr. Boardman came Dean Davis who continued his
work here until 1921. Mr. J. Carl Laney succeeded Davis from 1921-1923, as the
last agent to be employed up until the drought emergency period of 1933.

Boys' and Girls' Club work, now known as 4-H had its first beginnings in 1920.
Club work then consisted of one main project such as livestock, corn, garden,
canning, sewing, etc.

Alex Pond, Ipswich was the first delegate of Edmunds County to National Club
Congress at Chicago in 1924. Alex was then a member of the Triangle Corn Club
under the leadership of Joe Clemens, Ipswich.

During the fall of 1933, Mr. Fred A. Beers was appointed as emergency agent.
Extension Homemakers was a new program for the women of Edmunds County in 1934.
Five Clubs were organized: Mrs. Carl Krueger, Ipswich was chairman of the
Belle-Pembrook Extension Club. Later the name was changed to Pioneer. Mrs. Carl
Blank was chairman of the Merry Bunch extension club. Others were: Mrs. E.E.
Hubbard of Harmony Club, Mrs. Charles Corliss of the Loyalton Club, and Mrs.
Delbert Hodson of the Powell Club which later changed to the Eastern All Star
Club.

Youth Club work became active again in 1934. Seven clubs were organized during
the year by Mr. Beers, Emergency Agricultural Agent. These clubs included four
sewing clubs, one potato, one corn and one handicraft club.

Extension work was rejuvenated in 1935 when the County Commissioners appointed
a County Extension Board. Members of the board were: William Stafford, George
Weishar, John J. Biegler, Richard F. Huber and J.A. Theis. They proceeded to
organize and hired Mr. A.D. Lienemann as the first County Agricultural Agent in
December 15, 1935. The first 4-H Achievement Days were held during the summer
of 1935, a joint event with the Women's program. The first Corn Show was also
held that year in the fall.

Oscar E. Prestegard transferred from Campbell County to Edmunds in 1937 and
served as County Agricultural Agent until the time of his death November 14,
1956. Mr. Prestegard will long be remembered by many for his keen interest in
youth activities. 4-H leaders and members expressed their appreciation for his
service by raising funds for the construction of a memorial 4-H building which
was completed in 1958.

In 1943, Jeanne Marie Wahl was appointed as the first Home Demonstration Agent
in Edmunds County. She was followed by Geraldine O'Hara in 1948 and Mrs. Mickey
Bovee from 1949 to 1952. Ferne Justice served as assistant home agent in 1952.

During the summer of 1954, Eleanore Grubele, now Mrs. Peter Krokosh, became
Home Demonstration Agent and served until 1972. From 1972 to 1976 Karen
Zilverberg Geditz served as the Area Extension Home Economist for Faulk,
Edmunds and McPherson counties.

        In 1949 the Edmunds County 4-H Band was organized. The 60 piece band
        was under the direction of Ipswich Public School band instructor Algie
        Faris. The county band performed at several local events and in the 4-H
        parade at South Dakota State Fair. Uniforms of the band members
        consisted of white shirts, white trousers, a green tie and tapped off
        with a white hat and green plume. County agent at that time was O.K.
        Prestegard.

Leonard Nelson served as the county Agricultural Agent from 1957-1965. He was
followed by Dennis Bunde who served the county until 1969. In 1970 Herb
Brandner assumed the position and was employed until 1979.

The 4-H Achievement Day Grounds were expanded in 1962, with the addition of a
livestock barn and again in 1972, with the construction of a large all-purpose
building.

An interesting special activity for many 4-H'rs was the 4-H Band organized in
the summer of 1949 under the leadership of Algie Faris, band instructor.
Today's 4-H members take part in a variety of activities ranging from: Career
Education and Citizenship to Livestock Judging and Special Foods Contests.

The present Extension Agents are Carole Sweeter, Extension Home Economist, who
assumed her duties in 1976 and Dale C. Curtis, County Agricultural Agent who
began his employment in 1979. Their duties include serving as advisors to the
175 members in 15 4-H clubs. Other organizations with which they work include:
Extension Homemakers, Crop Improvement Association, Livestock Improvement
Association and the County Weed Board, as well as numerous individuals on a day
to day basis.

The roots of the Extension Service have remained with the people of the county
due to the service of many dedicated extension board members over the years. At

present the members of the board are Lester (Dick) Giedt, Marvin Uhrich,
Dorothy Meyer, Bonnie Roesch and Vernon (Johnny) Oban.

        Edmunds County is served by five commissioners. they are left to right:
        Marvin Uhrich, Roscoe; Ray Bachman, Bowdle; Louie Thares, Ipswich;
        Raymond Forkel, rural Ipswich and not pictured Robert Stoecker, Hosmer.

                FARMERS CHANGING TIMES

The following three threshing crew pictures are token from one picture. The
original picture is nearly three feet wide. The picture has a caption--W. A.
Siegel, 1924.

        Today's modern 4-wheel drive tractors allow the farmer to work in year
        round comfort.

        The days of threshing machines gave way to the self propelled combines
        that allowed more independent farming methods.

        Early farmers all needed good work horses.

        Styles and size of tractors changed aver the years. This is a 4-wheel
        drive Caster-Action.

        The early style tractors allowed the farmers to start cultivating more
        land.

        Horses helped the farmer to turn the native prairie into a field of
        waving grain.

        The threshing machine has completed its task and allowed the farmer to
        become more mechanized. Today the machines set in the weeds, just a
        memory of the early days, but proud to hold the sign announcing the
        Ipswich Centennial.

        Market prices June 27, 1929

                IN THE LIMELIGHT

Our community is proud to have had an Eminent Homemaker and Eminent Farmer
named from this area. This program is carried out by the South Dakota State
College of Agriculture and Mechanic Arts, and the honor is bestowed by
recommendation of the Faculty of Division of Agriculture with approval of
Regents of Education of South Dakota.

The recipient is selected to recognize public service as a citizen and as a
leader in rural life. John Davies was selected as Eminent Farmer in 1944, and
Mrs. Mathew (Margaret) Evans was selected Eminent Homemaker in 1962.

        Eminent Farmer, John Davies

        Eminent Homemaker, Margaret Evans.

        Picture taken on south side of Ford garage. Left to right are: Ford
        Field Man, Lawrence Welk, Howard Lass, Frank Schalk Zahalka and Clifford
        Brunkow.

        The "Braun Sisters" Jessie, Lori and Fritz (Frances) on May, 1956
        singing at the Aragon Ballroom in Santa Monica, Calif. with Lawrence
        Welk.

        In 1967 Joanne Opsahl (Schlekewy) was selected as the South Dakota
        State Snow Queen She is the daughter of James and Mary Jane Richards
        Opsahl.

        On January 13, 1983 Julie Reil of Ipswich was selected as the South
        Dakota State Junior Snow Queen. She is the daughter of Sharon and Pete
        Reil.

        Aerial view of Ipswich taken by Jim Rappe in February of 1983.
